Semantic Channel Theory: Deductive Compression and Structural Fidelity for Multi-Agent Communication
Jianfeng Xu

Abstract
Shannon’s information theory deliberately excludes message semantics. This paper develops a rigorous framework for semantic communication that integrates formal proof systems with Shannon-theoretic tools. We introduce an axiomatic information model comprising ℒ sem \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}} -definable state sets linked by computable enabling maps, and define the semantic channel as a composition of Markov kernels whose supports respect the enabling structure. A fixed proof system induces an irredundant semantic core and a derivation-depth stratification, enabling four distortion measures of increasing semantic depth: Hamming, closure, depth, and a parameterized composite. Six families of computable semantic channel invariants are defined and their inter-relationships established, including a data processing bound, a semantic Fano bound, and an ideal-channel collapse theorem. The central quantitative result is a deductive compression gain: under closure-based fidelity, the minimum blocklength is determined by the irredundant core size rather than the full knowledge-base size. We instantiate the framework for heterogeneous multi-agent communication, introducing an overlap decomposition that yields necessary and sufficient conditions for closure-reliable communication. A semantic bottleneck phenomenon is identified in broadcast settings: vocabulary mismatch imposes irreducible fidelity limitations even over noiseless carriers. All results are verified on an explicit Datalog instance.



I
Introduction

Communication is, in Shannon’s celebrated
formulation, the problem of reproducing at one point a message
selected at another
[
34
]
.
Shannon’s mathematical theory deliberately sets aside the

meaning
of messages: “the semantic aspects of
communication are irrelevant to the engineering problem”

[
34
]
.
This separation has been extraordinarily productive, yielding
the fundamental limits of data compression, channel coding, and
network information
theory
[
8
,
9
]
.
Yet already in the companion essay by
Weaver
[
39
]
, a three-level hierarchy was
envisaged: the
technical
problem of accurate symbol
transmission (Level A), the
semantic
problem of conveying
intended meaning (Level B), and the
effectiveness
problem
of achieving the desired effect at the receiver (Level C).
Shannon’s theory addresses Level A with unmatched precision; the
present paper develops a rigorous framework for Level B that
is mathematically compatible with—and strictly
generalizes—Level A.

The need for such a framework has intensified with the rise of
knowledge-intensive communication systems.
In multi-agent coordination, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG)
pipelines, and federated knowledge-base synchronization, the
communicating parties exchange not raw symbols but structured

semantic states
—ground facts, rules, or queries drawn
from a shared or partially overlapping logical vocabulary.
A symbol-level error that leaves the deductive content unchanged
is harmless, while a symbol-level “success” that corrupts a
single irredundant axiom may destroy an entire branch of
derivable knowledge.
Classical information theory, which treats source and channel
alphabets as unstructured label sets, cannot make this
distinction.
A principled theory of semantic communication must therefore
integrate
logical structure
—deductive closure,
irredundant bases, derivation depth—with

information-theoretic structure
—entropy, mutual
information, channel capacity, and coding theorems.

Related Work and Gap Identification

Semantic information has a long intellectual history, from the
possible-worlds measure of Carnap and
Bar-Hillel
[
6
]
and Floridi’s theory of
strongly semantic information
[
11
]
,
through Kolchinsky and Wolpert’s viability-based
formulation
[
17
]
, to the recent
deep-learning-driven systems exemplified by
DeepSC
[
41
]
and surveyed
in
[
30
,
13
,
23
]
.
A parallel line of work has established a mathematical theory
of semantic communication based on synonymous
mappings
[
28
,
44
]
, deriving
semantic entropy, capacity, and rate-distortion functions, with
companion coding algorithms for many-to-one
sources
[
24
]
, semantic
rate-distortion computation
[
14
]
, and
semantic arithmetic coding
[
21
]
.
On the multi-agent side, recent contributions address
heterogeneous semantics via Bayesian contextual
reasoning
[
32
]
, logic-driven resilience
frameworks
[
3
]
, and goal-oriented
metric unification
[
20
,
40
]
.
The logical substrate for structured communication draws on
descriptive complexity
[
15
]
,
Datalog fixpoint
theory
[
7
,
1
]
, and
knowledge compilation
[
10
]
.

These diverse lines of research each illuminate important
facets of semantic communication, and together they suggest a
natural next step:
integrating formal proof systems
(deductive closure, irredundant cores, derivation depth) with
Shannon-theoretic tools (entropy, mutual information, channel
capacity, coding theorems) to characterize the fundamental
limits of semantic communication under knowledge-base
heterogeneity
.
The synonymous-mapping
approach
[
28
]
elegantly extends Shannon
theory through probabilistic equivalence classes and opens the
door to incorporating logical inference structure; the
deep-learning
approaches
[
41
,
30
]
demonstrate
impressive practical performance and motivate the development
of formal guarantees on deductive fidelity; the multi-agent
frameworks
[
32
,
3
]
advance
coordination and resilience analysis and invite companion
coding-theoretic results; and the logical-complexity
literature
[
15
,
25
]

provides the mathematical substrate on which communication-rate
and channel-capacity analyses can be built.
A detailed discussion of each line of work and its relationship
to the present framework is provided in
Section
II
.

Motivation and Approach

The gap identified above motivates the central question of this
paper:

Can one develop a unified framework that combines formal
logic (proof systems, deductive closure, irredundant bases) with
Shannon-theoretic tools (entropy, mutual information, channel
capacity, coding theorems) to characterize the fundamental limits
of semantic communication, and can this framework handle the
vocabulary heterogeneity that arises naturally in multi-agent
settings?

Our approach to this question originates from an

ontological
perspective on information
[
43
,
16
,
42
,
31
]
.
Rather than treating information as a probability distribution on
an abstract alphabet (the Shannon view) or as a set of excluded
possible worlds (the Carnap–Bar-Hillel view), we model
information as a collection of
states
of objects in
time—formalized as
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable
binary relations in a finite many-sorted logical
structure—together with a
computable enabling mechanism

that links semantic states to physical carrier states.
This ontological information model, developed axiomatically in
Section
III
, provides the deterministic and logical
substrate on which probabilistic structure is erected.
The key insight is that the proof system shared by communicating
agents induces a natural stratification of the semantic state
space into an
irredundant core

\text{Atom}(S_{O})
—the smallest set from which all stored knowledge
can be re-derived—and a collection of
stored shortcuts

J=S_{O}\setminus\text{Atom}(S_{O})
that are deductively redundant.
This stratification has direct coding-theoretic consequences:
under a
closure-based
fidelity criterion (which deems a
reconstruction acceptable if it preserves the deductive closure
of the original knowledge base), only the
|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|

irredundant core elements need to be transmitted reliably; the
remaining
|S_{O}|-|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|
states can be recovered by the
receiver’s inference engine at zero additional channel cost.

Building on this foundation, we introduce the
semantic
channel
as a composition of three enabling kernels—encoding,
carrier transmission, and decoding—each constrained by the
enabling structure of an underlying information model.
We then develop a suite of
semantic channel
invariants
—computable scalar quantities that characterize the
channel’s information throughput, structural fidelity, and
noise-pair structure—and derive
semantic coding theorems

that quantify the achievable rates and minimum blocklengths under
semantic reliability criteria.
Finally, we instantiate the framework in a

heterogeneous multi-agent
setting where the sender and
each receiver maintain different knowledge bases, and derive
results—including a “semantic bottleneck” phenomenon in
broadcast channels and a vocabulary-independent deductive
compression ratio—that have no counterpart in classical
Shannon theory.

Main Contributions

The main contributions of this paper are as follows.

C1.

Axiomatic information model

(Section
III
)
:

We introduce a many-sorted logical framework comprising

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable state sets, a
computable enabling mechanism linking semantic and carrier
states, composable information models, and an ideal-information
notion based on order-preserving semantic synonymy
\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}
.
A fixed proof system
\mathsf{PS}
induces an
irredundant semantic core
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and a
derivation-depth stratification
\text{Dd}(\cdot\mid B)
,
both of which are shown to be computable invariants.
A noisy-information extension via set perturbations

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
enables the analysis of non-ideal
carrier realizations.

C2.

Semantic channel and enabling kernels

(Section
IV
)
:

We define the semantic channel as a composition of Markov
kernels whose supports are constrained by the enabling
maps of three composable information models (encoding,
carrier, decoding).
The end-to-end kernel is shown to be an enabling kernel
for the composite model, ensuring that structural
constraints propagate through the probabilistic layer.

C3.

Semantic distortion measures

(Section
IV-C
)
:

We introduce four distortion functions of increasing
semantic depth: Hamming (symbol-level), closure (deductive
content), depth (inferential complexity), and a
parameterized composite.
Under closure distortion, errors on redundant states incur
zero penalty—a property absent from classical distortion
theory.

C4.

Semantic channel invariants

(Section
IV-D
)
:

We define six families of computable invariants—source-side
structural, set-level fidelity, noise-pair probabilistic,
structural quality, receiver-side comparison, and
information-theoretic—and establish their inter-relationships,
including a data processing bound

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\leq C(W)
, a semantic Fano bound
connecting the probabilistic core preservation index to
mutual information, and an ideal-channel collapse theorem.

C5.

Deductive compression gain

(Section
IV-E
)
:

We derive semantic converse and achievability bounds
showing that the minimum blocklength for closure-reliable
communication is determined by
\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|
rather
than
\log|S_{O}|
, yielding a deductive compression ratio

\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|/\log|S_{O}|
that measures the
channel-use savings afforded by receiver-side inference.
A semantic rate–distortion function

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(D;d_{\text{Cn}})
is shown to satisfy

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(0)\leq\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|
.

C6.

Heterogeneous multi-agent communication

(Section
V
)
:

We formalize knowledge-base heterogeneity via a pairwise

overlap decomposition
and derive
necessary and sufficient conditions for closure-reliable
communication in terms of core coverage and surplus
derivability.
The deductive compression ratio is shown to be
invariant under vocabulary heterogeneity.
In the broadcast setting, we prove that the minimum
blocklength is independent of the number of receivers
and identify a
semantic bottleneck

phenomenon—an irreducible fidelity limitation due to
vocabulary mismatch that persists even over noiseless
carriers—that has no counterpart in classical broadcast
theory.
All results are verified on an explicit Datalog
knowledge-base instance with full numerical computation of
every invariant.

Paper Organization

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
Section
III
establishes the axiomatic foundations:
the logical language, information models, synonymous state sets,
irredundant cores, derivation depth, and noisy information.
Section
IV
builds the probabilistic layer:
enabling kernels, the semantic channel, distortion measures,
channel invariants, and preliminary coding theorems.
Section
V
instantiates the framework for
heterogeneous multi-agent communication, derives the main
analytical results, and presents the numerical example.
Section
VI
discusses the significance of the
results, identifies limitations, and outlines directions for
future research.

Notation.

Sets are denoted by uppercase letters, elements by lowercase.

\mathbb{S}_{O}
is the ambient semantic universe;

S_{O},S_{C}
are semantic and carrier state sets;

\text{Cn}(\cdot)
is the deductive closure operator;

\text{Atom}(S_{O})
is the irredundant core;

\text{Dd}(s\mid B)
is the derivation depth;

\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}
denotes semantic synonymy;

\kappa:X\rightsquigarrow Y
denotes a Markov kernel;

H(\cdot)
,
I(\cdot;\cdot)
are Shannon entropy and mutual
information (base 2, in bits);

d_{H}
,
d_{\text{Cn}}
,
d_{\text{Dd}}
,
d_{\mathrm{sem}}
are
the four distortion functions.
Detailed notation is introduced where needed; a summary for the
multi-agent application appears in Table
I
.

II
Related Works

Semantic Information Theory.

The formal study of semantic information dates back to
Carnap and Bar-Hillel
[
6
]
, who proposed
measuring the information content of a proposition by the set
of possible worlds it excludes.
Floridi
[
11
]
further developed a theory
of strongly semantic information, defining information as
well-formed, meaningful, and truthful data.
These pioneering contributions established the philosophical
and logical foundations for semantic information and opened up
the question of how to connect such measures to operational
coding-theoretic frameworks in the tradition of
Shannon
[
34
]
.
Recently, Niu and
Zhang
[
28
,
44
]
established a
systematic mathematical theory of semantic communication,
introducing
synonymous mapping
as a core concept and
deriving semantic entropy, semantic channel capacity, and
rate-distortion functions.
A key result of their framework is that the semantic channel
capacity can exceed the Shannon capacity,
C_{s}\geq C
, by
exploiting an average synonymous length
S
that allows
multiple distinguishable source sequences to be treated as
equivalent when they express the same meaning.
Their framework elegantly extends Shannon theory by partitioning
the source alphabet into synonymous equivalence classes, and
leaves room for further exploration of how
logical
inference structure
—proof systems, deductive closure, and
irredundant cores—can be integrated into the
information-theoretic framework.
Our work pursues this complementary direction: the compression
gain in our framework arises from the receiver’s ability to

re-derive
redundant states via logical inference, which
complements the source-side synonymous-collapsing mechanism
of
[
28
,
44
]
.

Semantic Coding Theorems and Algorithms.

Building on semantic information theory, several recent works
have derived coding theorems and practical algorithms.
Ma et al.
[
24
]
proved a semantic channel coding
theorem for many-to-one sources, where multiple source
sequences express the same meaning, using a generalized Fano’s
inequality.
Han et al.
[
14
]
proposed an extended
Blahut–Arimoto (EBA) algorithm to compute the semantic
rate-distortion function under synonymous mappings, and further
integrated it with simulated annealing to find optimal
synonymous mappings.
Liang et al.
[
21
]
introduced semantic
arithmetic coding (SAC), achieving higher compression efficiency
than classical arithmetic coding by performing encoding over
synonymous sets.
These works provide valuable coding-theoretic results and
computational methods for achieving semantic compression and
transmission limits within the synonymous-mapping paradigm, and
invite further investigation into alternative compression
mechanisms grounded in logical structure.
Our work complements these contributions by approaching the
fundamental limits from a
logical-structural
perspective:
the minimum blocklength for closure-reliable communication is
determined by
\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|
rather than
\log|S_{O}|

(Theorem
4.6
and
Corollary
4.4
), where the compression
mechanism is receiver-side deductive inference rather than
source-side synonymous collapsing.

Heterogeneous and Multi-Agent Semantic
Communication.

The challenge of heterogeneous knowledge bases in multi-agent
systems has gained increasing attention.
Seo et al.
[
32
]
addressed the inverse
contextual reasoning problem, where an agent must infer the
communication context of others from noisy observations, using
Bayesian inference and Markov Chain Monte Carlo methods.
Alshammari and Bennis
[
3
]
proposed a
logic-driven semantic communication framework for resilient
multi-agent systems, formalizing epistemic and action resilience
using temporal epistemic logic and Kripke structures.
These works advance multi-agent semantic communication from
algorithmic and logical-resilience perspectives, respectively,
and open the door to further study of the coding-theoretic
limits of communication under vocabulary mismatch.
Our work addresses this aspect by introducing an
overlap
decomposition

(Definition
5.4
) that characterizes
vocabulary mismatch via core loss
(A_{-}^{ij})
and
non-derivable surplus
(S_{+,n}^{ij})
, and by deriving
minimum-blocklength bounds and identifying a
semantic
bottleneck
phenomenon
(Proposition
5.9
) that complement
the algorithmic and resilience-oriented analyses
of
[
32
,
3
]
.

Goal-Oriented and Task-Oriented
Communications.

Goal-oriented communication has emerged as a prominent
paradigm, where the effectiveness of communication is measured
by task performance rather than bit-level
accuracy
[
36
,
18
]
.
Li et al.
[
20
]
proposed a goal-oriented tensor
framework that unifies various significance metrics such as Age
of Information, Value of Information, and Age of Incorrect
Information.
Wu et al.
[
40
]
introduced information-theoretic
metrics including semantic entropy, semantic distortion, and
semantic communication rate, and provided guidelines for
designing interpretable semantic communication systems.
These works establish a rich landscape of task-specific fidelity
metrics and motivate further exploration of how such metrics
relate to the logical structure of the communicated content.
Our distortion measures
(Definitions
4.13
–
4.15
)
offer one such connection: the composite semantic distortion

d_{\mathrm{sem}}=\alpha\,d_{H}+\beta\,d_{\text{Cn}}+\gamma\,d_{\text{Dd}}

subsumes Hamming distortion (classical), closure distortion
(deductive content), and depth distortion (inferential
complexity) as special cases, and the semantic Fano bound
(Theorem
4.3
) links these proof-system-aware
distortion measures to mutual information, offering a
perspective that complements the metric-design viewpoint
of
[
20
,
40
]
.

Logical and Database Foundations.

The logical substrate of our framework draws on descriptive
complexity and database theory.
Immerman
[
15
]
established the
correspondence between
\mathrm{FO(LFP)}
and polynomial
time on ordered finite structures, which we use to ensure
computational tractability of all semantic operations
(Assumption
3.1
).
Marx
[
25
]
introduced submodular width as a
complexity measure for conjunctive query evaluation, and
Abo Khamis and Chen
[
2
]
recently
proposed the Jaguar algorithm achieving

O(N^{\mathrm{subw}(Q)+\epsilon})
-time query evaluation.
Mu
[
26
]
studied the identification of
formula roles in inconsistency, introducing causal and
informational responsibility measures that parallel, at a
methodological level, our distinction between irredundant core
elements and redundant stored shortcuts.
These results from logic and database theory provide the
technical foundations on which our framework builds, and they
suggest further opportunities for cross-fertilization with
communication theory.
In our framework, the irredundant core
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
serves as
the minimal generating set for a deductive closure
(Definition
3.8
,
Proposition
3.3
), and its cardinality
directly determines the minimum number of channel uses under
closure reliability
(Corollary
4.4
)—a connection between
proof-system structure and communication fundamental limits that,
to the best of our knowledge, has not been previously
established.

Summary of Positioning.

The works reviewed above have made substantial contributions to
semantic information measures, coding algorithms, multi-agent
coordination, goal-oriented metrics, and logical complexity,
collectively advancing the field toward a mature theory of
semantic communication.
Our work aims to build upon these contributions by integrating

formal proof systems
with
Shannon-theoretic tools
,
with the goal of deriving
computable semantic channel
invariants
and
deductive compression gains
applicable
to heterogeneous multi-agent settings.
We view our framework as complementary to the existing body of
work: the synonymous-mapping
approach
[
28
]
captures source-side
semantic equivalence, the deep-learning
approaches
[
41
,
30
]
provide practical
system implementations, the multi-agent
frameworks
[
32
,
3
]
address
coordination and resilience, and the
logical-complexity
literature
[
15
,
25
]

supplies the mathematical substrate.
Our contribution is to connect these threads through a unified
coding-theoretic framework in which the proof-system structure
of the communicated knowledge base enters directly into the
channel capacity, distortion, and blocklength expressions.

III
System Model and Axiomatic Foundations

This section lays the formal foundations on which the semantic channel theory
and the associated information metrics are built.
The framework is organized in five layers:
(i) a fixed many-sorted logical language
\mathcal{L}
, a designated semantic
sublanguage
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and an effective proof system;
(ii) a two-domain
information model
with time-indexed semantic and carrier state
sets
(S_{O},S_{C})
linked by a computable enabling mechanism;
(iii) an
ideal-information
notion characterized by order-preserving semantic synonymy

S_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
;
(iv) an
irredundant semantic core

\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and a base-relative

derivation-depth
stratification
\text{Dd}(\cdot\mid B)
, both induced by the immediate
consequence operator
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
; and
(v) a
noisy-information
framework that extends the ideal theory to non-ideal carrier
realizations via set perturbations of
S_{O}
.
Together these components supply the deterministic and logical substrate to which
probabilistic structure—Markov kernels, source distributions, semantic distortion
measures—is adjoined in Section
IV
to define the
semantic channel

and its invariants.

III-A

Underlying Logical System
\mathcal{L}
and Expressible State Sets

Throughout we fix a many-sorted logical language
\mathcal{L}
, taken to be
first-order logic with least fixed-point operators
\mathrm{FO(LFP)}

[
15
]
, extended with multiple sorts including at least

\mathsf{Obj}
(objects/entities),
\mathsf{Time}
(time points), and

\mathsf{Carrier}
(physical carriers).

Assumption 3.1

(Finite ordered structures (standing convention))
.

We restrict attention to
finite

\mathcal{L}
-structures.
Moreover, whenever descriptive-complexity claims are invoked, we work over
ordered
finite
structures, i.e., structures equipped with a built-in linear order (e.g., a distinguished binary
relation symbol
<
interpreting a total order on the active domain(s)).
This convention is used solely to justify the standard correspondence that
\mathrm{FO(LFP)}

captures polynomial time on ordered finite structures

[
27
,
38
]
.

Remark 3.1

(Ordered representations vs. semantic content)
.

The built-in linear order on finite structures is used only as a
representation artifact

to support descriptive-complexity statements (e.g., that
\mathrm{FO(LFP)}
captures PTIME on ordered
finite structures). It is treated as an element of
\Sigma_{\mathrm{rep}}
and is therefore excluded from
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}

(Assumption
3.2
).

Assumption 3.2

(Designated semantic sublanguage via a semantic signature)
.

Fix the non-logical signature
\Sigma
of the ambient language
\mathcal{L}
.
We assume a fixed partition

\Sigma\;=\;\Sigma_{\mathrm{sem}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Sigma_{\mathrm{rep}},

where
\Sigma_{\mathrm{rep}}
contains auxiliary representation/bookkeeping symbols
(in particular, the built-in order symbol
<
whenever present).

Define the designated semantic sublanguage
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
to be the restriction of

\mathcal{L}
to the semantic signature:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}\;:=\;\mathcal{L}\!\upharpoonright_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{sem}}},

For example, if
\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{FO(LFP)}[\Sigma]
, then

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}=\mathrm{FO(LFP)}[\Sigma_{\mathrm{sem}}]
.

Disclosure convention.

Every non-logical symbol used in formal definitions is understood to belong to exactly one of

\Sigma_{\mathrm{sem}}
or
\Sigma_{\mathrm{rep}}
; symbols are semantic by default unless explicitly
declared representation-only.
All notions of semantic indistinguishability and definable recoding in
Section
III-C
are taken relative to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
.

Since we work with finite
\mathcal{L}
-structures, model checking

(\mathfrak{R}\models\varphi)
is decidable:
given a finite encoding of
\mathfrak{R}
and a sentence
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}
, one can
decide whether
\mathfrak{R}\models\varphi
.
(For
\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{FO(LFP)}
on ordered finite structures, the
data complexity
—i.e.,
the complexity measured in the size of
\mathfrak{R}
for a fixed formula—is polynomial

[
27
,
38
]
;
the
combined complexity
in both
|\mathfrak{R}|
and
|\varphi|
is higher.
Only effective decidability, not a specific complexity bound, is used in the sequel.)
Assumption
3.1
is used only when invoking descriptive-complexity correspondences
(e.g.,
\mathrm{FO(LFP)}
capturing PTIME on
ordered
finite structures).
This semantic decidability is used only for the evaluation of
\mathcal{L}
-descriptions on finite structures,
and should be kept conceptually separate from syntactic derivability in the proof system fixed below.

Remark 3.2

(Definability and effective enumerability on finite structures)
.

Since the ambient structure
\mathfrak{R}
is finite
(Assumption
3.1
), the extension of any

\mathcal{L}
-definable (or
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable) relation in

\mathfrak{R}
can be computed by exhaustive model checking over the finite domain.
In particular, for any
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable state set
S
in

\mathfrak{R}
, the set of pairs
(x,t)
satisfying
S(x,t)
is effectively enumerable.
This bridge between definability and computability is invoked without further comment
whenever a definable relation is treated as computable data.

Definition 3.1

(Expressible object–time state sets)
.

Let
\mathfrak{R}
be a finite
\mathcal{L}
-structure and let
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
be a fixed sublanguage.

Assume that
x
(resp.
t
) ranges over a fixed object sort (resp. time sort) of
\mathcal{L}
.
We allow
X
and
T
to be
definable subdomains
of these sorts: namely,
X
and
T
are specified by unary

\mathcal{L}
-formulas
\delta_{X}(x)
and
\delta_{T}(t)
.
(When
X
or
T
is the full carrier of its sort, the corresponding
\delta
-formula can be taken as
\top
.)

Language convention for subdomains.

The subdomain formulas
\delta_{X}
and
\delta_{T}
are permitted to belong to the full ambient language
\mathcal{L}
,
not only to the sublanguage
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
. Thus the domains
X
and
T
are fixed as part of the ambient structure,
while the
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-definability requirement below applies only to the state relation
S
itself.

An
(object–time) state domain
over
(X,T)
is an
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-definable
binary relation
S(x,t)
such that

\mathfrak{R}\models\forall x\,\forall t\ \bigl(S(x,t)\rightarrow(\delta_{X}(x)\wedge\delta_{T}(t))\bigr).

We read
S(x,t)
as “
x
is in a (well-formed) state at time
t
”.

A set of states
A\subseteq X\times T
is
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-
expressible
(over
S
)
if there exists an
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-formula
\varphi(x,t)
such that for all
x,t
,

(x,t)\in A\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathfrak{R}\models S(x,t)\wedge\varphi(x,t).

Convention (state notation as definitional abbreviation).

Whenever convenient, we write a state variable
s\in S
as a meta-level abbreviation for a pair
(x,t)

with
S(x,t)
. Under this convention,

\mathsf{time}(s)=t,\qquad\text{and (when needed) }\mathsf{obj}(s)=x,

and quantification over states abbreviates quantification over
(x,t)
guarded by
S(x,t)
.

When
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
, we also say “
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable/expressible”
in this sense.

Assumption 3.3

(Fixed effective proof system for the inference fragment)
.

We fix a syntactic
inference fragment

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}

(typically a decidable rule/KB language such as Datalog or a Horn fragment)
and an effective proof system
\mathsf{PS}
over the syntax of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}
such that
proof checking is decidable.

For any finite
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}
and any
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}
, write

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{kb}}\varphi
to denote derivability in
\mathsf{PS}
, and define the deductive closure operator

\text{Cn}(\Gamma)\;:=\;\{\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}:\ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{kb}}\varphi\}.

Basic closure properties.

The operator
Cn
satisfies three standing properties used throughout without further comment:

(Cn1)

Reflexivity (inclusion of premises):

\Gamma\subseteq\text{Cn}(\Gamma)
for every
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}
.

(Cn2)

Monotonicity (weakening):

if
\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
, then
\text{Cn}(\Gamma)\subseteq\text{Cn}(\Gamma^{\prime})
.

(Cn3)

Idempotence (cut):

\text{Cn}\bigl(\text{Cn}(\Gamma)\bigr)=\text{Cn}(\Gamma)
for every
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}
.
Equivalently: if
\Delta\subseteq\text{Cn}(\Gamma)
, then
\text{Cn}(\Delta)\subseteq\text{Cn}(\Gamma)
.

Properties
(Cn1)
and
(Cn2)
follow from the standard weakening rule;
(Cn3)

follows from the transitivity (cut) property of derivability: if every premise in a derivation of

\varphi
from
\Delta
is itself derivable from
\Gamma
, then
\varphi
is derivable
from
\Gamma
.

State–formula convention.

When elements of a state set
S_{O}
are used as premises for
\text{Cn}(\cdot)

(Section
III-D
), each state
(o,\tau)\in S_{O}
is identified with the
corresponding ground atom
S_{O}(o,\tau)\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}
.

III-B

Computable Information Axioms and Information Model

Basic objects (preview).

We fix semantic/carrier domains with time-indexed state sets; the precise assumptions are stated in
Axioms
3.1
–
3.3
.

Definition 3.2

(State spaces as object–time relations (notation))
.

Information will be modeled using domains
O,C
with time domains
T_{O},T_{C}
and state sets

S_{O}\subseteq O\times T_{O}
and
S_{C}\subseteq C\times T_{C}
.
We write
s_{o}=(o,\tau)\in S_{O}
and
s_{c}=(c,\theta)\in S_{C}
, where the second coordinate is the time index.

Axiom 3.1

(Time domains, indexing, and precedence)
.

Fix a finite many-sorted
\mathcal{L}
-structure
\mathfrak{R}
.
Let
O,C,T_{O},T_{C}
be
\mathcal{L}
-definable domains in
\mathfrak{R}
, and let
S_{O}\subseteq O\times T_{O}
and
S_{C}\subseteq C\times T_{C}
be

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable state sets (i.e., definable by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-formulas)
over the
\mathcal{L}
-definable domains
O,T_{O}
and
C,T_{C}
in the sense of
Definition
3.1
(with
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
).

Time indexing is the second coordinate. For
s_{o}=(o,\tau)\in S_{O}
and
s_{c}=(c,\theta)\in S_{C}
, define

\mathsf{time}_{O}(s_{o}):=\tau,\qquad\mathsf{time}_{C}(s_{c}):=\theta,

as definitional abbreviations.

There exist
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable relations
\prec_{O}\subseteq T_{O}\times T_{O}
,

\prec_{C}\subseteq T_{C}\times T_{C}
, and
\prec_{OC}\subseteq T_{O}\times T_{C}
such that:

(T1)

(T_{O},\prec_{O})
and
(T_{C},\prec_{C})
are linearly ordered time domains.

(T2)

Cross-domain compatibility:

\prec_{OC}
is monotone with respect to
\prec_{O}
and
\prec_{C}
, i.e.,
for all
\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in T_{O}
and
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in T_{C}
,

(\tau^{\prime}\prec_{O}\tau\ \wedge\ \tau\prec_{OC}\theta)\ \Rightarrow\ \tau^{\prime}\prec_{OC}\theta,

and

(\tau\prec_{OC}\theta\ \wedge\ \theta\prec_{C}\theta^{\prime})\ \Rightarrow\ \tau\prec_{OC}\theta^{\prime}.

(T3)

Nontriviality (mild totality):
for every
\tau\in T_{O}
there exists
\theta\in T_{C}
such that

\tau\prec_{OC}\theta
.

Axiom 3.2

(State representation)
.

There exist injective encodings

\text{enc}_{O}:S_{O}\to\{0,1\}^{*},\qquad\text{enc}_{C}:S_{C}\to\{0,1\}^{*}

such that the following predicates are decidable from the codes:

(R1)

Semantic-time precedence:
given
\text{enc}_{O}(s_{o})
and
\text{enc}_{O}(s_{o}^{\prime})
, decide whether

\mathsf{time}_{O}(s_{o})\prec_{O}\mathsf{time}_{O}(s_{o}^{\prime})
.

(R2)

Carrier-time precedence:
given
\text{enc}_{C}(s_{c})
and
\text{enc}_{C}(s_{c}^{\prime})
, decide whether

\mathsf{time}_{C}(s_{c})\prec_{C}\mathsf{time}_{C}(s_{c}^{\prime})
.

(R3)

Cross-domain precedence:
given
\text{enc}_{O}(s_{o})
and
\text{enc}_{C}(s_{c})
, decide whether

\mathsf{time}_{O}(s_{o})\prec_{OC}\mathsf{time}_{C}(s_{c})
.

(R4)

Membership (optional):
given
w\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, decide whether
w\in\text{enc}_{O}(S_{O})
(resp.
w\in\text{enc}_{C}(S_{C})
).

Axiom 3.3

(Enabling mapping (realization mechanism))
.

There exists a relation

R_{\mathcal{E}}\ \subseteq\ O\times T_{O}\times C\times T_{C},

and the induced set-valued map
\mathcal{E}:S_{O}\Rightarrow S_{C}
, defined for
s_{o}=(o,\tau)\in S_{O}
by

\mathcal{E}(s_{o})\;:=\;\{\,(c,\theta)\in S_{C}:\ (o,\tau,c,\theta)\in R_{\mathcal{E}}\,\},

such that:

(E1)

Totality on semantic states:

for every
s_{o}\in S_{O}
,
\mathcal{E}(s_{o})\neq\varnothing
.

(E2)

Coverage of the carrier state space:

for every
s_{c}\in S_{C}
, there exists
s_{o}\in S_{O}
with
s_{c}\in\mathcal{E}(s_{o})
.
Equivalently,
\bigcup_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\mathcal{E}(s_{o})=S_{C}
.

(E3)

Computable enabling selector:

there exists a (partial) computable function
e:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}^{*}
that is total on
\text{enc}_{O}(S_{O})
,
such that for every
s_{o}\in S_{O}
,
e(\text{enc}_{O}(s_{o}))
halts and equals
\text{enc}_{C}(s_{c})
for some
s_{c}\in\mathcal{E}(s_{o})
.

(E4)

Precedence compatibility:

if
(o,\tau)\in S_{O}
,
(c,\theta)\in S_{C}
, and
(o,\tau,c,\theta)\in R_{\mathcal{E}}
,
then
\tau\prec_{OC}\theta
.

Remark 3.3

(Notes on the enabling mapping)
.

(i)
Unless stated otherwise, the relation
R_{\mathcal{E}}
is treated as meta-level instance data and is

not
assumed to be
\mathcal{L}
-definable or
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable.
When a definable channel law is needed, one may add the extra assumption that
R_{\mathcal{E}}
is

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable.

(ii)
Clause
(E2)
does not claim that
S_{C}
contains all physically possible carrier states;

S_{C}
comprises the admissible semantic-bearing carrier states, and pure noise states can be modeled
outside
S_{C}
.

(iii)
Clause
(E3)
requires only a computable
selector
that produces one admissible realization

s_{c}\in\mathcal{E}(s_{o})
per
s_{o}
; it does not imply that the full relation
R_{\mathcal{E}}
or the full
fiber
\mathcal{E}(s_{o})
is decidable or effectively enumerable.

Definition 3.3

(Information model)
.

An
information model
is the tuple

\mathcal{I}\;=\;\langle O,\;T_{O},\;S_{O},\;C,\;T_{C},\;S_{C},\;R_{\mathcal{E}}\rangle,

together with the temporal structure (orders and cross-domain precedence) postulated in
Axiom
3.1
.

Remark 3.4

(Background data suppressed from the instance notation)
.

For brevity, we suppress from the tuple notation of
\mathcal{I}
both
(i) the temporal relations
\prec_{O},\prec_{C},\prec_{OC}
specified by Axiom
3.1
, and
(ii) the representation/encoding maps
\text{enc}_{O},\text{enc}_{C}
postulated in Axiom
3.2
.
All of these are treated as background instance data fixed throughout the paper.

Definition 3.4

(Composition of information models)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
be information models
(Definition
3.3
) with induced enabling maps

\mathcal{E}_{1}:S_{O}^{(1)}\Rightarrow S_{C}^{(1)}
and

\mathcal{E}_{2}:S_{O}^{(2)}\Rightarrow S_{C}^{(2)}
, respectively.
The pair
(\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2})
is
composable
if

S_{C}^{(1)}=S_{O}^{(2)}
as state sets (with compatible encodings).

For a composable pair, the
composite information model

\mathcal{I}_{2}\circ\mathcal{I}_{1}
is the information model whose semantic state set is

S_{O}^{(1)}
, whose carrier state set is
S_{C}^{(2)}
, and whose

composite enabling map

\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}:S_{O}^{(1)}\Rightarrow S_{C}^{(2)}
is defined by

\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}(s_{o})\;:=\;\bigcup_{s_{c}\,\in\,\mathcal{E}_{1}(s_{o})}\mathcal{E}_{2}(s_{c}),\qquad s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(1)}.

(3.1)

The remaining instance data (domains, time indices, cross-domain precedence) of the
composite model are inherited from the constituents in the natural way and are suppressed
from notation following the convention of Remark
3.4
.

Proposition 3.1

(Composition preserves enabling axioms)
.

Let
(\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2})
be a composable pair of information models, each
satisfying Axiom
3.3
.
Then the composite enabling map

\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}
of
\mathcal{I}_{2}\circ\mathcal{I}_{1}
satisfies:

(i)

Totality (E1):

\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}(s_{o})\neq\varnothing
for every

s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(1)}
.

(ii)

Coverage (E2):

\displaystyle\bigcup_{s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(1)}}\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}(s_{o})=S_{C}^{(2)}
.

(iii)

Computable selector (E3):

if
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are computable enabling selectors for
\mathcal{I}_{1}

and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
respectively, then
e_{2\circ 1}:=e_{2}\circ e_{1}
is a
computable enabling selector for
\mathcal{I}_{2}\circ\mathcal{I}_{1}
.

(i) For any
s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(1)}
, totality of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
gives some

s_{c}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(s_{o})
.
Since
s_{c}\in S_{C}^{(1)}=S_{O}^{(2)}
, totality of

\mathcal{E}_{2}
gives
\mathcal{E}_{2}(s_{c})\neq\varnothing
, so

\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}(s_{o})\supseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}(s_{c})\neq\varnothing
.

(ii) Take any
s^{\prime}\in S_{C}^{(2)}
.
Coverage of
\mathcal{E}_{2}
yields

s_{c}\in S_{O}^{(2)}=S_{C}^{(1)}
with
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(s_{c})
.
Coverage of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
then yields
s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(1)}
with

s_{c}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(s_{o})
, whence

s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}(s_{o})
.

(iii) The selector
e_{1}
is total on codes of
S_{O}^{(1)}
and outputs codes of
elements in
\mathcal{E}_{1}(s_{o})\subseteq S_{C}^{(1)}=S_{O}^{(2)}
;

e_{2}
is total on codes of
S_{O}^{(2)}
.
Hence
e_{2\circ 1}=e_{2}\circ e_{1}
is total on codes of
S_{O}^{(1)}
,
computable as a composition of computable functions, and satisfies

e_{2\circ 1}(s_{o})\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(e_{1}(s_{o}))\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}(s_{o})
.
∎

Remark 3.5

(Associativity and iterated composition)
.

Composition of enabling maps is associative: for a composable triple

(\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2},\mathcal{I}_{3})
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3\circ(2\circ 1)}(s_{o})\;=\;

\displaystyle\bigcup_{s\,\in\,\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}(s_{o})}\mathcal{E}_{3}(s)

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\bigcup_{s_{c}\,\in\,\mathcal{E}_{1}(s_{o})}\;\bigcup_{s\,\in\,\mathcal{E}_{2}(s_{c})}\mathcal{E}_{3}(s)

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{(3\circ 2)\circ 1}(s_{o}),

so
(\mathcal{I}_{3}\circ\mathcal{I}_{2})\circ\mathcal{I}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{3}\circ(\mathcal{I}_{2}\circ\mathcal{I}_{1})
.
Consequently, an iterated composition

\mathcal{I}_{n}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{I}_{1}
is unambiguously defined whenever
consecutive pairs are composable.
This allows any end-to-end information pipeline to be modeled as a single
composite information model at the coarsest granularity, or decomposed into
sub-models at any desired level of refinement; the two viewpoints yield the same
enabling structure.

III-C

Synonymous State Sets and Ideal Information

This subsection formalizes when two state sets carry the same
semantic content

under an
ideal
(lossless) notion of synonymy. In the ideal setting adopted here,
temporal precedence is part of the semantic structure; hence any synonymy must preserve it.

Semantic sublanguage.

All definability requirements below are taken relative to the designated semantic sublanguage

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
from Assumption
3.2
.

Induced precedence on states.

Let
S\subseteq X\times T
be a state set and let
\prec
be a linear order on
T
.
For
s=(x,t)
and
s^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})
in
S
, define the induced strict precedence relation

s\prec_{S}s^{\prime}\quad:\Longleftrightarrow\quad t\prec t^{\prime}.

Definition 3.5

(
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable coding isomorphism between state sets)
.

Let
S_{1}\subseteq X_{1}\times T_{1}
and
S_{2}\subseteq X_{2}\times T_{2}
be
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable
state sets in the ambient structure
\mathfrak{R}
.

A relation
G_{12}\subseteq S_{1}\times S_{2}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable coding isomorphism graph

from
S_{1}
to
S_{2}
if there exists an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-formula
\psi(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2})
such that
for all
x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2}
,

\displaystyle G_{12}\bigl((x_{1},t_{1}),(x_{2},t_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow S_{1}(x_{1},t_{1})\wedge S_{2}(x_{2},t_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\wedge\psi(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2}),

and
G_{12}
is a bijection between
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
in the sense of
(B1)
–
(B2)
below:

(B1)

for every
s_{1}\in S_{1}
there exists a unique
s_{2}\in S_{2}
with
G_{12}(s_{1},s_{2})
;

(B2)

for every
s_{2}\in S_{2}
there exists a unique
s_{1}\in S_{1}
with
G_{12}(s_{1},s_{2})
.

The uniqueness in
(B1)
–
(B2)
induces mutually inverse maps

\tau_{12}:S_{1}\to S_{2}
and
\tau_{21}:S_{2}\to S_{1}
.

Definition 3.6

(Synonymous state sets (ideal, order-preserving))
.

Let
S_{1}\subseteq X_{1}\times T_{1}
and
S_{2}\subseteq X_{2}\times T_{2}
be

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable state sets in
\mathfrak{R}
, equipped with
linear orders
\prec_{1}
on
T_{1}
and
\prec_{2}
on
T_{2}
.
We say
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are
synonymous
(in the ideal, order-sensitive sense), written

S_{1}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{2},

if there exists an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable coding isomorphism graph
G_{12}\subseteq S_{1}\times S_{2}

(Definition
3.5
) inducing a bijection
\tau_{12}:S_{1}\to S_{2}
such that
\tau_{12}
is a
time-order isomorphism
for the induced precedence on states, i.e.,

s\prec_{S_{1}}s^{\prime}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \tau_{12}(s)\prec_{S_{2}}\tau_{12}(s^{\prime})\quad(\forall s,s^{\prime}\in S_{1}),

where
\prec_{S_{i}}
is induced from
\prec_{i}
via
(x,t)\prec_{S_{i}}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\Longleftrightarrow t\prec_{i}t^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.6

(Nonlinearity, time-slice constraints, and role of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
)
.

The induced relation
\prec_{S}
compares states only by their time indices and is generally not a linear order
when multiple states share the same time point.
The preservation/reflection condition in Definition
3.6
implies that
for each time point
t\in T_{1}
, every state
(x,t)\in S_{1}
is mapped by
\tau_{12}

to a state whose time index is the same value
t^{\prime}\in T_{2}

(since states at the same time are pairwise
\prec_{S_{1}}
-incomparable, and
\tau_{12}

preserves and reflects the induced precedence).
Hence
\tau_{12}
restricts to a bijection between the time-slices

\{x:(x,t)\in S_{1}\}
and
\{y:(y,t^{\prime})\in S_{2}\}
, and corresponding time-slices must have
equal cardinality.
Restricting coding/decoding definability to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
prevents auxiliary representation predicates
(e.g., a built-in order used only for encoding) from trivializing equivalence or enabling arbitrary matchings
between equipotent finite sets.

Proposition 3.2

(
\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}
is an equivalence relation)
.

The relation
\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}
on
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable state sets is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive.

Reflexivity is witnessed by the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable identity graph.
Symmetry follows by swapping coordinates in the witnessing graph.
For transitivity, let
G_{12}\subseteq S_{1}\times S_{2}
and
G_{23}\subseteq S_{2}\times S_{3}
be the two witnessing
graphs. Define the composed graph by

G_{13}(s_{1},s_{3}):\Leftrightarrow\exists\,s_{2}\,[G_{12}(s_{1},s_{2})\wedge G_{23}(s_{2},s_{3})]
,
where, following the state notation convention of Definition
3.1
,
the existential quantifier
\exists\,s_{2}
abbreviates
\exists\,x_{2}\,\exists\,t_{2}\,[S_{2}(x_{2},t_{2})\wedge\cdots]
.
This is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable since
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
is closed under
first-order connectives and quantification over its own sorts.
The uniqueness clauses (B1)–(B2) of each component graph ensure that
G_{13}
again
satisfies (B1)–(B2), so the induced map is a bijection that preserves and reflects
the induced time precedence by composition of the two order isomorphisms.
∎

Definition 3.7

(Ideal information)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}=\langle O,T_{O},S_{O},C,T_{C},S_{C},R_{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
be an information model.
We call
\mathcal{I}

ideal (with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
)
if there exists an

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable coding isomorphism graph
G_{OC}\subseteq S_{O}\times S_{C}

witnessing
S_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
, with induced bijection
\tau_{OC}:S_{O}\to S_{C}
, such that the enabling map is
singleton-valued and agrees with
\tau_{OC}
: for every
s_{o}\in S_{O}
,

\mathcal{E}(s_{o})=\{\tau_{OC}(s_{o})\}.

Remark 3.7

(Cross-sort definability, witness dependence, and canonical representative)
.

The requirement
S_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
is nontrivial in a many-sorted setting: the witnessing

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-formula must relate variables of the object sort and the carrier sort.
If
\Sigma_{\mathrm{sem}}
contains no relations or functions that can link these sorts
(directly or indirectly), then no definable coding isomorphism exists and the instance cannot be ideal.
Moreover, the witnessing isomorphism
\tau_{OC}
need not be unique; hence the pointwise identification
between
S_{O}
and
S_{C}
depends on the chosen witness.

Convention (fixing a witness and canonical representative).

Whenever we restrict attention to an ideal instance, we fix one witnessing graph
G_{OC}
and its induced bijection

\tau_{OC}:S_{O}\to S_{C}
(with inverse
\tau_{CO}
) once and for all, identify
S_{O}
and
S_{C}
via this choice,
and take
S_{O}
as the canonical semantic representative of
\mathcal{I}
.
With a mild abuse of notation, we may refer to the “semantics of
\mathcal{I}
” simply as
S_{O}
.

When
\mathcal{I}
is not ideal, one can still analyze its semantic structure
through a carrier-equivalent semantic component; this is the subject of the noisy information
framework developed in Section
III-E
.

III-D

Irredundant Cores and Derivation Depth

This subsection introduces two concepts unified by the immediate consequence operator

T_{\mathsf{PS}}
of the fixed proof system:
(i) an
irredundant semantic core

\text{Atom}(S_{O})
, defined via the deductive closure

\text{Cn}=T_{\mathsf{PS}}^{\omega}
, that separates intrinsic vs. operational inference;
(ii) a base-relative
derivation depth

\text{Dd}(\cdot\mid B)
, defined as the stratum index
of the
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
-iteration from
B
, that measures the minimum number of
single-step inferences needed to reach a given formula.

Ambient semantic state space.

We fix a countable set
\mathbb{S}_{O}\supseteq S_{O}
of potential semantic states.
Under the State–formula convention of Assumption
3.3
, each element of

\mathbb{S}_{O}
is identified with a ground atom of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}

(extending the identification of
S_{O}
-elements with atoms of the form
S_{O}(o,\tau)
).
In particular, the derivability relation
\vdash_{\mathrm{kb}}
and the operator

T_{\mathsf{PS}}
introduced below act on (subsets of)
\mathbb{S}_{O}
via this
identification.

The set
\mathbb{S}_{O}
is equipped with an injective encoding

\text{enc}_{O}:\mathbb{S}_{O}\to\{0,1\}^{*}
extending that of
S_{O}

(Axiom
3.2
) and an extension of
\mathsf{time}_{O}
to
\mathbb{S}_{O}
,
such that membership in
\mathbb{S}_{O}
is decidable from the codes
(i.e., given
w\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, one can decide whether
w\in\text{enc}_{O}(\mathbb{S}_{O})
).
Further standing properties of
\mathbb{S}_{O}

(closure under definable recodings) are specified in Assumption
3.6
.

Available premise bases.

A finite set
B\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
is called an
available premise base
if its elements are treated as
depth-
0
premises when measuring derivation depth from
B
.

Assumption 3.4

(Finite and effectively listable knowledge bases)
.

The knowledge bases
S_{O}
considered in this paper are finite and effectively listable under a fixed canonical order.

Assumption 3.5

(Effective redundancy test (core extractability))
.

For the knowledge bases
S_{O}
considered, the predicate

s\in\text{Cn}(\Gamma)

is decidable whenever
\Gamma\subseteq S_{O}
is finite and
s\in S_{O}
.
(Equivalently: redundancy of a stored formula relative to the remaining stored formulas is decidable.)

Remark 3.8

(When Assumption
3.5
is reasonable)
.

Assumption
3.5
is strong for unrestricted logics, but it is satisfied in many
practically relevant decidable fragments used for knowledge bases and rule
systems
[
7
,
1
,
10
]
(e.g., Datalog/Horn-style
settings and bounded-domain theories), where entailment and redundancy checking are decidable and often tractable;
see, e.g.,
[
1
,
10
]
.

Definition 3.8

(Semantic atomic core (canonical irredundant generating set))
.

Let
S_{O}
be a finite knowledge base.
Define
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
to be the output of the following fixed deterministic
irredundantization
procedure:

(i)

Initialize
A\leftarrow S_{O}
.

(ii)

Scan elements of
S_{O}
in the fixed canonical order; for each
s\in S_{O}
, if

s\in\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})
, then set
A\leftarrow A\setminus\{s\}
.

(iii)

Output
A
and denote it by
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
.

Proposition 3.3

(Core correctness: equivalence, irredundancy, and derivability)
.

Under Assumptions
3.4
and
3.5
, for every finite knowledge base
S_{O}
,
the set
A:=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
satisfies:

(i)

Closure equivalence:

\text{Cn}(A)=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.

(ii)

Irredundancy:
for every
a\in A
,
a\notin\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{a\})
.

(iii)

Canonicality:

\text{Atom}(S_{O})
is uniquely determined by
S_{O}
and the fixed canonical order.

(iv)

Stored derivability:

S_{O}\subseteq\text{Cn}(\text{Atom}(S_{O}))
.

We first verify that each removal step preserves the deductive closure.
Suppose at some stage the current set is
A
and some
s\in A
satisfies

s\in\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})
; we claim
\text{Cn}(A)=\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})
.
Since
A\setminus\{s\}\subseteq A
, monotonicity (Cn2) gives

\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})\subseteq\text{Cn}(A)
.
For the reverse inclusion: reflexivity (Cn1) gives

A\setminus\{s\}\subseteq\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})
, and by hypothesis

s\in\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})
, so
A\subseteq\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})
.
By monotonicity,
\text{Cn}(A)\subseteq\text{Cn}\bigl(\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})\bigr)
, and by
idempotence (Cn3),

\text{Cn}\bigl(\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})\bigr)=\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})
.
Hence
\text{Cn}(A)\subseteq\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{s\})
.

By induction over the finitely many removal steps in the scan of
Definition
3.8
, the final set
A
satisfies

\text{Cn}(A)=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
, proving (i).

For irredundancy (ii): any
a
retained in
A
was
not
removable at its
scan stage, i.e.,
a\notin\text{Cn}(A^{\prime}\setminus\{a\})
for the set
A^{\prime}
at that stage.
Since subsequent removals only shrink
A^{\prime}
to the final
A\subseteq A^{\prime}
,
monotonicity (Cn2) gives

\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{a\})\subseteq\text{Cn}(A^{\prime}\setminus\{a\})
, so

a\notin\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{a\})
.

Canonicality (iii) follows from determinism of the procedure and the fixed
canonical order.

For (iv), reflexivity (Cn1) gives

S_{O}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})=\text{Cn}(A)
by (i).
∎

Definition 3.9

(Intrinsic vs. operational premise bases)
.

For a knowledge base
S_{O}
, define:

\displaystyle A:=\text{Atom}(S_{O})\quad\text{(core premises)},

\displaystyle J:=S_{O}\setminus A\quad\text{(stored shortcuts)}.

We call
A
the
intrinsic
premise base and
A\cup J=S_{O}
the
operational
premise base.

Derivation depth from the immediate consequence operator.

We derive the derivation depth directly from
the proof system
\mathsf{PS}
(Assumption
3.3
) via its

immediate consequence operator
, which simultaneously characterizes
Cn
and stratifies
the derivation process.

Definition 3.10

(Immediate consequence operator)
.

Write
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{kb}}^{1}s
to denote that
s
is derivable from premises in

\Gamma
by
exactly one inference step
(i.e., one rule application) of
\mathsf{PS}
.
For any set
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
, define

T_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma)\;:=\;\Gamma\;\cup\;\bigl\{\,s\in\mathbb{S}_{O}:\,\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{kb}}^{1}s\,\bigr\}.

Define the iteration
T^{0}_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma):=\Gamma
and

T^{n+1}_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma):=T_{\mathsf{PS}}\bigl(T^{n}_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma)\bigr)
for
n\geq 0
.

Axiom 3.4

(Properties of the immediate consequence operator)
.

The operator
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
satisfies:

(IC1)

Monotonicity:

\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
implies
T_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma)\subseteq T_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma^{\prime})
.

(IC2)

Computability:

for any finite
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
(given by the codes of its elements),
the set
T_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma)
is finite and effectively computable.

(IC3)

Closure characterization:

\text{Cn}(\Gamma)=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}T^{n}_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma)
for every finite

\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
.

(IC4)

Finite stabilization:

for every finite
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
, the ascending chain

\bigl(T^{n}_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma)\bigr)_{n\geq 0}
stabilizes in finitely many steps;
i.e., there exists
N<\infty
such that

T^{N+1}_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma)=T^{N}_{\mathsf{PS}}(\Gamma)=\text{Cn}(\Gamma)
.

Remark 3.9

(When Axiom
3.4
holds; derived properties)
.

In Datalog and Horn-clause settings over finite domains, the immediate consequence operator

T_{P}
satisfies all four properties:
monotonicity is a standard property of
T_{P}
;
computability follows from the decidability of single-step rule application;
the closure characterization is the classical van Emden–Kowalski fixpoint
theorem
[
1
]
; and
finite stabilization follows from finiteness of the Herbrand base.
More broadly, Axiom
3.4
holds for any effective proof system whose
single-step relation is decidable and whose ground term universe is finite.

Two useful consequences:

(a)

(IC2)
+
(IC4)
imply that
\text{Cn}(\Gamma)
is
finite

for every finite
\Gamma
;

(b)

(IC2)
+
(IC3)
+
(IC4)
together imply
Assumption
3.5
as a special case (deciding

s\in\text{Cn}(\Gamma)
by iterating
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
from
\Gamma
until stabilization).
We state Assumption
3.5
separately to clarify the minimal
requirements for core extraction independently of the
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
-axiomatics.

Definition 3.11

(Base-relative derivation depth (stratum))
.

Let
B\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
be a finite set of
available premises
.
The
derivation depth
(or
stratum index
) of
s\in\mathbb{S}_{O}
from
B
is

\text{Dd}(s\mid B)\;:=\;\min\bigl\{\,n\geq 0:s\in T^{n}_{\mathsf{PS}}(B)\,\bigr\},

(3.2)

with the convention
\text{Dd}(s\mid B):=\infty
if
s\notin\text{Cn}(B)
.

Remark 3.10

(Recursive characterization)
.

The stratum definition (
3.2
) admits an equivalent recursive form:

\text{Dd}(s\mid B)=0
if
s\in B
, and otherwise

\displaystyle\text{Dd}(s\mid B)=1+\min\Bigl\{

\displaystyle\max_{s^{\prime}\in P}\text{Dd}(s^{\prime}\mid B):

\displaystyle P\subseteq\text{Cn}(B)\text{ finite},\;P\vdash_{\mathrm{kb}}^{1}s\Bigr\}

where the minimum is over all finite premise sets
P
from which
s
is derivable
in one step of
\mathsf{PS}
.
This makes explicit that
\text{Dd}(s\mid B)
equals the depth of the
shallowest
proof tree
of
s
from
B
.

Lemma 3.1

(Properties of derivation depth)
.

Under Axiom
3.4
, for any finite premise base
B\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
:

(i)

For every
s\in\text{Cn}(B)
,
\text{Dd}(s\mid B)
is a unique, finite, computable non-negative integer.

(ii)

\text{Dd}(s\mid B)=0
if and only if
s\in B
.

(iii)

Monotonicity in the base:
if
B\subseteq B^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}

with
B^{\prime}
finite and
s\in\text{Cn}(B)
, then
\text{Dd}(s\mid B^{\prime})\leq\text{Dd}(s\mid B)
.

(i) By (IC4), the iteration
\bigl(T^{n}_{\mathsf{PS}}(B)\bigr)_{n\geq 0}
stabilizes at

\text{Cn}(B)
in finitely many steps.
Since
s\in\text{Cn}(B)
, the set
\{n:s\in T^{n}_{\mathsf{PS}}(B)\}
is nonempty;
its minimum is therefore a unique finite non-negative integer.
Computability: iterate
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
from
B
(computable by (IC2))
and check membership at each stage until
s
appears.

(ii)
\text{Dd}(s\mid B)=0
iff
s\in T^{0}_{\mathsf{PS}}(B)=B
.

(iii) Monotonicity of
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
(IC1) yields, by induction on
n
,

T^{n}_{\mathsf{PS}}(B)\subseteq T^{n}_{\mathsf{PS}}(B^{\prime})
for all
n\geq 0
.
Hence

\min\{n:s\in T^{n}_{\mathsf{PS}}(B^{\prime})\}\leq\min\{n:s\in T^{n}_{\mathsf{PS}}(B)\}
,
i.e.,
\text{Dd}(s\mid B^{\prime})\leq\text{Dd}(s\mid B)
.
∎

Definition 3.12

(Intrinsic and operational derivation depths)
.

Let
S_{O}
be a knowledge base with core
A:=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
.
For any
q\in\text{Cn}(A)=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
, define

n_{\mathrm{int}}(q):=\text{Dd}(q\mid A),\qquad n_{\mathrm{op}}(q):=\text{Dd}(q\mid S_{O}).

Definition 3.13

(Semantic atomicity measure)
.

The
semantic atomicity
of an information model
\mathcal{I}
with semantic space
S_{O}
is

\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{I})\;:=\;\lvert\text{Atom}(S_{O})\rvert.

Definition 3.14

(Maximum intrinsic derivation depth)
.

The
maximum intrinsic derivation depth
of an information model
\mathcal{I}
with semantic space
S_{O}
is

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})\;:=\;\max_{q\,\in\,S_{O}}\;\text{Dd}\bigl(q\mid\text{Atom}(S_{O})\bigr),

with the convention
\max\varnothing:=0
when
S_{O}=\varnothing
.

Theorem 3.1

(Computable semantic invariants of an ideal information model)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an ideal information model

(Definition
3.7
)
with canonical semantic representative
S_{O}
.
Under Assumptions
3.4
–
3.5

and Axiom
3.4
,
the following hold.

(i)

Well-definedness and uniqueness.

The semantic atomicity
\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{I})=\lvert\text{Atom}(S_{O})\rvert
and the maximum intrinsic derivation depth

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})=\max_{q\in S_{O}}\text{Dd}(q\mid\text{Atom}(S_{O}))

are uniquely determined by
S_{O}
and the fixed canonical order.

(ii)

Finiteness and computability.

Both
\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{I})
and
\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})
are finite non-negative integers,
and are computable from
S_{O}
.

(iii)

Monotonicity of derivation depth.

For every
q\in S_{O}
,

n_{\mathrm{op}}(q)\;\leq\;n_{\mathrm{int}}(q)\;\leq\;\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I}).

(iv)

Characterization of zero depth.

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})=0
if and only if
\text{Atom}(S_{O})=S_{O}

(
i.e.,
S_{O}
is already irredundant
)
.

(i)
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
is uniquely determined by Proposition
3.3
(iii);
hence
\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{I})
is unique.
By Proposition
3.3
(iv),
every
q\in S_{O}
lies in
\text{Cn}(\text{Atom}(S_{O}))
,
so the stratum
\text{Dd}(q\mid\text{Atom}(S_{O}))
is unique by Lemma
3.1
(i),
and
\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})
is unique.

(ii)
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
is computable by the deterministic procedure of Definition
3.8

under Assumptions
3.4
–
3.5
,
so
\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{I})=|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|
is computable.
By Lemma
3.1
(i),
each
\text{Dd}(q\mid\text{Atom}(S_{O}))
is finite and computable.
Since
S_{O}
is finite and effectively listable (Assumption
3.4
),
the maximum
\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})
is computable by enumeration.

(iii) Since
\text{Atom}(S_{O})\subseteq S_{O}
, Lemma
3.1
(iii) gives

\text{Dd}(q\mid S_{O})\leq\text{Dd}(q\mid\text{Atom}(S_{O}))
for every

q\in\text{Cn}(\text{Atom}(S_{O}))\supseteq S_{O}

(the inclusion by Proposition
3.3
(iv)).
Restricting to
q\in S_{O}
yields
n_{\mathrm{op}}(q)\leq n_{\mathrm{int}}(q)
.
The bound
n_{\mathrm{int}}(q)\leq\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})
is immediate from the definition
of the maximum.

(iv) If
\text{Atom}(S_{O})=S_{O}
, then every
q\in S_{O}
satisfies
q\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})=T^{0}_{\mathsf{PS}}(\text{Atom}(S_{O}))
,
so
\text{Dd}(q\mid\text{Atom}(S_{O}))=0
and
\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})=0
.
Conversely, if
\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})=0
, then
\text{Dd}(q\mid\text{Atom}(S_{O}))=0
for all

q\in S_{O}
, which by Lemma
3.1
(ii) implies
q\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})
;
thus
S_{O}\subseteq\text{Atom}(S_{O})
, and since
\text{Atom}(S_{O})\subseteq S_{O}
by construction,

\text{Atom}(S_{O})=S_{O}
.
∎

Remark 3.11

(Interpretation and connection to logical depth)
.

The intrinsic depth
n_{\mathrm{int}}
captures inferential content relative to the irredundant core,
whereas the operational depth
n_{\mathrm{op}}
captures actual online cost under stored shortcuts.
Since
\text{Dd}(q\mid B)
is the depth of the shallowest proof tree from
B
,
it is closely related to Bennett’s logical depth
[
5
]
;
later sections connect intrinsic/operational derivation depth to conditional logical depth
under explicit simulation assumptions.

The irredundant core
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
is also related to the notion of a

prime implicate basis
or
irredundant base
in knowledge
compilation
[
10
]
and to the concept of a

minimal generating set
for a deductive closure in logic
programming
[
1
,
7
]
.
The key difference in our setting is that
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
is defined
relative to a fixed proof system
\mathsf{PS}
and a fixed canonical
order, yielding a unique and computable representative; moreover, the
derivation-depth stratification
\text{Dd}(\cdot\mid\text{Atom}(S_{O}))
provides
a
quantitative
measure of inferential complexity that goes
beyond the binary distinction between base and derived facts.

Remark 3.12

(Application to time-free knowledge bases)
.

In many knowledge-base applications (including the Datalog examples of
Section
V
), the time coordinate plays no active role:
all states reside at a single (implicit) time point, and the time domain

T_{O}
degenerates to a singleton.
In this case the object–time pair
(o,\tau)
reduces to the object
o

alone, the induced precedence
\prec_{S}
is trivial (no distinct time points
to compare), and the synonymy condition of
Definition
3.6
reduces to the existence of an

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable bijection between state sets.
All definitions and results of Sections
III
–
IV

remain valid in this degenerate setting; the time-indexed formulation is
retained for generality.

III-E

Noisy Information

Sections
III-B
–
III-D
developed the theory of
information models and their semantic invariants under the assumption that the model is

ideal
, i.e.,
S_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
.
In practice, a given information model need not be ideal: the carrier may realize states that
were not semantically intended, or fail to realize some intended states.
This subsection introduces a framework for analyzing such non-ideal models by constructing,
for each information model whose carrier admits a semantic representation, a noisy information
object whose semantic component is synonymous with the carrier space.
The semantic component is a set perturbation of the original
S_{O}
;
the perturbation pair
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
quantifies the gap between intended and
carrier-equivalent semantics, and the semantic invariants of the perturbed component serve as
computable proxies for the effective semantic structure of the original model as seen through
its carrier realization.

Assumption 3.6

(Common semantic universe for set operations)
.

The ambient set
\mathbb{S}_{O}\supseteq S_{O}
introduced in
Section
III-D
is assumed to satisfy the following additional
closure property: if
S\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
is an

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable state set in
\mathfrak{R}
and

S^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable state set over subdomains of the
object and time sorts with
S^{\prime}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S
witnessed by an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable
coding isomorphism graph (Definition
3.5
),
then
S^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
.
(Since elements of
\mathbb{S}_{O}
are object-sort/time-sort pairs, the closure condition
is relevant only when
S^{\prime}
is over the same sorts; for state sets over other sorts
the condition is vacuously satisfied.)

Moreover,
\mathbb{S}_{O}
is effectively representable: the injective encoding
\text{enc}_{O}
and
time-index map
\mathsf{time}_{O}
from Section
III-D
extend to all of

\mathbb{S}_{O}
, and membership in
\mathbb{S}_{O}
remains decidable from the codes.

Definition 3.15

(Noisy semantic base (set perturbation))
.

Let
S_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
be the intended semantic state set.
A
noisy semantic base
associated with
S_{O}
is any set of the form

\tilde{S}_{O}:=(S_{O}\setminus S_{O}^{-})\cup S_{O}^{+},

where
S_{O}^{-}\subseteq S_{O}
(lost states) and

S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}\setminus S_{O}
(spurious states).
We call
S_{O}^{-}
and
S_{O}^{+}
the
noise pair
and say the perturbation is

trivial
when
S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
, i.e.,
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
.

Assumption 3.7

(Carrier representability)
.

For the information model
\mathcal{I}
under consideration, there exists an

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable state set

S^{\prime}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
such that
S^{\prime}_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}

(Definition
3.6
).

Remark 3.13

(When carrier representability holds)
.

Assumption
3.7
requires a cross-sort

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable bijection between some

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable subset of
\mathbb{S}_{O}

and
S_{C}
; by Remark
3.7
, this is nontrivial in a many-sorted setting.
The assumption is naturally satisfied whenever the semantic signature

\Sigma_{\mathrm{sem}}
contains at least one relation or function symbol that links the
object sort and the carrier sort (directly or through intermediate sorts), and the ambient
structure
\mathfrak{R}
interprets this symbol so as to induce a bijection between the
time-slices of a suitable subset of
\mathbb{S}_{O}
and those of
S_{C}
.
In typical semantic communication scenarios, such a link is built into the model
(e.g., an encoding/decoding relation between messages and signals).
When
\mathcal{I}
is already ideal, the assumption holds with
S^{\prime}_{O}=S_{O}
and the
perturbation is trivial.

Definition 3.16

(Noisy information (carrier-equivalent representation))
.

Let
\mathcal{I}=\langle O,T_{O},S_{O},C,T_{C},S_{C},R_{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
be an information model
satisfying Assumption
3.7
, and let

\tilde{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
be an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable state set
with
\tilde{S}_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
,
witnessed by an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable coding isomorphism graph

G\subseteq\tilde{S}_{O}\times S_{C}
inducing a bijection

\tau_{OC}:\tilde{S}_{O}\to S_{C}
(with inverse
\tau_{CO}
).

The
noisy information
associated with
\mathcal{I}
via
(\tilde{S}_{O},G)
is the
tuple

\tilde{\mathcal{I}}\;=\;\bigl\langle\,\tilde{S}_{O},\;S_{C},\;G,\;\tau_{OC},\;\tau_{CO}\,\bigr\rangle,

together with the noise pair
(S_{O}^{-},\,S_{O}^{+})
defined by

S_{O}^{-}:=S_{O}\setminus\tilde{S}_{O}\qquad\text{(intended but unrealized)},

and

S_{O}^{+}:=\tilde{S}_{O}\setminus S_{O}\qquad\text{(unintended but realized)},

so that
\tilde{S}_{O}=(S_{O}\setminus S_{O}^{-})\cup S_{O}^{+}
is a noisy semantic base of
S_{O}

in the sense of Definition
3.15
.

Remark 3.14

(Connection to ideal information and computability of semantic invariants)
.

By construction,
\tilde{S}_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
and the singleton map

\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\tilde{s}_{o}):=\{\tau_{OC}(\tilde{s}_{o})\}
is a bijective enabling
assignment that agrees with the witnessing isomorphism, reproducing the algebraic
content of Definition
3.7
(synonymy plus singleton enabling map).
Whether the induced enabling relation additionally satisfies the precedence compatibility
clause
(E4)
of Axiom
3.3
depends on the interaction
between the witnessing graph
G
and the cross-domain precedence
\prec_{OC}
;
this may be imposed as an extra condition on the construction when the full information model
axiomatics are needed.

Independently of the full model structure, the semantic invariants

\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{D_{d}}

(Definitions
3.13
–
3.14
) depend only on the semantic
space and the proof system.
The hypotheses used in the proof of Theorem
3.1
hold
for
\tilde{S}_{O}
:

(a)

\tilde{S}_{O}
is finite, being in bijection with the finite set
S_{C}
;

(b)

\tilde{S}_{O}
is effectively listable under the canonical order inherited from

\text{enc}_{O}
, since
S_{C}
is finite and
\tau_{CO}
is computable
(induced by an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable graph in the finite
structure
\mathfrak{R}
);

(c)
the effective redundancy test holds, since

\tilde{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
is finite and
Axiom
3.4
allows deciding
s\in\text{Cn}(\Gamma)
by iterating

T_{\mathsf{PS}}
until stabilization

(Remark
3.9
(b))
.

We therefore define

\mathsf{A}(\tilde{\mathcal{I}}):=|\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O})|
and

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\tilde{\mathcal{I}}):=\max_{q\in\tilde{S}_{O}}\text{Dd}(q\mid\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O}))
;
by the same reasoning as in Theorem
3.1

(applied to
\tilde{S}_{O}
in place of
S_{O}
), both are well-defined, finite,
and computable.

Proposition 3.4

(Existence of carrier-equivalent noisy information)
.

Under Assumption
3.7
, for every information model

\mathcal{I}=\langle O,T_{O},S_{O},C,T_{C},S_{C},R_{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
,
there exists a noisy information
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}

(Definition
3.16
)
such that:

(i)

\tilde{S}_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
, i.e., the semantic component of
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}

is synonymous with the carrier space.

(ii)

\tilde{\mathcal{I}}
shares the carrier space
S_{C}
with
\mathcal{I}
.

(iii)

If
\mathcal{I}
is already ideal, the perturbation can be chosen to be trivial:

\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
,
S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
.

By Assumption
3.7
, there exists an

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable

S^{\prime}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
with
S^{\prime}_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
.
Set
\tilde{S}_{O}:=S^{\prime}_{O}
and let
G
be a witnessing

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable coding isomorphism graph.
Then
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})=(S_{O}\setminus S^{\prime}_{O},\;S^{\prime}_{O}\setminus S_{O})
, and

\tilde{S}_{O}=(S_{O}\setminus S_{O}^{-})\cup S_{O}^{+}=S^{\prime}_{O}
, confirming
(i) and (ii).
For (iii): if
\mathcal{I}
is ideal, then
S_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
by
Definition
3.7
; choosing
S^{\prime}_{O}:=S_{O}
yields

S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
.
∎

Remark 3.15

(Non-uniqueness and the role of the noise pair)
.

The noisy information
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}
depends on the choice of
\tilde{S}_{O}

(and its witnessing graph
G
); it is not in general unique.
Different choices yield different noise pairs, reflecting different ways of “reading”
the carrier semantically.
Regardless of the choice, the noise pair
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
provides a structured
decomposition of the discrepancy between the intended semantics
S_{O}
and the
carrier-equivalent semantics
\tilde{S}_{O}
:

S_{O}^{-}
collects semantic states that the carrier fails to realize, and

S_{O}^{+}
collects carrier-realized states absent from the original semantic intent.
The semantic invariants
\mathsf{A}(\tilde{\mathcal{I}})
and

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\tilde{\mathcal{I}})
then serve as computable measures of the

effective
semantic structure of
\mathcal{I}
as mediated by its carrier.

Definition 3.17

(Closure fidelity)
.

For finite sets
S,\hat{S}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
, the
closure fidelity
is
the Jaccard index of their deductive closures
[
22
]
:

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S,\hat{S})\;:=\;\frac{\lvert\text{Cn}(S)\cap\text{Cn}(\hat{S})\rvert}{\lvert\text{Cn}(S)\cup\text{Cn}(\hat{S})\rvert},

(3.3)

with
0/0:=1
.
We have
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}\in[0,1]
with

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S,\hat{S})=1
if and only if

\text{Cn}(S)=\text{Cn}(\hat{S})
.

Definition 3.18

(Atom-core preservation ratio)
.

For a knowledge base
S_{O}
with core
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and any

\hat{S}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
, define

\rho_{\text{Atom}}(S_{O},\hat{S})\;:=\;\frac{\lvert A\cap\hat{S}\rvert}{\lvert A\rvert},

(3.4)

with the convention
0/0:=1
when
A=\varnothing
.
This measures the fraction of irredundant core elements of
S_{O}
that are
present in
\hat{S}
.

Proposition 3.5

(Noise pair, core preservation, and closure fidelity)
.

Let
\tilde{S}_{O}=(S_{O}\setminus S_{O}^{-})\cup S_{O}^{+}
be a noisy semantic base of

S_{O}

(Definition
3.15
)
and let
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
.

(i)

Core preservation:

\rho_{\text{Atom}}(S_{O},\tilde{S}_{O})=1
if and only if

A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
, i.e., no core element is lost.

(ii)

Closure inclusion under full core preservation:

if
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
, then

\text{Cn}(S_{O})\subseteq\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
.

(iii)

Closure equality:

if
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
and

S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})

(
all spurious states are derivable from the original
)
,
then
\text{Cn}(S_{O})=\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
and

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O},\tilde{S}_{O})=1
.

(iv)

Trivial noise:

S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
implies

\rho_{\text{Atom}}=1
and
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}=1
.

(i)
A\cap\tilde{S}_{O}=A\cap\bigl[(S_{O}\setminus S_{O}^{-})\cup S_{O}^{+}\bigr]=(A\setminus S_{O}^{-})\cup(A\cap S_{O}^{+})
.
Since
A\subseteq S_{O}
and
S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}\setminus S_{O}
,

A\cap S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
.
Hence
A\cap\tilde{S}_{O}=A\setminus S_{O}^{-}
, so

\rho_{\text{Atom}}=|A\setminus S_{O}^{-}|/|A|=1
iff
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
.

(ii) If
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
, then
A\subseteq S_{O}\setminus S_{O}^{-}\subseteq\tilde{S}_{O}
.
By monotonicity (Cn2),

\text{Cn}(A)\subseteq\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
.
Since
\text{Cn}(A)=\text{Cn}(S_{O})

(Proposition
3.3
(i)),

\text{Cn}(S_{O})\subseteq\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
.

(iii) By (ii),
\text{Cn}(S_{O})\subseteq\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
.
For the reverse:

S_{O}\setminus S_{O}^{-}\subseteq S_{O}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
by
reflexivity (Cn1), and

S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
by hypothesis.
Hence
\tilde{S}_{O}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.
Monotonicity gives
\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})\subseteq\text{Cn}(\text{Cn}(S_{O}))=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
by
idempotence (Cn3).
Therefore
\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
and

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O},\tilde{S}_{O})=1
.

(iv) Immediate from
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
.
∎

IV
Semantic Channel

Section
III
established the
structural framework
of information
models—state spaces, enabling maps, deductive closure, irredundant cores, derivation
depth, and noisy perturbations—without invoking any probabilistic concepts.
The enabling map
\mathcal{E}
captures which carrier realizations are

admissible
for each semantic state, but assigns no likelihood to them; the noise
pair
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
measures set-theoretic discrepancy between intended and realized
semantics without modeling its stochastic origin.
Importantly, Section
III
does not
exclude
randomness: the state
spaces and enabling maps are defined in full generality as (possibly random) mathematical
objects.
What Section
III
does not provide is the
probabilistic apparatus

needed to reason about the likelihood of specific carrier realizations, the statistical
behavior of end-to-end transmission, or the fundamental limits of semantic
communication
[
8
,
29
]
.

This section furnishes precisely that apparatus.
The central construction is the
semantic channel
: a stochastic map—realized as
a composition of Markov kernels—that transforms semantic source states into
reconstructed semantic states through a carrier medium subject to noise.
The construction is governed by the following

design principle
:

Every Markov kernel in the semantic channel framework is an

enabling kernel
for some information model—the probabilistic refinement of
a set-valued enabling map whose support is constrained by the enabling structure of
that model.

Under this principle, the three stages of semantic communication—encoding,
carrier transmission, and decoding—are modeled as three composable information models
(Definition
3.4
), each equipped with an enabling kernel
(Definition
4.5
).
The end-to-end semantic channel kernel arises as the composition of these three
enabling kernels and is itself an enabling kernel for the composite information model
(Proposition
4.3
).
This ensures that the structural constraints established in
Section
III
—in particular, the enabling support inclusion and the
noise framework of Section
III-E
—are inherited by and preserved through
the probabilistic layer.

The section proceeds in five stages.
Section
IV-A
endows the finite state spaces of
Section
III
with probability distributions, introduces the Markov kernel
as the fundamental probabilistic building block, and defines the

enabling kernel
—the universal interface between structural enabling maps and
stochastic transitions.
Section
IV-B
applies this machinery to define the encoding kernel,
the carrier channel kernel, the decoding kernel, and their composition into an
end-to-end
semantic channel kernel
, each arising from a dedicated information
model.
Section
IV-C
introduces
semantic distortion
measures that
go beyond symbol-level error rates by exploiting the deductive closure and
derivation-depth structure inherited from Section
III
.
Section
IV-D
defines the principal invariants of a semantic
channel—semantic mutual information, semantic channel capacity, and structural
fidelity indices—and relates them to the classical Shannon capacity of the underlying
carrier channel.
Section
IV-E
formulates a semantic channel coding problem and states
preliminary achievability and converse bounds.

Throughout, we fix the information model

\mathcal{I}=\langle O,T_{O},S_{O},C,T_{C},S_{C},R_{\mathcal{E}}\rangle

from Section
III
and adopt all standing conventions and axioms
established there.

IV-A

Probabilistic Structure on State Spaces

Because all state spaces in our framework are finite
(Assumptions
3.1
and
3.4
), the required
measure-theoretic apparatus reduces to discrete probability distributions and
stochastic matrices.
This subsection introduces the key primitives: probability distributions, Markov
kernels, and—most importantly—the
enabling kernel
, which provides the
universal probabilistic interface to the structural enabling maps of
Section
III
.

Probabilistic notation convention.

Throughout Section
IV
, random elements drawn from a distribution
P

on a state space
S
are denoted by the corresponding sans-serif letter:
uppercase for random variables (e.g.,
\mathsf{S}_{o}
for a random semantic state
drawn from
P_{O}
) and lowercase for their realizations
(e.g.,
s_{o}\in S_{O}
).
Shannon entropy, conditional entropy, and mutual information are denoted

H(\cdot)
,
