H(\cdot\mid\cdot)
, and
I(\cdot\,;\cdot)
respectively, with
subscripts indicating the underlying distribution when
ambiguous
[
8
]
.
Expectations are denoted
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]
.
These standard information-theoretic quantities are instantiated on the semantic and
carrier state spaces; their interaction with the proof-system structure of
Section
III
is the subject of
Sections
IV-C
–
IV-D
.

Definition 4.1

(Probability distribution on a finite set)
.

Let
S
be a nonempty finite set.
A
probability distribution
(or
probability mass function
) on
S
is a
function
P:S\to[0,1]
satisfying

\sum_{s\in S}P(s)=1.

The
support
of
P
is
\supp(P):=\{s\in S:P(s)>0\}
.
We write
\Delta(S)
for the set of all probability distributions on
S

(the
probability simplex
over
S
), and set

\Delta(\varnothing):=\varnothing
by convention.

Definition 4.2

(Semantic source)
.

A
semantic source
for an information model
\mathcal{I}
is a pair

(\mathcal{I},\,P_{O})
where
P_{O}\in\Delta(S_{O})
.
We refer to
P_{O}
as the
source distribution
and, when
\mathcal{I}
is
clear from context, abbreviate the semantic source to
(S_{O},P_{O})
.

A semantic source is
full-support
if
\supp(P_{O})=S_{O}
, and
uniform
if

P_{O}(s_{o})=1/|S_{O}|
for every
s_{o}\in S_{O}
.

Remark 4.1

(A semantic source is not merely a random variable)
.

In Shannon’s framework
[
34
,
8
]
,
a discrete memoryless source is fully specified by a probability distribution on a
finite alphabet.
A semantic source carries additional structure: the underlying set
S_{O}
is endowed
with a deductive closure operator
Cn
, an irredundant core
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
, and a
derivation-depth stratification
Dd
(Section
III-D
).
These inferential structures are invisible to classical information-theoretic
measures (entropy, mutual information) but will enter the semantic distortion and
capacity definitions of Sections
IV-C
–
IV-D
.

Definition 4.3

(Markov kernel between finite state spaces)
.

Let
X
and
Y
be nonempty finite sets.
A
Markov kernel
(or
transition kernel
) from
X
to
Y
is a function

\kappa\;:\;X\times Y\;\longrightarrow\;[0,1]

such that for every
x\in X
,

\sum_{y\in Y}\kappa(y\mid x)=1,

i.e.,
\kappa(\cdot\mid x)\in\Delta(Y)
for each
x
.
We write
\kappa:X\rightsquigarrow Y
and, equivalently, view
\kappa
as the

|X|\times|Y|
row-stochastic matrix

[\kappa(y\mid x)]_{x\in X,\,y\in Y}
.
A kernel is
deterministic
if for every
x\in X
there exists a unique

y_{x}\in Y
with
\kappa(y_{x}\mid x)=1
; in this case
\kappa
is identified
with the function
f:X\to Y
given by
f(x):=y_{x}
.

Definition 4.4

(Kernel composition and identity kernel)
.

Let
\kappa_{1}:X\rightsquigarrow Y
and
\kappa_{2}:Y\rightsquigarrow Z
be Markov
kernels.
Their
composition

\kappa_{2}\circ\kappa_{1}:X\rightsquigarrow Z
is defined by

(\kappa_{2}\circ\kappa_{1})(z\mid x)\;:=\;\sum_{y\in Y}\kappa_{1}(y\mid x)\;\kappa_{2}(z\mid y),\qquad x\in X,\;z\in Z.

(4.1)

The
identity kernel
on
X
is the deterministic kernel

\mathrm{id}_{X}:X\rightsquigarrow X
with

\mathrm{id}_{X}(x^{\prime}\mid x):=\mathbf{1}[x^{\prime}=x]
.

Proposition 4.1

(Closure under composition)
.

The composition
\kappa_{2}\circ\kappa_{1}
is a Markov kernel from
X
to
Z
.
Kernel composition is associative, and the identity kernel is a two-sided identity:

\mathrm{id}_{Y}\circ\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}\circ\mathrm{id}_{Y}=\kappa_{2}
.

For each
x\in X
,

\sum_{z}(\kappa_{2}\circ\kappa_{1})(z\mid x)=\sum_{z}\sum_{y}\kappa_{1}(y\mid x)\,\kappa_{2}(z\mid y)=\sum_{y}\kappa_{1}(y\mid x)\bigl(\sum_{z}\kappa_{2}(z\mid y)\bigr)=\sum_{y}\kappa_{1}(y\mid x)=1
,
and each term is non-negative, so
\kappa_{2}\circ\kappa_{1}
is a Markov kernel.
Associativity and the identity laws follow from rearranging finite sums
(equivalently, from associativity of matrix multiplication and the property of the
identity matrix).
∎

Definition 4.5

(Enabling kernel for an information model)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an information model with semantic state set
S_{O}
,
carrier state set
S_{C}
, and enabling map

\mathcal{E}:S_{O}\Rightarrow S_{C}

(Axiom
3.3
).
An
enabling kernel
for
\mathcal{I}
is a Markov kernel

\kappa:S_{O}\rightsquigarrow S_{C}

satisfying the
enabling support constraint
: for every
s_{o}\in S_{O}
,

\supp\bigl(\kappa(\cdot\mid s_{o})\bigr)\;\subseteq\;\mathcal{E}(s_{o}).

(4.2)

Denote the set of all enabling kernels for
\mathcal{I}
by

\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I})
.

Proposition 4.2

(Every information model admits an enabling kernel)
.

For any information model
\mathcal{I}
satisfying
Axiom
3.3
,

\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I})\neq\varnothing
.
In particular, the computable enabling selector
e
of
Axiom
3.3
(E3)
induces a deterministic enabling kernel

\kappa_{e}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I})
via

\kappa_{e}(s_{c}\mid s_{o}):=\mathbf{1}[s_{c}=e(s_{o})]
.

By (E3),
e(s_{o})\in\mathcal{E}(s_{o})
for every
s_{o}\in S_{O}
.
Hence
\supp(\kappa_{e}(\cdot\mid s_{o}))=\{e(s_{o})\}\subseteq\mathcal{E}(s_{o})
,
and
\kappa_{e}
is a Markov kernel since

\sum_{s_{c}}\kappa_{e}(s_{c}\mid s_{o})=1
for each
s_{o}
.
∎

Proposition 4.3

(Enabling kernels compose under model composition)
.

Let
(\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2})
be a composable pair of information models

(Definition
3.4
)
.
If
\kappa_{1}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{1})
and

\kappa_{2}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{2})
, then

\kappa_{2}\circ\kappa_{1}\;\in\;\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{2}\circ\mathcal{I}_{1}).

By Proposition
4.1
,

\kappa_{2}\circ\kappa_{1}:S_{O}^{(1)}\rightsquigarrow S_{C}^{(2)}
is a Markov kernel.
It remains to verify the enabling support constraint.
Let
s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(1)}
and suppose

(\kappa_{2}\circ\kappa_{1})(s^{\prime}\mid s_{o})>0
for some
s^{\prime}\in S_{C}^{(2)}
.
By (
4.1
),

\sum_{s_{c}\in S_{C}^{(1)}}\kappa_{1}(s_{c}\mid s_{o})\,\kappa_{2}(s^{\prime}\mid s_{c})>0,

so there exists
s_{c}\in S_{C}^{(1)}
with
\kappa_{1}(s_{c}\mid s_{o})>0
and

\kappa_{2}(s^{\prime}\mid s_{c})>0
.
By the enabling support constraint of
\kappa_{1}
,

s_{c}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(s_{o})
;
by that of
\kappa_{2}
,
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(s_{c})
.
Hence

s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(s_{c})\subseteq\bigcup_{u\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(s_{o})}\mathcal{E}_{2}(u)=\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}(s_{o}),

confirming

\supp\bigl((\kappa_{2}\circ\kappa_{1})(\cdot\mid s_{o})\bigr)\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2\circ 1}(s_{o})
.
∎

Remark 4.2

(Design principle realized)
.

Proposition
4.3
is the probabilistic counterpart of
Proposition
3.1
: just as composite enabling maps
preserve the structural axioms of Section
III
, composite enabling kernels
preserve the enabling support constraint.
This guarantees that the end-to-end semantic channel kernel constructed in
Section
IV-B
is itself an enabling kernel for the composite
information model, fulfilling the design principle stated in the preamble of this
section.

IV-B

Semantic Channel: Definition via Markov Kernels

This subsection assembles the end-to-end
semantic channel
from three
information models—encoding, carrier transmission, and decoding—each equipped with
an enabling kernel.
The received carrier space
\hat{S}_{C}
and the reconstructed semantic space

\hat{S}_{O}
are
not
postulated independently; they arise as carrier state sets
of the channel and decoding information models, respectively.

Stage 1: Encoding

Definition 4.6

(Encoding kernel)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}=\langle O,T_{O},S_{O},C,T_{C},S_{C},R_{\mathcal{E}}\rangle
be the
information model fixed in Section
III
, with enabling map

\mathcal{E}:S_{O}\Rightarrow S_{C}
.
An
encoding kernel
is an enabling kernel for
\mathcal{I}

(Definition
4.5
)
, i.e., a Markov kernel

\kappa_{\text{enc}}:S_{O}\rightsquigarrow S_{C}
satisfying

\kappa_{\text{enc}}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I})
.

Remark 4.3

(Deterministic encoding as a degenerate kernel)
.

The computable enabling selector
e
of
Axiom
3.3
(E3)
induces a deterministic encoding kernel

\kappa_{e}(s_{c}\mid s_{o}):=\mathbf{1}[s_{c}=e(s_{o})]
,
which belongs to
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I})
by
Proposition
4.2
.
Thus every information model admits at least one encoding kernel.
Conversely, any
\kappa_{\text{enc}}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I})

can be interpreted as a
randomized selection
from the set of admissible carrier
realizations
\mathcal{E}(s_{o})
; randomized encoding may strictly outperform
deterministic encoding in the presence of channel noise, paralleling the classical
situation in Shannon theory
[
8
]
.

Proposition 4.4

(Induced joint and marginal distributions)
.

Let
(S_{O},P_{O})
be a semantic source and

\kappa_{\text{enc}}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I})
an encoding kernel.
Define:

(i)

the
joint distribution
on
S_{O}\times S_{C}
by

P_{OC}(s_{o},s_{c}):=P_{O}(s_{o})\,\kappa_{\text{enc}}(s_{c}\mid s_{o})
;

(ii)

the
induced carrier distribution
by

P_{C}(s_{c}):=\sum_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}P_{O}(s_{o})\,\kappa_{\text{enc}}(s_{c}\mid s_{o})
.

Then
P_{OC}\in\Delta(S_{O}\times S_{C})
,
P_{C}\in\Delta(S_{C})
,

P_{C}
is the
S_{C}
-marginal of
P_{OC}
, and

\supp(P_{OC})\;\subseteq\;\bigl\{(s_{o},s_{c})\in S_{O}\times S_{C}:s_{c}\in\mathcal{E}(s_{o})\bigr\}.

(4.3)

Moreover, if
P_{O}
is full-support and
\kappa_{\text{enc}}
is deterministic
with selector
e
, then
\supp(P_{C})=e(S_{O})
.

Non-negativity is immediate.
Normalization:

\sum_{s_{o},s_{c}}P_{OC}(s_{o},s_{c})=\sum_{s_{o}}P_{O}(s_{o})\sum_{s_{c}}\kappa_{\text{enc}}(s_{c}\mid s_{o})=\sum_{s_{o}}P_{O}(s_{o})=1
.
Hence
P_{OC}\in\Delta(S_{O}\times S_{C})
and
P_{C}
is its
S_{C}
-marginal, so

P_{C}\in\Delta(S_{C})
.
The support inclusion (
4.3
) follows from the enabling support
constraint (
4.2
):
if
P_{OC}(s_{o},s_{c})>0
, then
P_{O}(s_{o})>0
and

\kappa_{\text{enc}}(s_{c}\mid s_{o})>0
, hence

s_{c}\in\supp(\kappa_{\text{enc}}(\cdot\mid s_{o}))\subseteq\mathcal{E}(s_{o})
.
The final claim follows because under deterministic encoding,

P_{C}(s_{c})=\sum_{s_{o}:e(s_{o})=s_{c}}P_{O}(s_{o})
, which is positive if and only if

s_{c}\in e(S_{O})
(since
P_{O}
is full-support).
∎

Remark 4.4

(Pushforward under synonymy)
.

If
S_{1}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{2}
via
\tau_{12}

(Definition
3.6
) and
P_{1}\in\Delta(S_{1})
, the

pushforward distribution

(\tau_{12})_{\#}P_{1}\in\Delta(S_{2})
is defined by

\bigl[(\tau_{12})_{\#}P_{1}\bigr](s_{2}):=P_{1}\bigl(\tau_{12}^{-1}(s_{2})\bigr)

for
s_{2}\in S_{2}
.
Since
\tau_{12}
is a bijection that preserves and reflects the induced time
precedence, the pushforward preserves any temporal correlation structure of the
source.
When
\mathcal{I}
is ideal and
S_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
via
\tau_{OC}

(Definition
3.7
), the deterministic encoding kernel

\kappa_{e}
with
e=\tau_{OC}
yields

P_{C}=(\tau_{OC})_{\#}P_{O}
.

Stage 2: Carrier Channel

Definition 4.7

(Carrier channel information model)
.

A
carrier channel information model
(composable with
\mathcal{I}
) is an
information model

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}\;=\;\bigl\langle\,C,\;T_{C},\;S_{C},\;\hat{C},\;T_{\hat{C}},\;\hat{S}_{C},\;R_{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{ch}}\,\bigr\rangle,

satisfying Axiom
3.3
, whose semantic state set is

S_{C}
(the carrier state set of
\mathcal{I}
) and whose carrier state set is a
nonempty finite set
\hat{S}_{C}
called the
received carrier state space
,
equipped with an injective encoding

\text{enc}_{\hat{C}}:\hat{S}_{C}\to\{0,1\}^{*}
.

The induced enabling map

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ch}}:S_{C}\Rightarrow\hat{S}_{C}
specifies, for each transmitted
carrier state, the set of physically possible received states.
The
carrier channel kernel
is any enabling kernel for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}
:

W\;\in\;\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}),\qquad W:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}.

Remark 4.5

(Common special cases of the carrier channel)
.

(i)
In many applications the physical channel output alphabet coincides with
its input alphabet; setting
\hat{S}_{C}:=S_{C}
and

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ch}}(s_{c}):=S_{C}
for all
s_{c}
recovers the standard
unconstrained discrete memoryless channel (DMC) of
Shannon theory
[
34
]
.

(ii)
When the carrier channel is
noiseless
,

\hat{S}_{C}=S_{C}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ch}}(s_{c})=\{s_{c}\}
for every

s_{c}\in S_{C}
; the unique enabling kernel is the identity kernel

W=\mathrm{id}_{S_{C}}
.

(iii)
In the terminology of Section
III
, the roles of
“semantic space” and “carrier space” are relative to each information model.
In
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}
, the transmitted carrier states
S_{C}
play the
structural role of
S_{O}
, and the received carrier states
\hat{S}_{C}
play the
role of
S_{C}
.
This relabeling is purely notational; it reflects the fact that each link in a
communication chain is itself an instance of the general information model framework.

Stage 3: Decoding

Definition 4.8

(Decoding information model)
.

A
decoding information model
(composable with
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}
) is
an information model

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}\;=\;\bigl\langle\,\hat{C},\;T_{\hat{C}},\;\hat{S}_{C},\;\hat{O},\;T_{\hat{O}},\;\hat{S}_{O},\;R_{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{dec}}\,\bigr\rangle,

satisfying Axiom
3.3
, whose semantic state set is

\hat{S}_{C}
(the carrier state set of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}
) and whose carrier
state set

\hat{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
is called the

reconstructed semantic state space
, inheriting the encoding
\text{enc}_{O}
, the
time-index map
\mathsf{time}_{O}
, and the proof system

(\mathsf{PS},\,T_{\mathsf{PS}},\,\text{Cn})
from
Sections
III-A
–
III-D
.

The induced enabling map

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}:\hat{S}_{C}\Rightarrow\hat{S}_{O}
specifies, for each received
carrier state, the set of admissible decoded semantic states.
The
decoding kernel
is any enabling kernel for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}
:

D\;\in\;\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}),\qquad D:\hat{S}_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{O}.

Remark 4.6

(Inherited proof-system structure and heterogeneous receivers)
.

(i)
Since
\hat{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
and the proof system

\mathsf{PS}
acts on all of
\mathbb{S}_{O}

(Section
III-D
), the deductive closure

\text{Cn}(\hat{S}_{O})
, the irredundant core
\text{Atom}(\hat{S}_{O})
, and the derivation
depth
\text{Dd}(q\mid\text{Atom}(\hat{S}_{O}))
are all well-defined for
\hat{S}_{O}
.
In particular, the semantic invariants
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{D_{d}}

(Definitions
3.13
–
3.14
) are defined for

\hat{S}_{O}
by the same constructions as for
S_{O}
.
This structural inheritance is what distinguishes the semantic channel framework from a
purely symbol-level treatment.

(ii)
When the receiver seeks to reconstruct states in the original semantic
vocabulary, one takes
\hat{S}_{O}:=S_{O}
.
When the receiver operates with a coarser or finer semantic vocabulary
than the sender,
\hat{S}_{O}
may differ from
S_{O}
;
this generality is relevant to heterogeneous agent scenarios
(e.g., in multi-agent systems where different agents maintain different knowledge
bases
[
19
,
12
]
).
More generally, the same sender communicating with different receivers gives rise
to different information models (sharing the same
S_{O}
but differing in
S_{C}
,

\hat{S}_{C}
,
\hat{S}_{O}
, and in the associated enabling maps and noise structures).

End-to-End Composition

The three information models

\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}

form a composable chain:

S_{C}(\mathcal{I})=S_{O}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}})=S_{C}
and

S_{C}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}})=S_{O}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}})=\hat{S}_{C}
.
The composite information model
(Definition
3.4
, Remark
3.5
)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sem}}\;:=\;\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}\circ\mathcal{I}

(4.4)

has semantic state set
S_{O}
, carrier state set
\hat{S}_{O}
, and composite enabling
map

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o})\;=\;\bigcup_{s_{c}\,\in\,\mathcal{E}(s_{o})}\;\bigcup_{\hat{s}_{c}\,\in\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ch}}(s_{c})}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}(\hat{s}_{c}),\;s_{o}\in S_{O}.

(4.5)

End-to-End Noise Structure

The composite information model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sem}}
has semantic
state set
S_{O}
and carrier state set

\hat{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}

(Definition
4.8
).
Since both
S_{O}
and
\hat{S}_{O}
reside in the common semantic universe

\mathbb{S}_{O}
, the noisy information framework of
Section
III-E
applies directly to the end-to-end model,
yielding a structured decomposition of the semantic gap between sender and
receiver.

Proposition 4.5

(End-to-end carrier representability and noisy semantic base)
.

The composite information model

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sem}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}\circ\mathcal{I}

satisfies Assumption
3.7
.
Consequently, setting
\tilde{S}_{O}:=\hat{S}_{O}
, the reconstructed
semantic space is a noisy semantic base of
S_{O}

(Definition
3.15
)
with
end-to-end noise pair

S_{O}^{-}:=S_{O}\setminus\tilde{S}_{O}\qquad\textup{(intended but unreconstructable)},

(4.6)

S_{O}^{+}:=\tilde{S}_{O}\setminus S_{O}\qquad\textup{(unintended but reachable)},

(4.7)

so that

\tilde{S}_{O}\;=\;(S_{O}\setminus S_{O}^{-})\cup S_{O}^{+}.

(4.8)

Moreover, the noisy information

\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{sem}}

(Definition
3.16
)
associated with

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sem}}
is well-defined, and the semantic
invariants

\mathsf{A}(\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{sem}})=\lvert\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O})\rvert
and

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{sem}})=\max_{q\in\tilde{S}_{O}}\text{Dd}\bigl(q\mid\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O})\bigr)

are finite and computable

(Remark
3.14
)
.

In the composite model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sem}}
, the carrier state set
is
\hat{S}_{O}
.
By Definition
4.8
,

\hat{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
and
\hat{S}_{O}
is

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable
(Axiom
3.1
applied to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}
).
Setting
S^{\prime}_{O}:=\hat{S}_{O}
, the reflexivity of
\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}

(Proposition
3.2
) gives

S^{\prime}_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}\hat{S}_{O}
, witnessed by the identity graph

G_{\mathrm{id}}(s,s^{\prime}):=\mathbf{1}[s=s^{\prime}]
.
Hence Assumption
3.7
is satisfied with

\tilde{S}_{O}=\hat{S}_{O}
.
The noise pair follows from Definition
3.16
, and the
computability statement from Remark
3.14

(whose hypotheses are verified by the same argument given there, with

\tilde{S}_{O}
in place of the general carrier-equivalent state set).
∎

Corollary 4.1

(End-to-end core preservation and closure fidelity)
.

Let
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and let
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
be the end-to-end
noise pair of Proposition
4.5
.
Then all four conclusions of
Proposition
3.5
hold with

\tilde{S}_{O}=\hat{S}_{O}
:

(i)

\rho_{\text{Atom}}(S_{O},\tilde{S}_{O})=1
if and only if

A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing

(
no core element is lost
)
.

(ii)

If
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
, then

\text{Cn}(S_{O})\subseteq\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
.

(iii)

If
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
and

S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
, then

\text{Cn}(S_{O})=\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
and

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O},\tilde{S}_{O})=1
.

(iv)

S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing

(
i.e.,

\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
)
implies

\rho_{\text{Atom}}=1
and
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}=1
.

Immediate from Propositions
4.5

and
3.5
, since
\tilde{S}_{O}
is a noisy
semantic base of
S_{O}
with noise pair
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
.
∎

Remark 4.7

(Stage-wise noise decomposition)
.

The noisy information framework of Section
III-E
applies
not only to the composite model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sem}}
but also
to each constituent model individually, provided
Assumption
3.7
holds at that stage.
In particular:
(a) the encoding model
\mathcal{I}
has semantic space
S_{O}
and
carrier space
S_{C}
; if Assumption
3.7
holds
for
\mathcal{I}
, there exists a noisy semantic base

\tilde{S}_{O}^{(\mathrm{enc})}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
with

\tilde{S}_{O}^{(\mathrm{enc})}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
, yielding an encoding noise pair

(S_{O}^{-,\mathrm{enc}},\,S_{O}^{+,\mathrm{enc}})
;
(b) the carrier channel model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}
has its own
noise pair capturing physical-layer distortion;
(c) the decoding model
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}
has a noise pair
capturing decoder-induced mismatch.
The end-to-end noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
subsumes the combined effect of all three stages.
This stage-wise view can guide the identification of the dominant noise
source; a detailed treatment of the composition of stage-wise noise
pairs is deferred to future work.

Remark 4.8

(Notation convention:
\tilde{S}_{O}
and
\hat{S}_{O}
)
.

By Proposition
4.5
,
\tilde{S}_{O}=\hat{S}_{O}

as subsets of
\mathbb{S}_{O}
.
We adopt the following convention for the remainder of this section:

\hat{S}_{O}
is used when the reconstructed space is viewed as the

output alphabet
of the end-to-end channel (the coding-theoretic
perspective), while
\tilde{S}_{O}
is used when it is viewed as a

noisy semantic base
of
S_{O}
with noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
(the noise-structure perspective).
The semantic invariants
\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O})
,
\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
, and

\text{Dd}(\cdot\mid\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O}))
are always well-defined since

\tilde{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
.

Probabilistic Characterization of the End-to-End Noise Pair

The noise pair
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
is a
set-level

(deterministic) decomposition of the gap between
S_{O}

and
\tilde{S}_{O}
.
The semantic channel kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}:S_{O}\rightsquigarrow\tilde{S}_{O}
endows this
decomposition with
probabilistic
content by specifying, for each
sent state
s_{o}
, the likelihood that the reconstruction lands in
each region of
\tilde{S}_{O}
.

Definition 4.9

(Noise-region partition and per-input probabilities)
.

The noise pair
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
of
Proposition
4.5
induces a partition of

\tilde{S}_{O}
into a
preserved region
and a

spurious region
:

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}

\displaystyle\;:=\;S_{O}\cap\tilde{S}_{O}\;=\;S_{O}\setminus S_{O}^{-}

\displaystyle\text{(preserved)},

(4.9)

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{O}^{+}

\displaystyle\;:=\;S_{O}^{+}\;=\;\tilde{S}_{O}\setminus S_{O}

\displaystyle\text{(spurious)}.

(4.10)

The two regions are disjoint:

\tilde{S}_{O}=\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}\;\dot{\cup}\;\tilde{S}_{O}^{+}
.
For each
s_{o}\in S_{O}
, define the
preservation probability
and
the
spurious probability
:

\displaystyle p_{\cap}(s_{o})

\displaystyle\;:=\;\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o}),

(4.11)

\displaystyle p_{+}(s_{o})

\displaystyle\;:=\;\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}^{+}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o}),

(4.12)

with
p_{\cap}(s_{o})+p_{+}(s_{o})=1
.
For an element
a\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})\cap\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}
, the

per-core-element self-preservation probability
is

\pi(a)\;:=\;\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(a\mid a).

(4.13)

Proposition 4.6

(Noise-region decomposition of channel behavior)
.

Let
(S_{O},P_{O})
be a semantic source and let

\mathfrak{C}
be a semantic channel with end-to-end noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
and kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}:S_{O}\rightsquigarrow\tilde{S}_{O}
.
Let
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
.

(i)

Distortion decomposition:

The expected Hamming distortion decomposes as

\displaystyle\bar{d}_{H}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})\;=\;

\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}P_{O}(s_{o})\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}\\
\hat{s}_{o}\neq s_{o}\end{subarray}}\!\!\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})}_{\displaystyle\bar{d}_{H}^{\cap}\;\text{(within-vocabulary confusion)}}

\displaystyle\;+\;

\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}P_{O}(s_{o})\,p_{+}(s_{o})}_{\displaystyle\bar{d}_{H}^{+}\;\text{(spurious substitution)}}.

(4.14)

(ii)

Necessary condition for perfect symbol fidelity:

\bar{d}_{H}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})=0
for a full-support
P_{O}

requires
S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing

(
i.e.,
S_{O}\subseteq\tilde{S}_{O}
)
and

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}\mid s_{o})=1
for every

s_{o}\in S_{O}
.

(iii)

Probabilistic core preservation:

If
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
, then
\pi(a)
is
well-defined for every
a\in A
and

\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})\;:=\;\frac{\sum_{a\in A}P_{O}(a)\;\pi(a)}{P_{O}(A)}

(4.15)

measures the average self-preservation probability among core
elements

(
where
P_{O}(A):=\sum_{a\in A}P_{O}(a)
)
.

(iv)

Sufficient condition for closure preservation
with high probability:

If
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
and

S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
, then by
Corollary
4.1
(iii)
,

\text{Cn}(S_{O})=\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
.
Moreover, substituting any redundant state

s_{o}\in S_{O}\setminus A
by any

\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}
preserves the deductive
closure:

\text{Cn}\bigl((S_{O}\setminus\{s_{o}\})\cup\{\hat{s}_{o}\}\bigr)=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.

(
This closure-preservation property is
formalized as zero closure distortion in
Section
IV-C
;
see Definition
4.14

and
Remark
4.13
(a)
.
)

(i) Since
\tilde{S}_{O}=\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}\;\dot{\cup}\;\tilde{S}_{O}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\bar{d}_{H}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{o}}P_{O}(s_{o})\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\neq s_{o}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{o}}P_{O}(s_{o})\Bigl[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}\\
\hat{s}_{o}\neq s_{o}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})

\displaystyle+\;

\displaystyle\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}^{+}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})\Bigr].

For
s_{o}\in S_{O}
and
\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}^{+}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}\setminus S_{O}
, one has
\hat{s}_{o}\neq s_{o}
, so the second
sum equals
p_{+}(s_{o})
.

(ii) Since
d_{H}\geq 0
and
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})\geq 0
,

\bar{d}_{H}=0
under full-support
P_{O}
requires

d_{H}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})=0
whenever

P_{O}(s_{o})>0
and
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})>0
.
Hence
\hat{s}_{o}=s_{o}
for every reachable pair, i.e.,

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}\mid s_{o})=1
for all
s_{o}\in S_{O}
.
This requires
s_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}
for every
s_{o}
, i.e.,

S_{O}\subseteq\tilde{S}_{O}
, hence
S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
.

(iii) If
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
, then
A\subseteq\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}
,
so
a\in\tilde{S}_{O}
and
\pi(a)=\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(a\mid a)
is
well-defined for every
a\in A
.
The formula (
4.15
) is then an average of
values in
[0,1]
.

(iv) The first claim is Corollary
4.1
(iii).
For the second: if
s_{o}\in S_{O}\setminus A
, then

s_{o}\in\text{Cn}(S_{O}\setminus\{s_{o}\})
(definition of redundancy), so

\text{Cn}(S_{O}\setminus\{s_{o}\})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.
For any
\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}\subseteq\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
,
monotonicity and idempotence of
Cn
give

\text{Cn}\bigl((S_{O}\setminus\{s_{o}\})\cup\{\hat{s}_{o}\}\bigr)=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.
∎

Remark 4.9

(Interpretation: when does the noise pair matter?)
.

When
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
(i.e.,
S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
), the
noise pair is trivial, both noise regions collapse
(
\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}=S_{O}
,
\tilde{S}_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
),
and all semantic distortion information resides in the randomness of

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}:S_{O}\rightsquigarrow S_{O}
.
The noise pair structure of Section
III-E
becomes trivial
in this case, and the analysis reduces to the standard setting.

Conversely, when
\tilde{S}_{O}\neq S_{O}
—as in heterogeneous-agent
scenarios where sender and receiver maintain different vocabularies

(Remark
4.6
(ii))
—the noise
pair provides essential structural information beyond what the kernel
alone reveals:

S_{O}^{-}
identifies semantic states that the receiver’s vocabulary

cannot
represent (vocabulary loss), and

S_{O}^{+}
identifies states that the receiver can produce but the
sender did not intend (vocabulary surplus / hallucination).
The decomposition (
(i)
) separates the Hamming
distortion into confusion within the shared vocabulary and errors due
to vocabulary mismatch, providing design guidance for encoder/decoder
optimization.

Definition 4.10

(Semantic channel)
.

A
semantic channel
is a tuple

\mathfrak{C}\;=\;\bigl(\,\mathcal{I},\;\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}},\;\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}},\;\kappa_{\text{enc}},\;W,\;D\,\bigr),

where
\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}
,

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}
are as above,

\kappa_{\text{enc}}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I})
,

W\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}})
, and

D\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}})
.

The
semantic channel kernel
(or
end-to-end kernel
) is the composite
Markov kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}\;:=\;D\circ W\circ\kappa_{\text{enc}}\;:\;S_{O}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{O}.

(4.16)

Explicitly, for
s_{o}\in S_{O}
and
\hat{s}_{o}\in\hat{S}_{O}
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{c}\in S_{C}}\;\sum_{\hat{s}_{c}\in\hat{S}_{C}}\kappa_{\text{enc}}(s_{c}\mid s_{o})\;W(\hat{s}_{c}\mid s_{c})\;D(\hat{s}_{o}\mid\hat{s}_{c}).

(4.17)

Proposition 4.7

(Properties of the semantic channel kernel)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semantic channel with kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}=D\circ W\circ\kappa_{\text{enc}}
.

(i)

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}:S_{O}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{O}
is a Markov kernel.

(ii)

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sem}})
, where

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sem}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}\circ\mathcal{I}

is the composite information model (
4.4
).
In particular, for every
s_{o}\in S_{O}
,

\supp\bigl(\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\cdot\mid s_{o})\bigr)\;\subseteq\;\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}).

(iii)

Dependence on components:

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
depends on the triple

(\kappa_{\text{enc}},W,D)
.
Fixing
W
and varying
(\kappa_{\text{enc}},D)
recovers the classical
coding-theoretic setup: the encoder and decoder are design variables, while the
physical channel is given.

Part (i) follows from two applications of
Proposition
4.1
.
Part (ii) follows from two applications of
Proposition
4.3
:
first,
W\circ\kappa_{\text{enc}}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}\circ\mathcal{I})
;
then
D\circ(W\circ\kappa_{\text{enc}})\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}\circ\mathcal{I})=\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sem}})
.
Part (iii) is immediate from the definition.
∎

Remark 4.10

(End-to-end induced distributions)
.

Given a semantic source
(S_{O},P_{O})
and a semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}
, the
joint distribution over the full state chain

(s_{o},s_{c},\hat{s}_{c},\hat{s}_{o})\in S_{O}\times S_{C}\times\hat{S}_{C}\times\hat{S}_{O}
factors as

\displaystyle P(s_{o},s_{c},\hat{s}_{c},\hat{s}_{o})

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle P_{O}(s_{o})\;\kappa_{\text{enc}}(s_{c}\mid s_{o})\;W(\hat{s}_{c}\mid s_{c})\;D(\hat{s}_{o}\mid\hat{s}_{c}),

forming a Markov chain
S_{O}\to S_{C}\to\hat{S}_{C}\to\hat{S}_{O}
.
The end-to-end marginal satisfies

P(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})=\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})
.
By Proposition
4.7
(ii)
, the support of this
joint distribution respects the enabling structure at every stage.

Definition 4.11

(Ideal semantic channel)
.

A semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}
is
ideal
if all three constituent information
models are ideal
(Definition
3.7
):

S_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}S_{C}
,
S_{C}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}\hat{S}_{C}
, and
\hat{S}_{C}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}\tilde{S}_{O}
;
and the enabling kernels are the deterministic kernels induced by the respective
synonymy witnesses:

\kappa_{\text{enc}}(s_{c}\mid s_{o})=\mathbf{1}[s_{c}=\tau_{OC}(s_{o})],

W(\hat{s}_{c}\mid s_{c})=\mathbf{1}[\hat{s}_{c}=\tau_{C\hat{C}}(s_{c})],

D(\hat{s}_{o}\mid\hat{s}_{c})=\mathbf{1}[\hat{s}_{o}=\tau_{\hat{C}\tilde{O}}(\hat{s}_{c})].

In this case, the end-to-end kernel
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
is a deterministic
bijection from
S_{O}
to
\tilde{S}_{O}
and
S_{O}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}\tilde{S}_{O}

(by transitivity of
\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}
, Proposition
3.2
).

Remark 4.11

(Ideal channel, trivial noise, and noise pair characterization)
.

For an ideal semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}

(Definition
4.11
)
,
the end-to-end kernel
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
is deterministic and equals the
bijection

\tau_{\mathrm{e2e}}:=\tau_{\hat{C}\tilde{O}}\circ\tau_{C\hat{C}}\circ\tau_{OC}:S_{O}\to\tilde{S}_{O}
,
which preserves and reflects the induced time precedence
(by transitivity of
\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}}
).

When
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
, the end-to-end noise pair is trivial:

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})=(\varnothing,\varnothing)

(Proposition
4.5
)
.
The composed bijection
\tau_{\mathrm{e2e}}
is a
time-order automorphism of
S_{O}
;
it reduces to the identity kernel if and only if

\tau_{\mathrm{e2e}}=\mathrm{id}_{S_{O}}
.
In this case,
p_{\cap}(s_{o})=1
and
p_{+}(s_{o})=0
for every

s_{o}\in S_{O}
(Definition
4.9
), and every
per-core-element self-preservation probability satisfies

\pi(a)=1
for all
a\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})
.
By Corollary
4.1
(iv),

\rho_{\text{Atom}}(S_{O},\tilde{S}_{O})=1
and

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O},\tilde{S}_{O})=1
.

For a non-ideal channel, the noise pair
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
provides
a structured decomposition of the discrepancy between intended and
reconstructed semantics, and the noise-region
probabilities (
4.11
)–(
4.12
) quantify
the stochastic behavior of this discrepancy.
The semantic invariants of the noisy semantic base
\tilde{S}_{O}

(
\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O})
,
\mathsf{D_{d}}(\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{sem}})
)
serve as computable proxies for the effective semantic structure of the
channel—a connection made precise in the distortion analysis of
Section
IV-C
and the structural indices of
Section
IV-D
.

IV-C

Semantic Distortion and Fidelity

Classical channel coding measures distortion at the
symbol level
: a
transmitted symbol is either reproduced correctly or it is not.
The semantic channel framework inherits richer structure—deductive closure,
irredundant cores, and derivation depth—from Section
III
, enabling
distortion measures that capture whether the
inferential content
and

structural complexity
of a semantic state are preserved, even when the
literal symbol is altered.

This subsection introduces four per-pair distortion functions of increasing
semantic depth, a parameterized composite distortion, and two set-level fidelity
measures that connect to the noise pair of
Section
III-E
.

Standing data.

Throughout this subsection,
\mathfrak{C}
is a semantic channel
(Definition
4.10
) with source space
S_{O}
, reconstructed
space
\hat{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
, and end-to-end kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}:S_{O}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{O}
.
The irredundant core
A:=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and the proof system

(\mathsf{PS},\,T_{\mathsf{PS}},\,\text{Cn})
are as fixed in
Sections
III-A
–
III-D
.

Definition 4.12

(Distortion function)
.

A
distortion function
(relative to
S_{O}
and
\hat{S}_{O}
) is any function

d\;:\;S_{O}\times\hat{S}_{O}\;\longrightarrow\;[0,\infty)

satisfying
d(s,s)=0
for every
s\in S_{O}\cap\hat{S}_{O}
.
A distortion function is
bounded
if

\sup_{s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}}d(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})<\infty
, and
normalized
if its range
is contained in
[0,1]
.

Definition 4.13

(Symbol-level (Hamming) distortion)
.

Assume
\hat{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
so that equality of states is
well-defined.
The
Hamming distortion
is

d_{H}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})\;:=\;\mathbf{1}[s_{o}\neq\hat{s}_{o}],\qquad s_{o}\in S_{O},\;\hat{s}_{o}\in\hat{S}_{O}.

This is a normalized distortion function in the sense of
Definition
4.12
.

Definition 4.14

(Closure distortion (context-dependent))
.

Let
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
be a finite
reference base

(typically
\Gamma=S_{O}
or
\Gamma=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
).
For
s_{o}\in S_{O}
and
\hat{s}_{o}\in\hat{S}_{O}
, write

\Gamma_{-s_{o}}:=\Gamma\setminus\{s_{o}\},

and define the two
substitution closures

C_{s}:=\text{Cn}(\Gamma_{-s_{o}}\cup\{s_{o}\}),\qquad C_{\hat{s}}:=\text{Cn}(\Gamma_{-s_{o}}\cup\{\hat{s}_{o}\}).

The
closure distortion
relative to
\Gamma
is the Jaccard distance

d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid\Gamma)\;:=\;1-\frac{\lvert C_{s}\cap C_{\hat{s}}\rvert}{\lvert C_{s}\cup C_{\hat{s}}\rvert},

(4.18)

with the convention
0/0:=0
(both closures empty).
When
\Gamma=S_{O}
, we abbreviate
d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}):=d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})
.
When
s_{o}\notin\Gamma
, one has
\Gamma_{-s_{o}}=\Gamma
, so the operation becomes

addition
of
s_{o}
(resp.
\hat{s}_{o}
) to
\Gamma
rather than substitution;
the definition remains well-formed in this case.

Remark 4.12

(Conventions for degenerate Jaccard ratios)
.

The closure distortion
d_{\text{Cn}}
and the closure
fidelity
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}

(Definition
3.17
)
both use
Jaccard-type ratios but adopt dual conventions for the degenerate
case of two empty sets:

d_{\text{Cn}}
is a
distance
, so identical (empty) sets
have distance
0
(
0/0:=0
);

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}
is a
similarity
, so identical
(empty) closures have similarity
1
(
0/0:=1
).
The two conventions are consistent:

d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid\Gamma)=0
if and only if

C_{s}=C_{\hat{s}}
, and

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S,\hat{S})=1
if and only if

\text{Cn}(S)=\text{Cn}(\hat{S})
;
the degenerate case is assigned the appropriate extremal value
on each scale.

Remark 4.13

(Semantic content of the closure distortion)
.

The closure distortion measures the fraction of deductive consequences that change
when
s_{o}
is replaced by
\hat{s}_{o}
in the reference base.
Two key properties follow directly from the
Cn
-axiomatics

(Assumption
3.3
)
:

(a)
If
s_{o}\in\text{Cn}(\Gamma_{-s_{o}})
(i.e.,
s_{o}
is a stored shortcut
relative to
\Gamma
), then
C_{s}=\text{Cn}(\Gamma_{-s_{o}})
; hence any

\hat{s}_{o}\in\text{Cn}(\Gamma_{-s_{o}})
yields
C_{\hat{s}}=C_{s}
and

d_{\text{Cn}}=0
.
Errors on redundant states incur zero closure distortion—a natural semantic
property.

(b)
If
s_{o}\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and
\Gamma=S_{O}
, then

s_{o}\notin\text{Cn}(\Gamma_{-s_{o}})
, so the replacement genuinely matters and

d_{\text{Cn}}
is typically positive unless
\hat{s}_{o}
is deductively equivalent to

s_{o}
over
\Gamma_{-s_{o}}
.

Definition 4.15

(Derivation-depth distortion)
.

Let
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and
d_{\max}:=\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})

(Definition
3.14
)
.
For
s_{o}\in S_{O}
and
\hat{s}_{o}\in\hat{S}_{O}
, define

\displaystyle d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}):=\begin{cases}\min\!\Bigl(\frac{\lvert\text{Dd}(s_{o}\mid A)-\text{Dd}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid A)\rvert}{\max(d_{\max},\,1)},\;1\Bigr)\\
\qquad\text{if }\hat{s}_{o}\in\text{Cn}(A),\\[4.0pt]
1\qquad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(4.19)

This is a normalized distortion function.
The
\min(\cdot,1)
cap ensures normalization when

\hat{s}_{o}\in\text{Cn}(A)\setminus S_{O}
has derivation depth exceeding
d_{\max}
.

Remark 4.14

(Interpretation of depth distortion)
.

The depth distortion quantifies how much the
inferential complexity
of a
state changes through the channel:
\text{Dd}(s_{o}\mid A)
is the minimum number of
proof steps to derive
s_{o}
from the irredundant core

(Definition
3.11
)
.
If
\hat{s}_{o}
has the same depth as
s_{o}
relative to
A
, the depth
distortion is zero regardless of whether the two states are identical.
States not derivable from the sender’s core
(
\hat{s}_{o}\notin\text{Cn}(A)
) receive the maximum penalty
1
.

Definition 4.16

(Composite semantic distortion)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\geq 0
with
\alpha+\beta+\gamma=1
be fixed

distortion weights
and let
\Gamma
be a reference base for

d_{\text{Cn}}
.
The
composite semantic distortion
is

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})

\displaystyle\;:=\;

\displaystyle\alpha\,d_{H}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})\;+\;\beta\,d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid\Gamma)\;+\;\gamma\,d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}).

(4.20)

This is a normalized distortion function

(Definition
4.12
)
with values in
[0,1]
.

Remark 4.15

(Choice of weights and special cases)
.

Setting
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(1,0,0)
recovers the classical Hamming
distortion;
(0,1,0)
yields a purely deductive-content measure; and

(0,0,1)
isolates the structural-complexity component.
The choice of weights and reference base
\Gamma
reflects the
application-specific trade-off between symbol fidelity, inferential
completeness, and proof-complexity preservation.

Definition 4.17

(Expected semantic distortion)
.

Let
(S_{O},P_{O})
be a semantic source

(Definition
4.2
)
and let
d
be a distortion
function.
The
expected semantic distortion
under the joint distribution
induced by
P_{O}
and
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
is

\bar{d}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O}):=\sum_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\in\hat{S}_{O}}P_{O}(s_{o})\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})d(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}).

(4.21)

When
d=d_{\mathrm{sem}}
, we write

\bar{d}_{\mathrm{sem}}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})
.

Proposition 4.8

(Zero-distortion characterization)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semantic channel with
\hat{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}

and let
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
.

(i)

Hamming:

\bar{d}_{H}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})=0
for every full-support

P_{O}\in\Delta(S_{O})
if and only if

S_{O}\subseteq\hat{S}_{O}
and
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
is the deterministic
kernel corresponding to the inclusion

\mathrm{id}:S_{O}\hookrightarrow\hat{S}_{O}
.

(ii)

Closure:

If for every
s_{o}\in S_{O}
and every

\hat{s}_{o}\in\supp\bigl(\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\cdot\mid s_{o})\bigr)
,

d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})=0
,
then for each such pair
(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})
,

\text{Cn}(S_{O})=\text{Cn}\bigl((S_{O}\setminus\{s_{o}\})\cup\{\hat{s}_{o}\}\bigr)
.

(iii)

Ideal channel:

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be an ideal semantic channel

(Definition
4.11
)
with

\hat{S}_{O}=S_{O}
and

\tau_{\mathrm{e2e}}=\mathrm{id}_{S_{O}}
.
Then
\bar{d}_{\mathrm{sem}}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})=0
for every

P_{O}\in\Delta(S_{O})
and every choice of weights

(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
.

(i) Since
d_{H}\geq 0
and
P_{O}
is full-support,

\bar{d}_{H}=0
iff
d_{H}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})=0
whenever

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})>0
.
This forces
\hat{s}_{o}=s_{o}
for every reachable pair, i.e.,
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}

is deterministic with
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}\mid s_{o})=1
for all
s_{o}\in S_{O}
,
which requires
S_{O}\subseteq\hat{S}_{O}
.

(ii)
d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})=0
means the Jaccard distance (
4.18
)
vanishes, i.e.,
C_{s}=C_{\hat{s}}
, which is precisely

\text{Cn}(S_{O})=\text{Cn}\bigl((S_{O}\setminus\{s_{o}\})\cup\{\hat{s}_{o}\}\bigr)

(since
\text{Cn}(\Gamma_{-s_{o}}\cup\{s_{o}\})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
when
\Gamma=S_{O}
).

(iii) Under the stated hypotheses,
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
is the identity kernel on

S_{O}
, so
\hat{s}_{o}=s_{o}
deterministically.
Hence
d_{H}(s_{o},s_{o})=0
,
d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},s_{o}\mid\Gamma)=0

(since
C_{s}=C_{\hat{s}}
), and
d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},s_{o})=0

(since
s_{o}\in\text{Cn}(A)
by
Proposition
3.3
(iv) and the depth difference is
zero).
Therefore
d_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o},s_{o})=0
for all
s_{o}
, and

\bar{d}_{\mathrm{sem}}=0
.
∎

The per-pair distortion functions above quantify the quality of individual
state reconstructions.
We now introduce set-level fidelity measures that compare two knowledge bases

as wholes
, connecting directly to the noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
of Section
III-E
.

Proposition 4.9

(Noise-pair bounds on semantic distortion)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semantic channel with end-to-end noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})

(Proposition
4.5
)
, kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}:S_{O}\rightsquigarrow\tilde{S}_{O}
,
and source distribution
P_{O}\in\Delta(S_{O})
.
Let
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
.

(i)

Closure distortion under core preservation:

If
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
and

S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
, then for every

s_{o}\in S_{O}\setminus A
and every

\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}
,

d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})=0
.
Consequently, the expected closure distortion satisfies

\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})\;\leq\;P_{O}(A)\cdot\max_{a\in A}\;\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(a\mid\mathfrak{C}).

(4.22)

(ii)

Depth distortion under vocabulary match:

If
S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing

(
i.e.,

\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
)
,
then for every reachable pair
(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})
with

\hat{s}_{o}\in S_{O}=\tilde{S}_{O}
,

\hat{s}_{o}\in\text{Cn}(A)

(
by Proposition
3.3
(iv))
,
and the depth distortion reduces to

d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})=\min\!\Bigl(\frac{\lvert\text{Dd}(s_{o}\mid A)-\text{Dd}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid A)\rvert}{\max(\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I}),1)},1\Bigr).

(iii)

Spurious-region penalty:

If
\hat{s}_{o}\in S_{O}^{+}
and

\hat{s}_{o}\notin\text{Cn}(A)
, then

d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})=1
and

d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})
is typically positive.
In particular, spurious states that are not derivable from the
sender’s core receive the maximum depth penalty.

(i) For
s_{o}\in S_{O}\setminus A
: the state
s_{o}
is redundant, i.e.,

s_{o}\in\text{Cn}(S_{O}\setminus\{s_{o}\})
, so

\text{Cn}(S_{O}\setminus\{s_{o}\})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.
For any
\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}
: since

A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
and
S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
,
Corollary
4.1
(iii) gives

\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
, hence

\hat{s}_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}\subseteq\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.
Then

\text{Cn}\bigl((S_{O}\setminus\{s_{o}\})\cup\{\hat{s}_{o}\}\bigr)=\text{Cn}(S_{O}\setminus\{s_{o}\})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
,
so
d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})=0
.
The bound (
4.22
) follows because non-core states
contribute zero:

\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})=\sum_{a\in A}P_{O}(a)\,\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(a\mid\mathfrak{C})\leq P_{O}(A)\max_{a\in A}\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(a\mid\mathfrak{C})
.

(ii) If
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
, every reachable
\hat{s}_{o}\in S_{O}
lies in

\text{Cn}(A)=\text{Cn}(S_{O})\supseteq S_{O}

(Proposition
3.3
(iv)).
Hence the first branch of (
4.19
) applies.

(iii) If
\hat{s}_{o}\notin\text{Cn}(A)
, the second branch of (
4.19
)
gives
d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})=1
.
For the closure distortion, replacing
s_{o}
by an

\hat{s}_{o}\notin\text{Cn}(A)
generally adds consequences outside

\text{Cn}(S_{O})
, causing
C_{\hat{s}}\neq C_{s}
.
∎

Remark 4.16

(Hierarchy of semantic distortion and relation to classical measures)
.

The four distortion components are ordered by semantic depth.
Hamming distortion
d_{H}
is the coarsest: it detects any symbol change.
Closure distortion
d_{\text{Cn}}
is strictly finer—it can be zero even when

d_{H}=1
, namely when the substitution of
\hat{s}_{o}
for
s_{o}
preserves all
deductive consequences

(Remark
4.13
(a))
.
Depth distortion
d_{\text{Dd}}
is orthogonal to closure distortion: two states may
generate the same closure yet reside at different strata, or conversely share the
same depth but differ in deductive content.
The composite
d_{\mathrm{sem}}
reconciles these dimensions via the weight
vector.

At the set level, Proposition
3.5
(iii)
shows that
deductive completeness is robust to noise that respects the irredundant core:
as long as no core element is lost and all spurious additions are already
derivable, the deductive closure is perfectly preserved.
This result has no counterpart in classical (Hamming-based) distortion theory,
where any symbol error incurs positive distortion.

In Shannon’s rate–distortion theory
[
8
]
, the distortion
function is an arbitrary non-negative function on source–reconstruction pairs.
The semantic distortion functions defined above are instances of this general
framework, but they exploit the proof-system structure of
Section
III
to assign distortion values that reflect

inferential
rather than merely
syntactic
differences.
The expected distortion
\bar{d}_{\mathrm{sem}}

(Definition
4.17
)
serves as the fidelity
criterion in the semantic rate–distortion and channel coding problems
formulated in Sections
IV-D
–
IV-E
.

IV-D

Semantic Channel Invariants

This subsection defines the principal information-theoretic and structural
invariants of a semantic channel: semantic mutual information and semantic
channel capacity (quantifying the information throughput), and two structural
indices—
semantic fidelity
and
depth expansion
—that capture
how well the channel preserves the proof-system structure of
Section
III
.
Throughout, all logarithms are base
2
and informational quantities are
measured in bits.

Definition 4.18

(Shannon entropy, conditional entropy, and mutual information)
.

Let
X,Y
be jointly distributed finite random variables with joint pmf

P_{XY}
, marginals
P_{X},P_{Y}
, and conditional pmf

P_{Y\mid X}
.

(i)

The
Shannon entropy
of
X
is

H(X):=-\sum_{x}P_{X}(x)\log P_{X}(x)
.

(ii)

The
conditional entropy
is

H(Y\mid X):=-\sum_{x,y}P_{XY}(x,y)\log P_{Y\mid X}(y\mid x)
.

(iii)

The
mutual information
is

I(X;Y):=H(X)-H(X\mid Y)=H(Y)-H(Y\mid X)
.

We adopt the conventions
0\log 0:=0
and
0\log(0/0):=0
;
see
[
8
]
for standard properties.

Definition 4.19

(Semantic mutual information)
.

Let
(S_{O},P_{O})
be a semantic source

(Definition
4.2
)
and let
\mathfrak{C}
be a
semantic channel with end-to-end kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}:S_{O}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{O}

(Definition
4.10
)
.
Denote the induced joint distribution on
S_{O}\times\hat{S}_{O}
by

P_{O\hat{O}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}):=P_{O}(s_{o})\,\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o}).

The
semantic mutual information
is

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{sem}}(P_{O},\mathfrak{C}):=

\displaystyle I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\,\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}}P_{O\hat{O}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})\log\frac{P_{O\hat{O}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})}{P_{O}(s_{o})\,P_{\hat{O}}(\hat{s}_{o})},

(4.23)

where
P_{\hat{O}}(\hat{s}_{o}):=\sum_{s_{o}}P_{O\hat{O}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})
is the
marginal on
\hat{S}_{O}
.

Remark 4.17

(Where semantic structure enters)
.

The formula (
4.19
) is the standard Shannon mutual information;
there is no separate “semantic MI formula.”
The semantic content of the framework enters in three ways:

(a)
the kernel
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
is a composite enabling kernel
whose support respects the enabling structure at every stage

(Proposition
4.7
(ii))
;

(b)
the choice of encoder and decoder is constrained to

\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I})
and
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}})
;

(c)
the distortion and fidelity criteria that govern operational
performance exploit the proof-system structure

(Section
IV-C
)
.
Classical Shannon theory emerges when these constraints and criteria reduce to
their symbol-level counterparts.

Definition 4.20

(Shannon capacity of the carrier channel)
.

Let
W:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}
be the carrier channel kernel

(Definition
4.7
)
.
The
Shannon capacity
of
W
is

C(W)\;:=\;\max_{P_{C}\in\Delta(S_{C})}\;I(\mathsf{S}_{c};\,\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}),

(4.24)

where the mutual information is computed under the joint

P_{C}(s_{c})\,W(\hat{s}_{c}\mid s_{c})
.
Since
S_{C}
and
\hat{S}_{C}
are finite, the maximum exists and satisfies

0\leq C(W)\leq\log\min(|S_{C}|,|\hat{S}_{C}|)
.

Definition 4.21

(Semantic channel capacity)
.

Fix the carrier channel kernel
W
and the information models

\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}

(Section
IV-B
)
.
The
semantic channel capacity
is

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\;:=\;\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{O}\in\Delta(S_{O}),\\[2.0pt]
\kappa_{\text{enc}}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}),\\[2.0pt]
D\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}})\end{subarray}}I_{\mathrm{sem}}(P_{O},\,\mathfrak{C}),

(4.25)

where
\mathfrak{C}=(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}},\kappa_{\text{enc}},W,D)
and

I_{\mathrm{sem}}
is as in Definition
4.19
.
The maximum exists because the feasible set
(a product of probability simplices and stochastic-matrix polytopes
intersected with the enabling support constraints) is compact
and the objective is continuous.

Remark 4.18

(Dependence on enabling structures)
.

The notation
C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)
suppresses the dependence on

\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}
; more precisely,
the capacity depends on the enabling maps

\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}
that constrain the
feasible encoders and decoders.
When the enabling constraints are vacuous (full enabling), the dependence
vanishes and Theorem
4.1
(iii)
recovers

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)=C(W)
.

Theorem 4.1

(Data processing bound and equality conditions)
.

Let
W:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}
be a carrier channel kernel.

(i)

Upper bound.

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\;\leq\;C(W)
.

(ii)

Source entropy bound.

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\;\leq\;\log|S_{O}|
.
Consequently,

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\;\leq\;\min\!\bigl(C(W),\;\log|S_{O}|\bigr).

(4.26)

(iii)

Sufficient conditions for equality.

If the following three conditions hold:

(a)

\mathcal{E}(s_{o})=S_{C}
for every
s_{o}\in S_{O}

(
full encoding enabling
)
;

(b)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}(\hat{s}_{c})=\hat{S}_{O}
for every

\hat{s}_{c}\in\hat{S}_{C}

(
full decoding enabling
)
;

(c)

|S_{O}|\geq|S_{C}|
and
|\hat{S}_{O}|\geq|\hat{S}_{C}|
;

then
C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)=C(W)
.

(i) For any admissible
(\kappa_{\text{enc}},D,P_{O})
, the Markov chain

\mathsf{S}_{o}\to\mathsf{S}_{c}\to\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}\to\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}

holds by Remark
4.10
.
The data processing inequality
[
8
]
gives

I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\leq I(\mathsf{S}_{c};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c})\leq C(W)
.
Taking the maximum over all admissible triples yields

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\leq C(W)
.

(ii)
I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\leq H(\mathsf{S}_{o})\leq\log|S_{O}|
.

(iii) We exhibit a triple achieving
I_{\mathrm{sem}}=C(W)
.
Let
P_{C}^{*}\in\Delta(S_{C})
achieve
C(W)
.
Since
|S_{O}|\geq|S_{C}|
and the encoding enabling is full, there exists a
deterministic surjection
f:S_{O}\to S_{C}
with

f(s_{o})\in\mathcal{E}(s_{o})=S_{C}
.
Choose
P_{O}
so that

P_{C}^{*}(s_{c})=\sum_{s_{o}:\,f(s_{o})=s_{c}}P_{O}(s_{o})
for every
s_{c}

(possible since
f
is surjective: assign mass within each fiber
proportionally to
P_{C}^{*}
).
Set
\kappa_{\text{enc}}(s_{c}\mid s_{o}):=\mathbf{1}[s_{c}=f(s_{o})]
.

Since
|\hat{S}_{O}|\geq|\hat{S}_{C}|
and the decoding enabling is full,
fix an injection
g:\hat{S}_{C}\to\hat{S}_{O}
with

g(\hat{s}_{c})\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}(\hat{s}_{c})=\hat{S}_{O}
.
Set
D(\hat{s}_{o}\mid\hat{s}_{c}):=\mathbf{1}[\hat{s}_{o}=g(\hat{s}_{c})]
.

The encoding is deterministic, so
\mathsf{S}_{c}=f(\mathsf{S}_{o})
and

H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}\mid\mathsf{S}_{o})=H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}\mid\mathsf{S}_{c})

(because
\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}
depends on
\mathsf{S}_{o}
only through

\mathsf{S}_{c}=f(\mathsf{S}_{o})
).
Hence

\displaystyle I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c})=

\displaystyle H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c})-H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}\mid\mathsf{S}_{o})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c})-H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}\mid\mathsf{S}_{c})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(\mathsf{S}_{c};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}).

Since
g
is injective,
\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}=g(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c})

is a deterministic invertible function of
\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}

(on the range of
g
); hence

I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})=I(\mathsf{S}_{o};g(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}))=I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c})

by the data processing equality for invertible
transformations
[
8
]
.
Combining with the earlier chain,

I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})=I(\mathsf{S}_{c};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c})=C(W)
.
∎

Theorem 4.2

(Computability of semantic channel invariants)
.

Under the standing finiteness assumptions

(Assumptions
3.1

and
3.4
)
and
Axiom
3.4
, the following quantities are computable
from the finite instance data

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}},W)
:

(i)

The Shannon capacity
C(W)
.

(ii)

The semantic channel capacity
C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)
.

(iii)

For any fixed semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}
and source
P_{O}
:
the semantic mutual information

I_{\mathrm{sem}}(P_{O},\mathfrak{C})
,
the expected semantic distortion

\bar{d}_{\mathrm{sem}}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})
,
and the structural indices defined below.

(iv)

The semantic invariants
\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{I})
,

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})
,
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
,
and the derivation depths
\text{Dd}(q\mid\text{Atom}(S_{O}))
for all

q\in S_{O}
.

(i) and (ii):
All state spaces are finite, so the feasible set of each optimization
is a product of probability simplices intersected with finitely many
linear enabling-support constraints—hence a compact subset
of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
for finite
N
.
The mutual information is a continuous function of the joint
distribution (with the convention
0\log 0:=0
), and the joint
distribution is an affine (hence continuous) function of the
optimization variables.
By the extreme value theorem the maxima
in (
4.24
) and (
4.25
) exist.

Computability (to arbitrary precision).

For
C(W)
, the Blahut–Arimoto
algorithm
[
8
]
computes the maximum to
arbitrary precision in finitely many iterations.
For
C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)
, the feasible set is a compact
subset of a finite-dimensional Euclidean space
(all variables are entries of finite stochastic matrices).
The objective
I_{\mathrm{sem}}
is continuous on this
compact set.
Hence for any
\epsilon>0
, a finite
\epsilon
-net of the
feasible set yields a value within
\epsilon
of the true
maximum, and each evaluation is a finite computation.
(In practice, one may enumerate all deterministic
encoder–decoder pairs—of which there are finitely many—and
for each fixed pair perform a concave maximization
over
P_{O}
via the Blahut–Arimoto method, then take the
overall maximum; this yields an exact solution in finite time
since the pointwise maximum of finitely many concave programs
is attained.)

(iii):
For fixed
\mathfrak{C}
and
P_{O}
, the joint distribution

P_{O\hat{O}}
is a finite table computable by matrix multiplication
(equation (
4.10
)).
The mutual information (
4.19
) and expected
distortion (
4.21
) are finite sums over this table.
The closure distortion
d_{\text{Cn}}
and depth distortion
d_{\text{Dd}}
require
computing
\text{Cn}(\cdot)
and
\text{Dd}(\cdot\mid\cdot)
, which are computable
by iterating
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
(Axiom
3.4
).

(iv):
This is the content of
Theorem
3.1
(ii).
∎

The semantic mutual information and capacity characterize the

information throughput
of a semantic channel.
The following two indices characterize its
structural quality
:
how faithfully the channel preserves the deductive closure and the
derivation-depth stratification of the source.

Definition 4.22

(Per-input expected distortions)
.

For a semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}
and each
s_{o}\in S_{O}
, define the

per-input expected closure distortion
and

per-input expected depth distortion
:

\displaystyle\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\in\hat{S}_{O}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})\,d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O}),

\displaystyle\bar{d}_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\in\hat{S}_{O}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})\,d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}).

Definition 4.23

(Channel semantic fidelity index)
.

The
semantic fidelity index
of a semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}
is

\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C}):=\min_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\bigl(1-\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C})\bigr)=1-\max_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C}).

(4.27)

Definition 4.24

(Channel depth expansion index)
.

The
depth expansion index
of a semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}
is

\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C})\;:=\;\max_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\;\bar{d}_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C}).

(4.28)

Proposition 4.10

(Properties of structural indices)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semantic channel.

(i)

Range:

0\leq\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})\leq 1
and

0\leq\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C})\leq 1
.

(ii)

Perfect fidelity:

\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})=1
if and only if for every

s_{o}\in S_{O}
and every

\hat{s}_{o}\in\supp(\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\cdot\mid s_{o}))
,

d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})=0
.
Equivalently, every reachable substitution preserves the
deductive closure of
S_{O}

(Proposition
4.8
(ii))
.

(iii)

Zero depth expansion:

\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C})=0
if and only if for every

s_{o}\in S_{O}
and every

\hat{s}_{o}\in\supp(\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\cdot\mid s_{o}))
,

\hat{s}_{o}\in\text{Cn}(A)
and

\text{Dd}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid A)=\text{Dd}(s_{o}\mid A)
.

(iv)

Ideal channel:

If
\mathfrak{C}
is ideal

(Definition
4.11
)
with

\hat{S}_{O}=S_{O}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{e2e}}=\mathrm{id}_{S_{O}}
,
then
\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})=1
and

\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C})=0
.

(v)

Computability:

Both
\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})
and
\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C})

are computable from the instance data under
Axiom
3.4
.

(vi)

Expected distortion bounds:

For any
P_{O}\in\Delta(S_{O})
,

\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})\;\leq\;1-\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C}),\qquad\bar{d}_{\text{Dd}}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})\;\leq\;\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C}).

(i) Each per-input distortion lies in
[0,1]
(since
d_{\text{Cn}}
and
d_{\text{Dd}}

are normalized), so their convex combinations and extrema over
S_{O}
lie
in
[0,1]
.

(ii)
\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})=1
iff

\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C})=0
for all
s_{o}\in S_{O}
.
Since
d_{\text{Cn}}\geq 0
and
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})\geq 0
,

\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C})=0
iff

d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})=0
whenever

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})>0
.

(iii) Analogous to (ii), using that
d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})=0
iff

\hat{s}_{o}\in\text{Cn}(A)
and

\text{Dd}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid A)=\text{Dd}(s_{o}\mid A)
.

(iv) Under the stated hypotheses,
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
is the identity kernel
on
S_{O}
, so the only reachable pair for each
s_{o}
is

\hat{s}_{o}=s_{o}
.
Then
d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},s_{o}\mid S_{O})=0
and
d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},s_{o})=0

(Proposition
4.8
(iii)), giving

\mathsf{F}=1
and
\mathsf{E}=0
.

(v) For each
s_{o}\in S_{O}
and each

\hat{s}_{o}\in\hat{S}_{O}
, both
d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})
and

d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})
are computable
(Theorem
4.2
(iii)).
Since
S_{O}
and
\hat{S}_{O}
are finite, the sums and the

\max/\min
over
S_{O}
are computable.

(vi)
\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})=\sum_{s_{o}}P_{O}(s_{o})\,\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C})\leq\max_{s_{o}}\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C})=1-\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})
.
The depth bound is analogous.
∎

Corollary 4.2

(Fidelity concentration on core elements)
.

Under the conditions of
Proposition
4.9
(i)

(
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
and

S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
)
,
the semantic fidelity index

(Definition
4.23
)
satisfies

\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})\;=\;1-\max_{a\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})}\;\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(a\mid\mathfrak{C}).

(4.29)

That is, the worst-case closure distortion is attained at a core
element; redundant states contribute zero closure distortion.

By Proposition
4.9
(i),

\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C})=0
for every

s_{o}\in S_{O}\setminus A
.
Hence

\max_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C})=\max_{a\in A}\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(a\mid\mathfrak{C})
,
and the conclusion follows from the definition of

\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})

(Definition
4.23
).
∎

The structural indices
\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})
and

\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C})
characterize the worst-case quality of
individual state reconstructions.
The following indices exploit the noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})

(Proposition
4.5
) and the noise-region
partition (Definition
4.9
) to capture the
channel’s quality at the level of the knowledge base as a whole.

Definition 4.25

(Probabilistic core preservation index)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semantic channel with

A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
(no core element is lost from the
reconstructed vocabulary).
The
probabilistic core preservation index
is

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C}):=\min_{a\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})}\pi(a)=\min_{a\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(a\mid a).

(4.30)

When
A\cap S_{O}^{-}\neq\varnothing
, set

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C}):=0
.

Definition 4.26

(Spurious probability index)
.

The
spurious probability index
of a semantic
channel
\mathfrak{C}
is

\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C}):=\max_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\;p_{+}(s_{o})=\max_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\;\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\in S_{O}^{+}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o}).

(4.31)

Proposition 4.11

(Properties of noise-pair indices)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semantic channel.

(i)

Range:

0\leq\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})\leq 1
and

0\leq\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C})\leq 1
.

(ii)

Ideal channel:

If
\mathfrak{C}
is ideal

(Definition
4.11
)
with

\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
and

\tau_{\mathrm{e2e}}=\mathrm{id}_{S_{O}}
, then

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})=1
and

\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C})=0
.

(iii)

Relation to Hamming distortion:

For any full-support
P_{O}
,

\bar{d}_{H}^{+}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})\;\leq\;\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C}),

(4.32)

where
\bar{d}_{H}^{+}
is the spurious-substitution
component of (
(i)
).

(iv)

Relation to core self-preservation:

1-\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})\;\leq\;\max_{a\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})}\;\bar{d}_{H}(a\mid\mathfrak{C}),

(4.33)

where
\bar{d}_{H}(a\mid\mathfrak{C}):=1-\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(a\mid a)
when

a\in\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}
, and

\bar{d}_{H}(a\mid\mathfrak{C}):=1
when

a\in S_{O}^{-}
.

(v)

Upper bound on expected Hamming distortion:

For any
P_{O}\in\Delta(S_{O})
,

\bar{d}_{H}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})\;\leq\;1-\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})\cdot P_{O}\bigl(\text{Atom}(S_{O})\bigr).

(4.34)

In particular, if
P_{O}
is uniform on
S_{O}
,

\bar{d}_{H}\leq 1-\Phi_{\text{Atom}}\cdot|A|/|S_{O}|
.

(vi)

Computability:

Both
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})
and

\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C})
are computable from the finite
instance data.

(i) Each
\pi(a)\in[0,1]
and each
p_{+}(s_{o})\in[0,1]
,
so the extrema lie in
[0,1]
.

(ii) Under the stated hypotheses,
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
is the identity
kernel on
S_{O}
, so
\pi(a)=1
for all
a
and

S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
, giving
p_{+}(s_{o})=0
for all
s_{o}
.

(iii)
\bar{d}_{H}^{+}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})=\sum_{s_{o}}P_{O}(s_{o})\,p_{+}(s_{o})\leq\max_{s_{o}}p_{+}(s_{o})=\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C})
.

(iv) For
a\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})\cap\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}
,

\bar{d}_{H}(a\mid\mathfrak{C})=\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\neq a}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid a)=1-\pi(a)\geq 1-\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})

(since
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}\leq\pi(a)
).
The case
a\in S_{O}^{-}
gives
\bar{d}_{H}(a\mid\mathfrak{C})=1\geq 1-\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})
.
Hence

\max_{a}\bar{d}_{H}(a\mid\mathfrak{C})\geq 1-\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})
.

(v) For every
s_{o}\in S_{O}
,

\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\neq s_{o}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})=1-\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}\mid s_{o})
,
where
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}\mid s_{o}):=0
when

s_{o}\notin\tilde{S}_{O}
(since the kernel maps into

\tilde{S}_{O}
and
s_{o}\notin\tilde{S}_{O}
forces all mass to

\hat{s}_{o}\neq s_{o}
).
Hence

\displaystyle\bar{d}_{H}=

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}P_{O}(s_{o})\bigl(1-\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}\mid s_{o})\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\sum_{s_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}}P_{O}(s_{o})\,\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}\mid s_{o}).

If
A\cap S_{O}^{-}\neq\varnothing
, then

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}=0
and (
4.34
) reduces to

\bar{d}_{H}\leq 1
, which is trivially true.
If
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
, then

A\subseteq\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}
and

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{o}\in\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}}P_{O}(s_{o})\,\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}\mid s_{o})\;\geq\;

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A}P_{O}(a)\,\pi(a)

\displaystyle\;\geq\;

\displaystyle P_{O}(A)\cdot\Phi_{\text{Atom}},

so
\bar{d}_{H}\leq 1-P_{O}(A)\,\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
.

(vi) Each
\pi(a)
and
p_{+}(s_{o})
is a finite sum of entries
of
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
, which is computable
(Theorem
4.2
(iii)).
The extrema over the finite sets
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and
S_{O}
are
then computable by enumeration.
∎

Remark 4.19

(Summary of noise-pair-based channel quality descriptors)
.

The channel quality of a semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}
is now
described by two complementary families of indices.

The
structural indices

\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})
and
\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C})

(Definitions
4.23
–
4.24
)
measure worst-case closure and depth distortion across all source states.

The
noise-pair indices

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})
and
\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C})

(Definitions
4.25
–
4.26
)
measure the channel’s fidelity specifically with respect to the
noise-region partition of
\tilde{S}_{O}
:

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
captures how well the irredundant core is preserved
at the symbol level, while
\Psi_{+}
captures the worst-case
probability of “hallucinating” a state outside the sender’s
vocabulary.
Together, the four scalar indices

(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{E},\Phi_{\text{Atom}},\Psi_{+})
provide a
compact, computable fingerprint of the semantic channel’s quality
that is richer than any single classical metric.

When
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
(trivial noise pair),

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
reduces to the minimum diagonal entry of

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}
restricted to core elements, and

\Psi_{+}=0
; the noise-pair indices then complement the
structural indices without adding redundancy.
When
\tilde{S}_{O}\neq S_{O}
, the noise-pair indices capture
vocabulary-mismatch effects that are invisible to the structural
indices alone.

Definition 4.27

(Receiver-side structural comparison indices)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semantic channel with end-to-end noisy
semantic base
\tilde{S}_{O}
and source space
S_{O}
.

(i)

The
atomicity shift
is

\Delta\mathsf{A}(\mathfrak{C})\;:=\;\lvert\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O})\rvert\;-\;\lvert\text{Atom}(S_{O})\rvert.

(4.35)

(ii)

The
depth shift
is

\displaystyle\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathfrak{C})

\displaystyle\!:=\!

\displaystyle\max_{q\in\tilde{S}_{O}}\text{Dd}\bigl(q\!\mid\!\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O})\bigr)\!-\!\max_{q\in S_{O}}\text{Dd}\bigl(q\!\mid\!\text{Atom}(S_{O})\bigr).

(4.36)

Both quantities are well-defined, finite, and computable

(Remark
3.14
and
Theorem
3.1
(ii))
.

Proposition 4.12

(Properties of structural comparison indices)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semantic channel with noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
.

(i)

Trivial noise:

If
S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
, then

\Delta\mathsf{A}(\mathfrak{C})=0
and

\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathfrak{C})=0
.

(ii)

Core-preserving noise:

If
A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
and

S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
, then

\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})

(Corollary
4.1
(iii))

and
\text{Atom}(S_{O})\subseteq\tilde{S}_{O}
.
Consequently,

\lvert\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O})\rvert\leq\lvert\text{Atom}(S_{O})\rvert
,
i.e.,
\Delta\mathsf{A}(\mathfrak{C})\leq 0
:
core-preserving noise can only reduce or maintain the
receiver’s atomicity (additional derivable states may make
some previously irredundant elements redundant in
\tilde{S}_{O}
).

(iii)

Loss-only noise (
S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
):

\tilde{S}_{O}\subseteq S_{O}
, so

\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
and
the receiver’s closure can only shrink.
If some core element is lost
(
A\cap S_{O}^{-}\neq\varnothing
), then

\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})\subsetneq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
in general,
and
\Delta\mathsf{A}(\mathfrak{C})
may be negative
(fewer atoms needed to generate a smaller closure).

(iv)

Ideal channel with
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
:

\Delta\mathsf{A}=0
and
\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}=0
.

(i)
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
gives identical cores and depths.

(ii) By Corollary
4.1
(iii),

\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.
Since
A\subseteq\tilde{S}_{O}
and
\text{Cn}(A)=\text{Cn}(S_{O})=\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
,

A
is a generating set for
\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
; the irredundant core

\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O})
is a subset of some generating set with

|\text{Atom}(\tilde{S}_{O})|\leq|A|
.
(Formally: let
d\in S_{+,d}^{ij}
and suppose

a\in\text{Cn}\bigl((S_{O}^{(j)}\setminus\{a\})\bigr)
; this can
occur when
d
together with other elements of
S_{O}^{(j)}

provides an alternative derivation of
a
that bypasses the
direct core-level proof.
The irredundantization of
S_{O}^{(j)}
may then remove
a
,
yielding
|\text{Atom}(S_{O}^{(j)})|<|A^{(i)}|
.)

(iii)
\tilde{S}_{O}\subseteq S_{O}
gives

\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
by monotonicity (Cn2).
The strict inclusion when a core element is lost follows from the
irredundancy of
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
: if
a\in A\cap S_{O}^{-}
, then

a\notin\text{Cn}(A\setminus\{a\})\supseteq\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O}\cap A)
,
so
a\notin\text{Cn}(\tilde{S}_{O})
in general.

(iv) Immediate from (i).
∎

Remark 4.20

(Interpretation of structural shifts)
.

The atomicity shift
\Delta\mathsf{A}
measures whether the channel
makes the knowledge base
more or less compressed
in the
deductive sense:
\Delta\mathsf{A}<0
means the receiver’s core is
smaller than the sender’s (the noise has introduced derivable
redundancy), while
\Delta\mathsf{A}>0
means the receiver has
gained genuinely new irredundant content.
The depth shift
\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}
measures whether the
receiver’s deepest inference chain has grown or shrunk.
Together with the per-pair depth expansion
\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C})
,
these indices give a multi-scale view of the channel’s effect on
inferential structure:
\mathsf{E}
is a probabilistic worst-case
over individual states, while
\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}
is a
deterministic comparison of global maximum depths.

Corollary 4.3

(Irredundant source with trivial noise pair)
.

Suppose
\text{Atom}(S_{O})=S_{O}

(
i.e.,
S_{O}
is
irredundant
)
,
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}

(
trivial noise pair:
S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
)
,
and the enabling maps satisfy the full enabling conditions of
Theorem
4.1
(iii)(a)–(b)
with

|S_{O}|\geq|S_{C}|
and
|\hat{S}_{O}|\geq|\hat{S}_{C}|

(
the latter equivalent to
|S_{O}|\geq|\hat{S}_{C}|

since
\hat{S}_{O}=\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
)
.
Then:

(i)

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)=C(W)
.

(ii)

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})=0

(Theorem
3.1
(iv))
, so

d_{\text{Dd}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o})=0
for every reachable pair.

(iii)

The composite semantic distortion reduces to

d_{\mathrm{sem}}=\alpha\,d_{H}+\beta\,d_{\text{Cn}}

for every reachable pair.

(iv)

\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C})=0
and

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})=\min_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}\mid s_{o})
.

Parts (i)–(iii) follow from the same reasoning as
before (Theorem
4.1
(iii) and
Theorem
3.1
(iv)).
For (iv):
S_{O}^{+}=\varnothing
gives

\Psi_{+}=0
, and
\text{Atom}(S_{O})=S_{O}
with

\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
gives

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}=\min_{a\in S_{O}}\pi(a)=\min_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}(s_{o}\mid s_{o})
.
∎

Proposition 4.13

(Structural upper bounds on semantic channel capacity)
.

Let
W:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}
be a carrier channel kernel.

(i)

Combined bound:

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\leq\min\bigl(C(W),\;\log|S_{O}|,\;\log|\hat{S}_{O}|\bigr)
.

(ii)

Enabling-constrained bound:

If the encoding enabling map satisfies

|\mathcal{E}(s_{o})|\leq k
for all
s_{o}\in S_{O}
, then

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\;\leq\;\log\Bigl\lvert\bigcup_{s_{o}\in S_{O}}\mathcal{E}(s_{o})\Bigr\rvert\;\leq\;\log\bigl(|S_{O}|\cdot k\bigr).

(iii)

Core entropy bound:

For any semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}
and any

P_{O}\in\Delta(S_{O})
,

I_{\mathrm{sem}}(P_{O},\mathfrak{C})\;\leq\;H(\mathsf{S}_{o})\;\leq\;\log|S_{O}|.

(4.37)

(i) The bound
C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\leq C(W)
is
Theorem
4.1
(i).
The bound
C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\leq\log|S_{O}|
follows from

I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\leq H(\mathsf{S}_{o})\leq\log|S_{O}|
,
and
C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\leq\log|\hat{S}_{O}|
from

I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\leq H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\leq\log|\hat{S}_{O}|
.

(ii) By the enabling support constraint (
4.2
),

\supp(P_{C})\subseteq\bigcup_{s_{o}}\mathcal{E}(s_{o})
, so

H(\mathsf{S}_{c})\leq\log|\bigcup_{s_{o}}\mathcal{E}(s_{o})|
.
Data processing gives

I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\leq I(\mathsf{S}_{c};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c})\leq H(\mathsf{S}_{c})
.
The second inequality uses

|\bigcup_{s_{o}}\mathcal{E}(s_{o})|\leq|S_{O}|\cdot k
.

(iii)
I_{\mathrm{sem}}(P_{O},\mathfrak{C})=I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\leq H(\mathsf{S}_{o})\leq\log|S_{O}|
.
∎

Theorem 4.3

(Semantic Fano bound via noise-pair indices)
.

Let
(S_{O},P_{O})
be a full-support semantic source,

\mathfrak{C}
a semantic channel with end-to-end noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
, and let

\epsilon:=\bar{d}_{H}(\mathfrak{C},P_{O})
.
Write
h_{b}(p):=-p\log p-(1{-}p)\log(1{-}p)
for the binary entropy

(
base
2
)
.

(i)

Lower bound on
I_{\mathrm{sem}}
:

I_{\mathrm{sem}}(P_{O},\mathfrak{C})\;\geq\;H(\mathsf{S}_{o})-h_{b}(\epsilon)-\epsilon\log\bigl(|\tilde{S}_{O}|-1\bigr).

(4.38)

(ii)

Noise-pair substitution:

By Proposition
4.11
(v)
,

\epsilon\leq 1-\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})\cdot P_{O}(A)
.
Hence, when
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
is close to
1
and

P_{O}(A)
is bounded away from
0
,
the lower bound (
4.38
) is close to

H(\mathsf{S}_{o})
, i.e., the channel transmits nearly all
source entropy.

(iii)

Ideal channel:

If
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
,

\tau_{\mathrm{e2e}}=\mathrm{id}_{S_{O}}
, then

\epsilon=0
and

I_{\mathrm{sem}}=H(\mathsf{S}_{o})
.

(i) The standard Fano inequality

[
8
]
applied to the joint distribution

P_{O\hat{O}}
gives

H(\mathsf{S}_{o}\mid\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\;\leq\;h_{b}\!\bigl(\Pr[\mathsf{S}_{o}\neq\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}]\bigr)+\Pr[\mathsf{S}_{o}\neq\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}]\log\bigl(|\tilde{S}_{O}|-1\bigr).

Since
\Pr[\mathsf{S}_{o}\neq\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}]=\epsilon
and

I_{\mathrm{sem}}=H(\mathsf{S}_{o})-H(\mathsf{S}_{o}\mid\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})
,
inequality (
4.38
) follows.

(ii) Direct substitution of

\epsilon\leq 1-\Phi_{\text{Atom}}\cdot P_{O}(A)

into (
4.38
).
As
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}\to 1
with
P_{O}(A)
bounded below by some

p_{\min}>0
, one has
\epsilon\to 0
and the RHS tends to

H(\mathsf{S}_{o})
.

(iii) Under the stated conditions,
\epsilon=0

(Proposition
4.8
(iii)), so

h_{b}(0)=0
and the lower bound equals
H(\mathsf{S}_{o})
.
Since
I_{\mathrm{sem}}\leq H(\mathsf{S}_{o})
always, equality holds.
∎

Remark 4.21

(Role of the semantic Fano bound)
.

Theorem
4.3
closes the gap between the
noise-pair indices and the information-theoretic invariants:
a high probabilistic core preservation index

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}\approx 1
and a low spurious index

\Psi_{+}\approx 0
force
\bar{d}_{H}
to be small, which in
turn forces
I_{\mathrm{sem}}
to be close to the source entropy.
This quantifies the intuition that faithful transmission of the
irredundant core is both necessary and (approximately) sufficient
for high mutual information.
Conversely, a low
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
permits large
\bar{d}_{H}
and
thereby allows
I_{\mathrm{sem}}
to be small.
The bound is tight in the ideal-channel limit

(part (iii))
.

Remark 4.22

(Semantic compression advantage and effective source entropy)
.

The formal bound
I_{\mathrm{sem}}\leq\log|S_{O}|
treats all semantic
states as equally costly to transmit.
Under perfect closure fidelity

\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})=1

(Definition
4.23
)
, the situation improves
qualitatively.

Let
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and
J=S_{O}\setminus A
.
For any
redundant
state
j\in J
and any

\hat{s}_{o}\in\text{Cn}(S_{O})\cap\hat{S}_{O}
, Remark
4.13
(a)

gives
d_{\text{Cn}}(j,\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})=0
, because

\text{Cn}(S_{O}\setminus\{j\})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
(by definition of redundancy) implies

\text{Cn}\bigl((S_{O}\setminus\{j\})\cup\{\hat{s}_{o}\}\bigr)=\text{Cn}(S_{O})

whenever
\hat{s}_{o}\in\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.
Hence the channel need not distinguish among the
|J|
redundant states;
they can all be mapped to a single acceptable output.
Only the
|A|=|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|
irredundant core elements must be faithfully
transmitted.

If the decoder can compute
\text{Cn}(\cdot)
(e.g., by iterating

T_{\mathsf{PS}}
), it can reconstruct the full knowledge base
S_{O}

from a faithful copy of
A
alone.
The effective source entropy for closure-reliable transmission therefore
reduces to at most
\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|
bits, yielding a

deductive compression gain
of

\log(|S_{O}|/|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|)
bits.
This gain is zero precisely when
\text{Atom}(S_{O})=S_{O}

(Corollary
4.3
)
, recovering the
classical setting.
The formal achievability result exploiting this gain is
Theorem
4.6
in Section
IV-E
