.

Theorem 4.4

(Summary of semantic channel invariants and their
relationships)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a semantic channel

(Definition
4.10
)
with source
space
S_{O}
, reconstructed space

\tilde{S}_{O}=\hat{S}_{O}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
,
end-to-end kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}:S_{O}\rightsquigarrow\tilde{S}_{O}
,
carrier channel kernel
W
, and end-to-end noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
.
Let
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and let
P_{O}\in\Delta(S_{O})
be
full-support.
Under the standing assumptions of Section
III

and Axiom
3.4
, the following
invariants are all well-defined, finite, and computable:

I. Source-side structural invariants

(Section
III-D
):

\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{I})=|A|
,

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I})=\max_{q\in S_{O}}\text{Dd}(q\mid A)
.

II. Set-level fidelity invariants

(Definitions
3.17
–
3.18
,
Proposition
4.5
):

\rho_{\text{Atom}}(S_{O},\tilde{S}_{O})
,

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O},\tilde{S}_{O})
.

III. Noise-pair probabilistic indices

(Definitions
4.25
–
4.26
):

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C})
,

\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C})
.

IV. Structural quality indices

(Definitions
4.23
–
4.24
):

\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C})
,

\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C})
.

V. Receiver-side structural comparison

(Definition
4.27
):

\Delta\mathsf{A}(\mathfrak{C})
,

\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathfrak{C})
.

VI. Information-theoretic invariants

(Definitions
4.19
–
4.21
):

I_{\mathrm{sem}}(P_{O},\mathfrak{C})
,

C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)
,

C(W)
.

The key inter-family relationships are:

(a)

Data processing chain

(Theorem
4.1
):

I_{\mathrm{sem}}\leq C_{\mathrm{sem}}(W)\leq C(W)\leq\log\min(|S_{C}|,|\hat{S}_{C}|)
.

(b)

Distortion bounds via structural indices

(Proposition
4.10
(vi)):

\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}\leq 1-\mathsf{F}
and

\bar{d}_{\text{Dd}}\leq\mathsf{E}
.

(c)

Hamming bound via noise-pair index

(Proposition
4.11
(v)):

\bar{d}_{H}\leq 1-\Phi_{\text{Atom}}\cdot P_{O}(A)
.

(d)

Semantic Fano bound

(Theorem
4.3
):

I_{\mathrm{sem}}\geq H(\mathsf{S}_{o})-h_{b}(1{-}\Phi_{\text{Atom}}P_{O}(A))-(1{-}\Phi_{\text{Atom}}P_{O}(A))\log(|\tilde{S}_{O}|{-}1)
.

(e)

Core concentration of fidelity

(Corollary
4.2
):

under core-preserving noise,

\mathsf{F}=1-\max_{a\in A}\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(a\mid\mathfrak{C})
.

(f)

Closure fidelity under core preservation

(Corollary
4.1
(iii)):

A\cap S_{O}^{-}=\varnothing
and

S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
imply

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O},\tilde{S}_{O})=1
.

(g)

Ideal collapse:

when
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}
and

\tau_{\mathrm{e2e}}=\mathrm{id}_{S_{O}}
, all
distortion-type invariants vanish and all
fidelity/preservation invariants are maximal:

\mathsf{F}=\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}=\rho_{\text{Atom}}=\Phi_{\text{Atom}}=1
,

\mathsf{E}=\Psi_{+}=\Delta\mathsf{A}=\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}=0
,

I_{\mathrm{sem}}=H(\mathsf{S}_{o})
.

All individual claims have been established in the cited results.
The computability of every invariant follows from
Theorem
4.2
and
Proposition
4.12
.
The ideal-channel collapse (g) collects
Proposition
4.8
(iii),
Remark
4.11
,
Proposition
4.10
(iv),
Proposition
4.11
(ii), and
Proposition
4.12
(iv).
∎

IV-E

Semantic Channel Coding: Preliminary Results

This subsection formulates the semantic channel coding problem and states
preliminary converse and achievability bounds.
The principal novelty relative to classical channel coding is the

semantic reliability criterion
: under closure-based fidelity,
the decoder need only reconstruct the irredundant core faithfully;
redundant states can be recovered by deductive inference, yielding a
measurable “deductive compression gain.”

Block extension.

For the carrier channel kernel
W:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}

(Definition
4.7
), the
n
-fold
memoryless extension
W^{\otimes n}:S_{C}^{n}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}^{n}

is defined by

W^{\otimes n}(\hat{s}_{c}^{n}\mid s_{c}^{n})\;:=\;\prod_{i=1}^{n}W\!\bigl(\hat{s}_{c}^{(i)}\mid s_{c}^{(i)}\bigr),

where
s_{c}^{n}=(s_{c}^{(1)},\ldots,s_{c}^{(n)})\in S_{C}^{n}
and similarly
for
\hat{s}_{c}^{n}
.

Definition 4.28

(Semantic block code)
.

An
(n,M)
semantic block code
for the carrier channel
W

consists of:

(i)

a
message set

\mathcal{M}\subseteq S_{O}
with

|\mathcal{M}|=M
;

(ii)

an
encoding function

f_{n}:\mathcal{M}\to S_{C}^{n}
;

(iii)

a
decoding function

g_{n}:\hat{S}_{C}^{n}\to\hat{S}_{O}
.

The
rate
of the code is

R:=(\log M)/n
bits per channel use
(all logarithms base
2
).

Remark 4.23

(Message set as semantic states)
.

In classical channel coding, the message set
\{1,\ldots,M\}
is an
abstract index set.
Here,
\mathcal{M}\subseteq S_{O}
carries the full semantic structure
(deductive closure, irredundant core, derivation depth) inherited from
Section
III
.
This structure enters the reliability criteria defined below but plays
no role in the encoding/decoding mechanics, which remain purely
combinatorial.

Definition 4.29

(Semantic reliability criteria)
.

Let
(f_{n},g_{n})
be an
(n,M)
semantic block code, and for each

m\in\mathcal{M}
let

\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}^{(m)}:=g_{n}\!\bigl(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}^{n}\bigr)

be the random reconstructed state when the codeword
f_{n}(m)
is
transmitted through
W^{\otimes n}
.

(i)

Maximum Hamming error probability:

P_{e}^{(n)}\;:=\;\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\;\Pr\!\bigl[\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}^{(m)}\neq m\bigr].

(4.39)

(ii)

Maximum expected semantic distortion

(for a distortion function
d
):

D_{\max}^{(n)}(d)\;:=\;\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\;\mathbb{E}\!\bigl[d\!\bigl(m,\,\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}^{(m)}\bigr)\bigr].

(4.40)

(iii)

Maximum closure error probability:

P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n)}\;:=\;\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\;\Pr\!\bigl[d_{\text{Cn}}\!\bigl(m,\,\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}^{(m)}\mid S_{O}\bigr)>0\bigr].

(4.41)

Remark 4.24

(Hierarchy of reliability criteria)
.

The three criteria are increasingly permissive.
Hamming reliability (
P_{e}^{(n)}\to 0
) demands exact symbol
reconstruction; semantic distortion reliability
(
D_{\max}^{(n)}(d_{\mathrm{sem}})\to 0
with
\alpha>0
) demands
vanishing expected distortion, which implies
P_{e}^{(n)}\to 0
since

d_{\mathrm{sem}}\geq\alpha\,d_{H}
;
closure reliability (
P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n)}\to 0
) only demands that every
reachable reconstruction preserves the deductive closure of
S_{O}
,
tolerating symbol errors that leave the closure unchanged.
The deductive compression gain of
Remark
4.22
is realized under the third
criterion.

Definition 4.30

(Achievable semantic rate and semantic coding capacity)
.

Fix a reliability criterion
\mathsf{Rel}\in\{P_{e},\;D_{\max}(d),\;P_{e,\text{Cn}}\}
.
A rate
R\geq 0
is
\mathsf{Rel}
-achievable
if there
exists a sequence of
(n_{k},M_{k})
semantic block codes with

\liminf_{k\to\infty}(\log M_{k})/n_{k}\geq R
and

\mathsf{Rel}^{(n_{k})}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
.

The
semantic coding capacity
under criterion

\mathsf{Rel}
is

C_{\mathrm{code}}(W,\mathsf{Rel})\;:=\;\sup\bigl\{R\geq 0:R\text{ is $\mathsf{Rel}$-achievable}\bigr\}.

When
\mathsf{Rel}=P_{e}
, we write
C_{\mathrm{code}}(W)
and
recover the classical (Hamming) coding capacity.

Theorem 4.5

(Converse bounds for semantic block codes)
.

Let
W:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}
be a carrier channel kernel.

(i)

Hamming converse:

Any
(n,M)
code with
P_{e}^{(n)}\leq\epsilon
satisfies

\log M\;\leq\;\frac{nC(W)+1}{1-\epsilon}.

(4.42)

(ii)

Composite-distortion converse:

If
d=d_{\mathrm{sem}}
with
\alpha>0
, then

D_{\max}^{(n)}(d_{\mathrm{sem}})\geq\alpha\,P_{e}^{(n)}
, so

D_{\max}^{(n)}\to 0
implies
P_{e}^{(n)}\to 0
and
part
(i)
applies.

(iii)

Closure converse (under
Assumption
4.1
):

Any
(n,|S_{O}|)
code with
\mathcal{M}=S_{O}
and

P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n)}\leq\epsilon
satisfies

\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|\;\leq\;\frac{nC(W)+1}{1-\epsilon}.

(4.43)

(iv)

Source-size bound:

M\leq|S_{O}|
for any single-letter message set.

(i) Fix an
(n,M)
code with
P_{e}^{(n)}\leq\epsilon
.
For a uniform message
\mathsf{M}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{M})
,
Fano’s inequality gives

H(\mathsf{M}\mid\hat{\mathsf{M}})\leq 1+\epsilon\log(M-1)\leq 1+\epsilon\log M
.
Hence

\log M=I(\mathsf{M};\hat{\mathsf{M}})+H(\mathsf{M}\mid\hat{\mathsf{M}})\leq nC(W)+1+\epsilon\log M,

where
I(\mathsf{M};\hat{\mathsf{M}})\leq I\!\bigl(f_{n}(\mathsf{M});\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{c}^{n}\bigr)\leq nC(W)
by data processing and the memoryless
property
[
8
]
.
Rearranging gives (
4.42
).

(ii) Since
d_{\mathrm{sem}}\geq\alpha\,d_{H}
with
\alpha>0
,

D_{\max}^{(n)}\geq\alpha\,P_{e}^{(n)}
.
Hence
D_{\max}^{(n)}\to 0
implies
P_{e}^{(n)}\to 0
, and
(i) applies.

(iii) Let
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
.
Under Assumption
4.1
, the acceptable sets

\{R_{\text{Cn}}(a):a\in A\}
are pairwise disjoint.
The decoder
g_{n}:\hat{S}_{C}^{n}\to\hat{S}_{O}
is a deterministic function,
so the preimages
B_{a}:=\{y^{n}:g_{n}(y^{n})\in R_{\text{Cn}}(a)\}
for

a\in A
are pairwise disjoint subsets of
\hat{S}_{C}^{n}
.

For each
a\in A
, closure reliability gives

W^{\otimes n}(B_{a}\mid f_{n}(a))\geq 1-\epsilon
.
Define the deterministic “core decoder”

\hat{a}:\hat{S}_{C}^{n}\to A\cup\{?\}
by

\hat{a}(y^{n}):=a
if
y^{n}\in B_{a}
, and

\hat{a}(y^{n}):=\,?
otherwise.
Then
\Pr[\hat{a}\neq a\mid\mathsf{M}=a]\leq\epsilon
for every

a\in A
, and the pairwise disjointness of

\{B_{a}\}
ensures that this is a standard
(non-list) decoding problem on
|A|
messages.
The Fano argument of part (i), applied with message set
A

and decoder
\hat{a}
, gives

\log|A|\leq\frac{nC(W)+1}{1-\epsilon}
.

(iv) Immediate from
M\leq|S_{O}|
.
∎

Remark 4.25

(Tightness of the converse)
.

Part
(iii)
shows that the Shannon capacity of the carrier
channel is an
absolute ceiling
even under the most permissive
(closure-based) reliability criterion: semantic inference at the
receiver cannot increase the channel’s information-carrying ability
beyond
C(W)
.
What semantic inference
does
achieve is a reduction in the
number of messages that need to be reliably distinguished, as shown
in the achievability result below.

Theorem 4.6

(Achievability for semantic block codes)
.

Let
W:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}
with
C(W)>0
, and let

A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
.

(i)

Hamming achievability (classical):

For any message set
\mathcal{M}\subseteq S_{O}
with

|\mathcal{M}|=M
and
\hat{S}_{O}\supseteq\mathcal{M}
,
every rate
R<C(W)
is
P_{e}
-achievable in the
following sense: for every blocklength
n
satisfying

(\log M)/n<C(W)
, there exists an
(n,M)
semantic
block code
(f_{n},g_{n})
with

f_{n}:\mathcal{M}\to S_{C}^{n}
and

g_{n}:\hat{S}_{C}^{n}\to\hat{S}_{O}
such that

P_{e}^{(n)}\to 0
as

n\to\infty
with
M
fixed.
In particular, the full knowledge base

\mathcal{M}=S_{O}
can be communicated
Hamming-reliably using

n>\log|S_{O}|/C(W)
channel uses.
No single-letter alphabet-size comparison between

|S_{O}|
and
|S_{C}|
is required, since the block
code operates over the product alphabet
S_{C}^{n}
.

(ii)

Closure achievability under deductive decoding:

Suppose
\hat{S}_{O}\supseteq S_{O}
, the encoding enabling is
full

(
\mathcal{E}(s_{o})=S_{C}
for all
s_{o}
)
,
and the decoder has access to the proof system

(\mathsf{PS},T_{\mathsf{PS}},\text{Cn})
.
Then there exists a sequence of
(n,|S_{O}|)
semantic block
codes—i.e., codes with
\mathcal{M}=S_{O}
—satisfying

P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n)}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, provided

\frac{\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|}{n}\;<\;C(W).

(4.44)

Equivalently, the full knowledge base
S_{O}
can be
communicated with vanishing closure error using

n>\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|/C(W)
channel uses.

(i) This is the standard channel coding theorem
(Shannon
[
34
]
,
cf.
[
8
]
, Theorem 7.7.1).
The encoding function
f_{n}:\mathcal{M}\to S_{C}^{n}
maps each message
to an
n
-length codeword over the carrier alphabet; the decoding
function
g_{n}:\hat{S}_{C}^{n}\to\hat{S}_{O}
maps each received sequence
to a reconstructed state in
\hat{S}_{O}\supseteq\mathcal{M}
.
The classical random-coding argument yields a code with maximum error
probability
P_{e}^{(n)}\to 0
whenever
(\log M)/n<C(W)
.
The condition
\hat{S}_{O}\supseteq\mathcal{M}
ensures that the
decoder can output any message.

(ii) The code is constructed in two layers.

Layer 1 (core code).

By the classical channel coding theorem, since

\log|A|/n<C(W)
, there exists an
(n,|A|)
block code

(f_{n}^{A},g_{n}^{A})
for
W
with message set
A
and

P_{e}^{(n)}(A)\to 0
.

Layer 2 (redundant reconstruction).

For each redundant state
j\in J=S_{O}\setminus A
, the decoder
does not attempt symbol-level reconstruction.
Instead, the encoding function is extended to all of
S_{O}
as
follows: for
j\in J
, set
f_{n}(j):=f_{n}^{A}(a_{0})
for an
arbitrary fixed
a_{0}\in A
.
The decoder first applies
g_{n}^{A}
to recover a core element

\hat{a}\in A
(or, in the error event, an incorrect
element), and then outputs
\hat{a}
regardless of whether the
sent message was in
A
or
J
.

Closure analysis.

For
m\in A
: if the core code decodes correctly
(
\hat{a}=m
), then
\hat{s}_{o}=m
and

d_{\text{Cn}}(m,m\mid S_{O})=0
.
Error probability:
P_{e}^{(n)}(A)\to 0
.

For
m=j\in J
: the decoder outputs some
\hat{a}\in A\subseteq S_{O}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.
Since
j
is redundant,
\text{Cn}(S_{O}\setminus\{j\})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.
As shown in Remark
4.22
,

d_{\text{Cn}}(j,\hat{a}\mid S_{O})=0
for any

\hat{a}\in\text{Cn}(S_{O})\cap\hat{S}_{O}\supseteq S_{O}\ni\hat{a}
.
Hence the closure error probability for redundant messages
is
zero
for all
n
.

Combining:
P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n)}\leq P_{e}^{(n)}(A)\to 0
.
∎

Remark 4.26

(Deductive compression gain realized)
.

Combining Theorem
4.5
(iii)
and
Theorem
4.6
(ii)

(
under Assumption
4.1
)
:
the minimum blocklength for closure-reliable communication of
S_{O}

satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|}{C(W)}\;\lesssim\;

\displaystyle n^{*}(S_{O},W,P_{e,\text{Cn}},\epsilon)

\displaystyle\;\lesssim\;

\displaystyle\frac{\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|}{C(W)},

up to terms vanishing as
\epsilon\to 0
.
In contrast, Hamming-reliable communication requires

n_{H}^{*}\approx\log|S_{O}|/C(W)
channel uses.
The deductive compression ratio

n^{*}/n_{H}^{*}\approx\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|/\log|S_{O}|

measures the channel-use savings afforded by semantic inference at the
receiver.
When
S_{O}
is irredundant, no savings occur

(Corollary
4.3
)
.
The two-layer code structure bears a conceptual resemblance to the
semantic channel coding theorem of Ma et al.
[
24
]
,
who exploit the many-to-one structure of synonymous sources to
reduce the effective message set.
The key difference is that their compression arises from a
pre-defined synonymous partition of the source alphabet, whereas
ours arises from the receiver’s ability to
re-derive

redundant states via the shared proof system—a mechanism that
requires no pre-agreed partition and yields the
vocabulary-invariant compression ratio of
Theorem
5.2
.

Remark 4.27

(Noise pair and the achievability mechanism)
.

The achievability proof of Theorem
4.6
(ii) can be
reinterpreted through the noise-pair lens.
The two-layer code constructs a decoding rule whose output always
lies in
\text{Atom}(S_{O})\subseteq S_{O}\cap\tilde{S}_{O}=\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}
,
thereby ensuring
p_{+}(s_{o})=0
for every message
s_{o}

(Definition
4.9
).
Closure reliability then reduces to symbol-level reliability on the
core:
P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n)}\leq P_{e}^{(n)}(A)\to 0
, where

P_{e}^{(n)}(A)
is the Hamming error probability of the

(n,|A|)
core sub-code.
The condition
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C}_{n})\to 1
as

n\to\infty
(where
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
denotes the semantic channel
induced by the
n
-block code) is equivalent to

P_{e}^{(n)}(A)\to 0
: each core element is self-preserved with
probability tending to
1
.
The deductive compression gain arises precisely because the code need
not achieve
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}=1
for non-core elements; these are
handled by the closure structure at zero additional channel cost.

Corollary 4.4

(Minimum blocklength for semantic communication)
.

Let
W:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}
with
C(W)>0
and

A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
.
Define the
minimum blocklength
for communicating the full
knowledge base
S_{O}
under criterion
\mathsf{Rel}
with
maximum error at most
\epsilon
:

\displaystyle n^{*}(S_{O},W,\mathsf{Rel},\epsilon)

\displaystyle\;:=\;

\displaystyle\min\bigl\{\,n\geq 1:\,\exists\,(n,|S_{O}|)\text{ code with }\mathsf{Rel}^{(n)}\leq\epsilon\,\bigr\}.

Then, for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
:

(i)

Hamming:

\displaystyle n^{*}(S_{O},W,P_{e},\epsilon)\geq\frac{(1-\epsilon)\log|S_{O}|-1}{C(W)}
.

(ii)

Closure:

Under the hypotheses of
Theorem
4.6
(ii)
,

\displaystyle n^{*}(S_{O},W,P_{e,\text{Cn}},\epsilon)\;\leq\;\left\lceil\frac{\log|A|}{C(W)-\delta(\epsilon)}\right\rceil

for some
\delta(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Under the additional
Assumption
4.1
below,

\displaystyle n^{*}(S_{O},W,P_{e,\text{Cn}},\epsilon)\;\geq\;\frac{(1-\epsilon)\log|A|-1}{C(W)}
.

The
deductive compression ratio
is therefore

\frac{n^{*}(S_{O},W,P_{e,\text{Cn}},\epsilon)}{n^{*}(S_{O},W,P_{e},\epsilon)}\;\approx\;\frac{\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|}{\log|S_{O}|}\,,

which equals
1
when
S_{O}
is irredundant and is strictly less
than
1
whenever
|J|=|S_{O}\setminus\text{Atom}(S_{O})|>0
.

Assumption 4.1

(Deductive independence of core elements)
.

For every pair of distinct core elements
a_{1},a_{2}\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})
,
the closure-acceptable output sets are disjoint:

R_{\text{Cn}}(a_{1})\;\cap\;R_{\text{Cn}}(a_{2})\;=\;\varnothing,

where
R_{\text{Cn}}(m):=\{\hat{s}_{o}\in\hat{S}_{O}:d_{\text{Cn}}(m,\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O})=0\}
.

Remark 4.28

(When Assumption
4.1
holds)
.

Assumption
4.1
is satisfied whenever each core
element
a\in\text{Atom}(S_{O})
contributes a unique “deductive increment”
to the closure—i.e., the set of formulas derivable from

S_{O}
but not from
S_{O}\setminus\{a\}
is disjoint for different
core elements.
This holds in many natural knowledge-base settings (e.g., when core
elements are ground facts about distinct entities and the inference
rules respect the entity structure).
When the assumption fails, the converse still holds with

|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|
replaced by the size of a maximal subset of

\text{Atom}(S_{O})
with pairwise disjoint acceptable sets.

Definition 4.31

(Semantic rate–distortion function)
.

Let
(S_{O},P_{O})
be a semantic source
(Definition
4.2
) and let
d
be a bounded
distortion function (Definition
4.12
).
The
semantic rate–distortion function
is

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(D)\;:=\;\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{\hat{S}_{o}\mid S_{o}}:\\[1.0pt]
\hat{S}_{o}\in\hat{S}_{O},\\[1.0pt]
\mathbb{E}\bigl[d(S_{o},\hat{S}_{o})\bigr]\leq D\end{subarray}}I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\,\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}),

(4.45)

where the minimization is over all conditional distributions

P_{\hat{S}_{o}\mid S_{o}}:\,S_{O}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{O}
satisfying
the expected distortion constraint, and the mutual information is
computed under the joint
P_{O}\cdot P_{\hat{S}_{o}\mid S_{o}}
.

Proposition 4.14

(Properties of the semantic rate–distortion function)
.

Under the standing finiteness assumptions, for any bounded distortion
function
d
:

(i)

Existence:

The minimum in (
4.45
) exists for every

D\geq 0
such that the feasible set is nonempty.

(ii)

Monotonicity:

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(D)
is non-increasing in
D
.

(iii)

Convexity:

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(D)
is convex in
D
.

(iv)

Boundary values:

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(0)\leq\log|S_{O}|
in general.
When
d=d_{H}
,

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(0)=H(P_{O})\leq\log|S_{O}|
.

(v)

Closure distortion at
D=0
:

When
d=d_{\text{Cn}}(\cdot,\cdot\mid S_{O})
and

\hat{S}_{O}\supseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})\cap\mathbb{S}_{O}
, then

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(0;\,d_{\text{Cn}})\;\leq\;\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|.

(4.46)

(vi)

Computability:

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(D)
is computable from the finite instance
data under Axiom
3.4
.

Parts (i)–(iv) follow from standard rate–distortion
theory on finite alphabets
(see
[
8
]
, Chapter 10): the minimization is over a
compact set (the probability simplex with a linear constraint), and
mutual information is continuous, convex in the conditional distribution
for fixed
P_{O}
, and lower-semicontinuous.

(v) We exhibit a feasible conditional distribution achieving

I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\leq\log|A|
at
D=0
.
Let
A=\text{Atom}(S_{O})
and
J=S_{O}\setminus A
.
Fix an arbitrary
a_{0}\in A
and define the deterministic mapping

\phi(s_{o}):=\begin{cases}s_{o}&\text{if }s_{o}\in A,\\
a_{0}&\text{if }s_{o}\in J.\end{cases}

Set
P_{\hat{S}_{o}\mid S_{o}}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o}):=\mathbf{1}[\hat{s}_{o}=\phi(s_{o})]
.

Distortion check.

For
s_{o}\in A
:
\hat{s}_{o}=s_{o}
, so

d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},s_{o}\mid S_{O})=0
.
For
s_{o}=j\in J
:
\hat{s}_{o}=a_{0}\in A\subseteq S_{O}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})
.
Since
j
is redundant,

\text{Cn}(S_{O}\setminus\{j\})=\text{Cn}(S_{O})
, so

\text{Cn}\bigl((S_{O}\setminus\{j\})\cup\{a_{0}\}\bigr)=\text{Cn}(S_{O})

(because
S_{O}\setminus\{j\}\subseteq(S_{O}\setminus\{j\})\cup\{a_{0}\}

and
(S_{O}\setminus\{j\})\cup\{a_{0}\}\subseteq S_{O}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}\setminus\{j\})
, so both directions of the inclusion follow
from monotonicity and idempotence of
Cn
).
Hence
d_{\text{Cn}}(j,a_{0}\mid S_{O})=0
and

\mathbb{E}[d_{\text{Cn}}]=0
.

Rate bound.

The mapping is deterministic, so

H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}\mid\mathsf{S}_{o})=0
and

I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})=H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})
.
Since
\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}=\phi(\mathsf{S}_{o})
takes values
in
A
,
H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\leq\log|A|
.

(vi) The feasible set is a compact subset of a finite-dimensional
Euclidean space (probability simplex with a linear distortion
constraint), and the objective
I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})

is continuous.
An
\epsilon
-net yields an approximation within
\epsilon

of the true minimum.
The distortion values
d_{\text{Cn}}
and
d_{\text{Dd}}
are computable by
iterating
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
(Axiom
3.4
).
∎

Remark 4.29

(Separation of semantic source and channel coding)
.

The classical source–channel separation theorem

[
34
,
8
]
states that source
coding and channel coding can be performed independently without loss
of optimality.
In the semantic framework, the same separation structure applies at
the formal level: the semantic source can be compressed to rate

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(D)
bits per symbol using the semantic distortion
measure, and these bits can be transmitted through the carrier channel
at any rate below
C(W)
.
The semantic rate–distortion function generalizes Shannon’s
classical rate–distortion formulation
[
33
,
8
]
.
The computational aspects of the semantic rate–distortion function
under synonymous mappings have been recently addressed by
Han et al.
[
14
]
via an extended Blahut–Arimoto algorithm;
our definition subsumes their setting when the distortion is specialized
to closure distortion
d_{\text{Cn}}
.
The semantic novelty is that the semantic rate–distortion function

R_{\mathrm{sem}}(D;\,d_{\text{Cn}})
can be strictly smaller than its
Hamming counterpart
R(D;\,d_{H})
, because closure-preserving
substitutions are penalty-free.
In particular, the zero-distortion rate under closure fidelity is at
most
\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|

(inequality (
4.46
))
,
compared to
H(P_{O})\leq\log|S_{O}|
under Hamming fidelity.

Remark 4.30

(Connection to knowledge-augmented communication
and multi-agent systems)
.

The semantic channel framework, and the deductive compression gain in
particular, are directly relevant to emerging applications.

In
retrieval-augmented generation
(RAG) pipelines, a knowledge
base is transmitted (or cached) between a retrieval module and a
language model.
The semantic coding results suggest that only the irredundant core of
the knowledge base needs to be transmitted; the remaining entries can
be reconstructed by the receiver’s inference engine, reducing
communication cost.

In
multi-agent systems
, heterogeneous agents maintain different
knowledge bases

(Remark
4.6
(ii))
.
The semantic channel framework provides a principled model for
analyzing inter-agent communication: the encoding and decoding enabling
constraints capture each agent’s representational capabilities, the
closure-based fidelity criterion captures whether the communicated
knowledge is
inferentially complete
for the receiver (rather
than merely symbol-accurate), and the deductive compression gain
quantifies the savings achievable when agents share a common proof
system.

These connections motivate the further development of semantic channel
codes, multi-letter extensions, and explicit code constructions as
subjects of ongoing and future work.

V
Application: Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Semantic Communication

The theoretical framework developed in Sections
III

and
IV
is fully general: the semantic state
space
S_{O}
, the reconstructed space
\hat{S}_{O}
, and the
enabling structures that constrain encoding and decoding are
left as abstract parameters.
This section instantiates the framework in a concrete and
practically motivated setting—
heterogeneous multi-agent
semantic communication
—and derives new results that
demonstrate the framework’s explanatory and predictive power
beyond what classical channel coding theory can provide.

The distinguishing feature of the heterogeneous setting is that
the sender and receiver maintain
different
knowledge
bases: the sender’s semantic space is
S_{O}
while the
receiver’s reconstructed space
\hat{S}_{O}
may differ
from
S_{O}
both in vocabulary (the set of expressible states)
and in inferential structure (the irredundant core and
derivation-depth stratification).
In the terminology of Section
III-E
, the
end-to-end noise pair
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
is generically

non-trivial
:
S_{O}^{-}\neq\varnothing
captures
sender concepts absent from the receiver’s vocabulary
(
vocabulary loss
), and
S_{O}^{+}\neq\varnothing

captures receiver concepts absent from the sender’s intent
(
vocabulary surplus
).
Classical Shannon theory, which treats sender and receiver
alphabets as abstract label sets, cannot distinguish vocabulary
loss from vocabulary surplus, nor can it exploit shared
deductive structure to reduce communication cost.
The semantic channel invariants of Section
IV-D

are precisely the tools needed to make these distinctions precise
and quantitative.

The section is organized as follows.
Section
V-A
describes the multi-agent
communication scenario and identifies the key design questions.
Section
V-B
formalizes the scenario
within the information model framework and states the standing
assumptions specific to this application.
Section
V-C
instantiates the semantic channel
machinery and derives closed-form relationships between
knowledge-base overlap structure and semantic channel invariants.
Section
V-D
presents the main analytical
results: conditions for closure-reliable heterogeneous
communication, a heterogeneous deductive compression theorem,
and a broadcast extension to one-sender–multi-receiver
scenarios.
Section
V-E
verifies all results on an
explicit Datalog knowledge-base instance with full numerical
computation of every invariant.

V-A

Problem Description: Heterogeneous Agent Communication

Consider a network of
K+1
autonomous agents—indexed by

i\in\{0,1,\ldots,K\}
—that must coordinate by exchanging semantic
states over noisy physical links.
Each agent
i
maintains a finite knowledge base

S_{O}^{(i)}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
, where
\mathbb{S}_{O}
is the
common ambient semantic universe introduced in
Section
III-D
.
All agents share the same proof system

(\mathsf{PS},\,T_{\mathsf{PS}},\,\text{Cn})
and the same semantic
sublanguage
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
; they differ, however, in
the
sets of semantic states they store and operate on
.
Agent
i
can derive consequences within
\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
and
possesses the irredundant core
A^{(i)}:=\text{Atom}(S_{O}^{(i)})

together with the associated derivation-depth stratification
(Definitions
3.8
and
3.11
).

This knowledge-base heterogeneity is the defining feature of the
scenario and the source of all phenomena that distinguish it from
classical Shannon-theoretic
communication
[
34
,
8
]
.
Recent work on semantic
communication
[
23
,
35
,
4
]

has highlighted the need for frameworks that go beyond
symbol-level fidelity, but a rigorous logical-information-theoretic
treatment of heterogeneous knowledge bases has been lacking.
When agent
i
(the
sender
) transmits a semantic
state
s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(i)}
to agent
j
(the
receiver
),
the receiver reconstructs a state

\hat{s}_{o}\in S_{O}^{(j)}
—not necessarily in

S_{O}^{(i)}
—because agent
j
can only produce outputs
expressible in its own vocabulary.
Unless
S_{O}^{(i)}=S_{O}^{(j)}
, the end-to-end noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
of
Proposition
4.5
is generically nontrivial:
states in
S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)}
have no direct
counterpart in the receiver’s vocabulary (
vocabulary loss
),
while states in
S_{O}^{(j)}\setminus S_{O}^{(i)}
can appear in the
receiver’s output without having been intended by the sender
(
vocabulary surplus
).
Classical channel coding theory, which treats the source and
reconstruction alphabets as unstructured label sets, is blind to
this distinction: it can detect that a symbol error has occurred, but
cannot determine whether the error represents a genuine loss of
semantic content or a harmless reformulation within the receiver’s
richer (or merely different) vocabulary.

Communication Sub-Scenarios

Three sub-scenarios of increasing structural complexity arise
naturally in the multi-agent setting; they are listed below in
decreasing order of analytical depth in this paper.

Definition 5.1

(Pairwise unicast scenario)
.

Fix a sender–receiver pair
(i,j)
with
i\neq j
.
Agent
i
wishes to communicate its full knowledge base

S_{O}^{(i)}
to agent
j
over a noisy carrier channel

W_{ij}:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}

(Definition
4.7
).
Agent
j
reconstructs a state in its own vocabulary

\hat{S}_{O}:=S_{O}^{(j)}
, using a decoding kernel

D\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}^{(j)})
.
The end-to-end semantic channel is

\mathfrak{C}^{ij}\;=\;\bigl(\,\mathcal{I}^{(i)},\;\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}^{ij},\;\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}^{(j)},\;\kappa_{\text{enc}},\;W_{ij},\;D\,\bigr),

with semantic source space
S_{O}^{(i)}
, reconstructed
space
S_{O}^{(j)}
, and noise pair

S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)},\qquad S_{O}^{+}=S_{O}^{(j)}\setminus S_{O}^{(i)}.

(5.1)

The constituent information models

\mathcal{I}^{(i)}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}^{ij}
, and

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}^{(j)}
are formalized in
Section
V-B
.

The pairwise unicast scenario is the primary focus of
Sections
V-B
–
V-D
.
All new theorems are stated and proved for this case first;
generalizations to the broadcast setting are given as corollaries.

Definition 5.2

(Broadcast scenario)
.

A designated sender (agent
0
) communicates its knowledge base

S_{O}^{(0)}
simultaneously to
K
receivers
(agents
1,\ldots,K
) over a common carrier channel
W
.
Each receiver
j
maintains a distinct vocabulary

\hat{S}_{O}^{(j)}:=S_{O}^{(j)}
and observes a
(possibly receiver-specific) noise pair

(S_{O}^{-,j},\;S_{O}^{+,j})\;=\;\bigl(S_{O}^{(0)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)},\;S_{O}^{(j)}\setminus S_{O}^{(0)}\bigr).

All
K
receivers observe the
same
channel
output
\hat{S}_{C}
; the receiver-specific noise pairs arise
solely from vocabulary mismatch, not from different physical
channel realizations.
The broadcast semantic channel is a family

\{\mathfrak{C}^{0j}\}_{j=1}^{K}
of pairwise channels sharing the
same sender, the same encoding kernel, and the same carrier channel,
but differing in decoding model and noise pair.

The broadcast scenario reveals a phenomenon absent from classical broadcast channel
theory
[
8
]
: even over a
noiseless
carrier
(
W=\mathrm{id}_{S_{C}}
), the achievable fidelity at each receiver is
constrained by its vocabulary overlap with the sender—a purely
semantic bottleneck (see Proposition
5.9

in Section
V-D
).

Remark 5.1

(Relay scenario (future work))
.

A third sub-scenario arises when an intermediate agent
k
acts as
a relay: agent
i
transmits to agent
k
, which performs
inference within
\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(k)})
and then re-encodes and forwards
the result to agent
j
.
This is naturally modeled as a composition of two pairwise channels,

\mathfrak{C}^{ij}_{\mathrm{relay}}=\mathfrak{C}^{kj}\circ\mathfrak{C}^{ik}
,
using the information-model composition machinery of
Definition
3.4
and
Remark
3.5
.
The relay setting raises the question of whether intermediate inference can

change
the effective capacity of the end-to-end
link—a possibility that has no direct classical analogue,
since in classical relay channel
theory
[
8
]
the relay cannot exploit
logical structure to reduce the message set.
A complete treatment requires multi-letter extensions and is deferred
to future work; the pairwise and broadcast results of this section
provide the necessary building blocks.

Key Questions

The heterogeneous multi-agent setting gives rise to four interrelated
design and analysis questions that the classical framework cannot
address.

Q1 (Closure reliability from vocabulary overlap).

Under what conditions on the overlap between
S_{O}^{(i)}
and

S_{O}^{(j)}
can agent
j
reconstruct the
deductive closure

of agent
i
’s knowledge base—i.e., achieve

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)}\bigr)=1

(Definition
3.17
)—even if the literal symbols
differ?
Proposition
3.5
provides necessary and sufficient
conditions in the abstract noise-pair language; the task here is to
translate those conditions into explicit, verifiable predicates on the
knowledge-base pair
(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)})
.

Q2 (Heterogeneous deductive compression).

How many channel uses are needed to communicate the full knowledge base

S_{O}^{(i)}
to agent
j
under closure reliability, and how does
this compare with the Hamming-reliable baseline?
Theorem
4.6
and
Corollary
4.4
(cf.
[
34
,
8
]
) establish the deductive compression
ratio
\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|/\log|S_{O}|
in the homogeneous setting
(
\hat{S}_{O}=S_{O}
); the question is whether the same ratio persists,
improves, or degrades under vocabulary heterogeneity.

Q3 (Invariant diagnosis).

How do the six families of semantic channel invariants catalogued in
Theorem
4.4
—source-side structural
invariants, set-level fidelity, noise-pair indices, structural quality
indices, receiver-side comparison indices, and information-theoretic
invariants—depend on the knowledge-base overlap?
Explicit formulas in terms of the overlap structure would allow a system
designer to predict channel performance
a priori
from
knowledge-base metadata, without computing the full channel kernel.

Q4 (Broadcast bottleneck).

In the broadcast scenario, which receiver determines the minimum
blocklength, and does the cost grow with the number of receivers?
Classical broadcast channel theory identifies the weakest receiver via
the degradation order of the physical channel; the semantic setting
introduces a second axis of “weakness” rooted in vocabulary overlap,
and the interaction between the two axes must be characterized.

Preview of Main Results

The answers to Q1–Q4 are developed in full in
Section
V-D
; an informal summary is given here to
guide the reader.

For
Q1
, the answer has two layers.
At the
set level
, closure fidelity

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)})=1

holds if and only if
(a) every core element of the sender is
derivable from

the receiver’s knowledge base
(
A^{(i)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
), and
(b) every surplus state in the receiver’s vocabulary is
derivable from the sender’s knowledge base
(Proposition
5.3
).
At the
operational
level, the two-layer code of
Theorem
5.1
achieves vanishing
closure error under a stronger but readily verifiable form
of (a): the sender’s core is
literally contained
in
the receiver’s vocabulary
(
A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}
).

For
Q2
, the deductive compression ratio is shown to be

invariant
under vocabulary heterogeneity, provided the
two-condition criterion of Q1 is satisfied
(Theorem
5.2
).
The minimum blocklength for closure-reliable communication remains

n^{*}\approx\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O}^{(i)})|/C(W_{ij})
, identical to the
homogeneous case.
When condition (a) fails—i.e., some sender core element is
not derivable from the receiver’s knowledge base—no code of
any blocklength can achieve
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}=1
, and the
achievable fidelity is bounded by the core preservation ratio
(Corollary
5.3
).

For
Q3
, each invariant is expressed in terms of the
overlap decomposition introduced in
Section
V-B

(Propositions
5.2
–
5.6
).
The set-level invariants (
\rho_{\text{Atom}}
,

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}
,
\Delta\mathsf{A}
,

\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}
) are fully determined by the
knowledge-base pair alone, via the overlap scalars

|A_{-}^{ij}|
,
|S_{+,d}^{ij}|
, and

|S_{+,n}^{ij}|
; the probabilistic indices
(
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
,
\Psi_{+}
,
\mathsf{F}
,

\mathsf{E}
) are
constrained
by these scalars
(e.g.,
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}=0
whenever
A_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
)
but additionally depend on the channel kernel; and the
information-theoretic invariants depend further on the carrier
channel
W_{ij}
.

For
Q4
, the broadcast minimum blocklength is shown to depend
only on the
sender’s
core and the physical channel capacity,
independent of the number of receivers—provided every receiver’s
vocabulary covers the sender’s core
(Theorem
5.3
).
When some receiver violates this coverage condition, it becomes the
broadcast bottleneck not because of channel degradation but because of
vocabulary loss, and no amount of coding can compensate
(Proposition
5.9
).
This “semantic bottleneck” phenomenon has no counterpart in classical
broadcast theory and constitutes the sharpest qualitative distinction
between the two frameworks.

Remark 5.2

(Classical recovery as a special case)
.

When all agents share the same knowledge base
(
S_{O}^{(i)}=S_{O}^{(j)}
for all
i,j
), the noise pair is trivial,
the two-condition criterion is vacuously satisfied, and all results of
this section reduce to the homogeneous theory of
Section
IV-E
(and, in the irredundant case, to
classical Shannon theory via
Corollary
4.3
).
The heterogeneous analysis thus strictly generalizes rather than
replaces the earlier results.

V-B

Formal Model and Standing Assumptions

This subsection formalizes the multi-agent communication scenario
of Section
V-A
within the information model
framework of Sections
III
–
IV
, and
introduces the
overlap decomposition
—the combinatorial
structure through which all semantic channel invariants are
expressed as functions of the sender–receiver knowledge-base pair.

Definition 5.3

(Agent knowledge base)
.

Fix a set of
K+1
agents indexed by

i\in\{0,1,\ldots,K\}
.
Each agent
i
is associated with a finite knowledge base

S_{O}^{(i)}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
that is

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}
-definable in the ambient
structure
\mathfrak{R}
and satisfies
Assumptions
3.4
–
3.5
.
The
irredundant core
and
stored shortcuts
of
agent
i
are

A^{(i)}:=\text{Atom}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)}\bigr),\qquad J^{(i)}:=S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus A^{(i)}.

By Proposition
3.3
,

\text{Cn}\bigl(A^{(i)}\bigr)=\text{Cn}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)}\bigr)
,
the core
A^{(i)}
is irredundant, and

S_{O}^{(i)}\subseteq\text{Cn}\bigl(A^{(i)}\bigr)
.

Assumption 5.1

(Common proof system and ambient universe)
.

All agents share:

(CP1)

the same proof system

(\mathsf{PS},\,T_{\mathsf{PS}},\,\text{Cn})
and inference
fragment
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}

(Assumption
3.3
,
Axiom
3.4
)
;

(CP2)

the same ambient semantic universe
\mathbb{S}_{O}

with its injective encoding
\text{enc}_{O}
and canonical order

(Section
III-D
,
Assumption
3.6
)
;

(CP3)

the same semantic sublanguage
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sem}}

(Assumption
3.2
)
.

The agents differ
only
in the knowledge bases

S_{O}^{(0)},S_{O}^{(1)},\ldots,S_{O}^{(K)}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}

that they store and operate on.

Remark 5.3

(Relaxation to compatible sub-systems)
.

Assumption
5.1
is the strongest form of
proof-system homogeneity: every agent can apply every inference
rule of
\mathsf{PS}
.
A natural relaxation allows each agent
i
to use a
sub-system
\mathsf{PS}^{(i)}\subseteq\mathsf{PS}
with

\text{Cn}^{(i)}(\Gamma)\subseteq\text{Cn}(\Gamma)
for
all
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
.
The results of this section remain valid with
Cn
replaced by
the weakest common sub-system

\text{Cn}^{\cap}:=\bigcap_{i}\text{Cn}^{(i)}
, at the cost of
potentially larger irredundant cores and weaker compression gains.
We do not pursue this relaxation and work throughout with the
common operator
Cn
.

Definition 5.4

(Pairwise overlap decomposition)
.

For a fixed sender–receiver pair
(i,j)
with
i\neq j
,
define the following subsets of
\mathbb{S}_{O}
.

Three-way partition of

S_{O}^{(i)}\cup S_{O}^{(j)}
:

\displaystyle S_{\cap}^{ij}

\displaystyle\;:=\;S_{O}^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{(j)}

\displaystyle\text{(common states)},

(5.2)

\displaystyle S_{-}^{ij}

\displaystyle\;:=\;S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)}

\displaystyle\text{(lost states)},

(5.3)

\displaystyle S_{+}^{ij}

\displaystyle\;:=\;S_{O}^{(j)}\setminus S_{O}^{(i)}

\displaystyle\text{(surplus states)}.

(5.4)

Core partition:

\displaystyle A_{\cap}^{ij}

\displaystyle\;:=\;A^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{(j)}

\displaystyle\text{(preserved core)},

(5.5)

\displaystyle A_{-}^{ij}

\displaystyle\;:=\;A^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)}

\displaystyle\text{(lost core)}.

(5.6)

Surplus stratification:

\displaystyle S_{+,d}^{ij}

\displaystyle\;:=\;S_{+}^{ij}\cap\text{Cn}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\text{(derivable surplus)},

(5.7)

\displaystyle S_{+,n}^{ij}

\displaystyle\;:=\;S_{+}^{ij}\setminus\text{Cn}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\text{(non-derivable surplus)}.

(5.8)

Proposition 5.1

(Overlap partition properties)
.

The overlap decomposition of
Definition
5.4
satisfies:

(i)

Three-way disjoint union:

S_{O}^{(i)}\cup S_{O}^{(j)}=S_{-}^{ij}\;\dot{\cup}\;S_{\cap}^{ij}\;\dot{\cup}\;S_{+}^{ij}
.

(ii)

Sender decomposition:

S_{O}^{(i)}=S_{\cap}^{ij}\;\dot{\cup}\;S_{-}^{ij}
.

(iii)

Receiver decomposition:

S_{O}^{(j)}=S_{\cap}^{ij}\;\dot{\cup}\;S_{+}^{ij}
.

(iv)

Core partition:

A^{(i)}=A_{\cap}^{ij}\;\dot{\cup}\;A_{-}^{ij}
.

(v)

Surplus partition:

S_{+}^{ij}=S_{+,d}^{ij}\;\dot{\cup}\;S_{+,n}^{ij}
.

(vi)

Noise-pair consistency:

S_{-}^{ij}=S_{O}^{-}
and
S_{+}^{ij}=S_{O}^{+}
,
where
(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})
is the end-to-end noise
pair of
Definition
5.1
.

(vii)

Core loss refines state loss:

A_{-}^{ij}\subseteq S_{-}^{ij}
.

(viii)

Computability:

All seven sets and their cardinalities are computable
from the finite knowledge bases

S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)}
under
Axiom
3.4
.

Parts (i)–(iii) are standard set partition
identities:
S_{\cap}^{ij}
,
S_{-}^{ij}
,
S_{+}^{ij}

are pairwise disjoint by construction, and their union equals

S_{O}^{(i)}\cup S_{O}^{(j)}
; restricting to
S_{O}^{(i)}

yields (ii), and to
S_{O}^{(j)}
yields (iii).

Part (iv): since
A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}
,

A^{(i)}=(A^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{(j)})\;\dot{\cup}\;(A^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)})=A_{\cap}^{ij}\;\dot{\cup}\;A_{-}^{ij}
.

Part (v): immediate from the definition of

S_{+,d}^{ij}
and
S_{+,n}^{ij}
as complementary
subsets of
S_{+}^{ij}
.

Part (vi): comparing
(
5.3
)–(
5.4
) with
(
5.1
) gives the identification
directly.

Part (vii):
A_{-}^{ij}=A^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)}=S_{-}^{ij}
, since
A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}
.

Part (viii): since
S_{O}^{(i)}
and
S_{O}^{(j)}

are finite and effectively listable
(Assumption
3.4
), membership is
decidable by exhaustive comparison.
The sets
S_{\cap}^{ij}
,
S_{-}^{ij}
,
S_{+}^{ij}
,

A_{\cap}^{ij}
,
A_{-}^{ij}
are then computable by
enumeration.
The surplus stratification requires testing

s\in\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
for each
s\in S_{+}^{ij}
, which
is decidable by iterating
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
from

S_{O}^{(i)}
until stabilization
(Axiom
3.4
(IC2)–(IC4)).
∎

Remark 5.4

(Core preservation is tested against

S_{O}^{(j)}
, not against
A^{(j)}
)
.

The preserved core

A_{\cap}^{ij}=A^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{(j)}
tests whether each
sender core element is
present in the receiver’s
vocabulary
—that is, an element of
S_{O}^{(j)}
—not
whether it belongs to the receiver’s irredundant core

A^{(j)}
.
A sender core element
a\in A^{(i)}
may appear in

S_{O}^{(j)}
as a
redundant
stored shortcut
(i.e.,
a\in J^{(j)}
); it is still counted as preserved,
because the receiver can produce it as a decoding output
regardless of its redundancy status in
S_{O}^{(j)}
.

Remark 5.5

(Asymmetry of the overlap decomposition)
.

The decomposition of
Definition
5.4
depends on which
agent is designated as sender and which as receiver.
Swapping the roles yields the
transposed
decomposition:

S_{\cap}^{ji}=S_{\cap}^{ij}
,

S_{-}^{ji}=S_{+}^{ij}
,

S_{+}^{ji}=S_{-}^{ij}
,

A_{\cap}^{ji}=A^{(j)}\cap S_{O}^{(i)}
,

A_{-}^{ji}=A^{(j)}\setminus S_{O}^{(i)}
.
In particular, the core partition

(A_{\cap}^{ij},A_{-}^{ij})
and the surplus
stratification
(S_{+,d}^{ij},S_{+,n}^{ij})

are generally
not
symmetric in
(i,j)
.

Remark 5.6

(Key scalar summaries of the overlap)
.

Although the overlap decomposition produces seven subsets, the

set-level
conditions governing closure fidelity

(Proposition
5.3
)
reduce to two
binary tests:

|A_{-}^{ij}|=0
(no core loss) and

|S_{+,n}^{ij}|=0
(no non-derivable surplus).
The
coding-theoretic
results of
Section
V-D
(blocklength, compression
ratio) depend additionally on
|A^{(i)}|
and
C(W_{ij})
,
while the
probabilistic
indices
(
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
,
\Psi_{+}
,
\mathsf{F}
,

\mathsf{E}
) depend further on the channel kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}
.
The remaining overlap cardinalities are related by simple
accounting:

|A_{\cap}^{ij}|=|A^{(i)}|-|A_{-}^{ij}|
,

|S_{+}^{ij}|=|S_{+,d}^{ij}|+|S_{+,n}^{ij}|
;
the quantities
|S_{-}^{ij}|
,
|S_{\cap}^{ij}|
, and

|S_{O}^{(j)}|
are mutually determined once any one of them
is known, via

|S_{\cap}^{ij}|=|S_{O}^{(i)}|-|S_{-}^{ij}|
and

|S_{O}^{(j)}|=|S_{\cap}^{ij}|+|S_{+}^{ij}|
.

Remark 5.7

(Broadcast overlap decomposition)
.

In the broadcast scenario
(Definition
5.2
), the sender is
agent
0
and the receivers are agents
1,\ldots,K
.
For each receiver
j
, the overlap decomposition

(Definition
5.4
)
is
applied to the pair
(0,j)
, yielding receiver-specific
quantities

A_{\cap}^{0j}
,
A_{-}^{0j}
,

S_{+,d}^{0j}
,
S_{+,n}^{0j}
, etc.
The
broadcast core coverage condition
—that

A_{-}^{0j}=\varnothing
for every

j\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
—plays a central role in
Theorem
5.3
and
Proposition
5.9
.

Definition 5.5

(Heterogeneous semantic channel (formal))
.

For a sender–receiver pair
(i,j)
with
i\neq j
, the

heterogeneous semantic channel
is the semantic channel

(Definition
4.10
)

\mathfrak{C}^{ij}\;=\;\bigl(\,\mathcal{I}^{(i)},\;\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}^{ij},\;\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}^{(j)},\;\kappa_{\text{enc}},\;W_{ij},\;D\,\bigr),

where the constituent models are defined as follows.

(i)

Sender information model.

\mathcal{I}^{(i)}=\langle O^{(i)},T_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(i)},C,T_{C},S_{C},R_{\mathcal{E}}^{(i)}\rangle

is an information model

(Definition
3.3
)
with
semantic state set
S_{O}^{(i)}
, carrier state set

S_{C}
, and enabling map

\mathcal{E}^{(i)}:S_{O}^{(i)}\Rightarrow S_{C}
.

(ii)

Carrier channel model.

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ch}}^{ij}
is a carrier channel
information model

(Definition
4.7
)

with input
S_{C}
, output
\hat{S}_{C}
, and carrier
channel kernel

W_{ij}:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}
.

(iii)

Receiver decoding model.

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}^{(j)}=\langle\hat{C},T_{\hat{C}},\hat{S}_{C},\hat{O}^{(j)},T_{\hat{O}}^{(j)},S_{O}^{(j)},R_{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{dec},(j)}\rangle

is a decoding information model

(Definition
4.8
)
with
reconstructed space

\hat{S}_{O}:=S_{O}^{(j)}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
and
enabling map

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}^{(j)}:\hat{S}_{C}\Rightarrow S_{O}^{(j)}
.

(iv)

Kernels.

\kappa_{\text{enc}}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}^{(i)})

is the encoding kernel and

D\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dec}}^{(j)})

is the decoding kernel.

The
end-to-end kernel
is

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}\;:=\;D\circ W_{ij}\circ\kappa_{\text{enc}}\;:\;S_{O}^{(i)}\rightsquigarrow S_{O}^{(j)},

(5.9)

and the
end-to-end noise pair
is

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})=(S_{-}^{ij},S_{+}^{ij})

(Proposition
5.1
(vi))
.

Remark 5.8

(Inherited proof-system structure at the receiver)
.

Since
S_{O}^{(j)}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{O}
and the proof system

\mathsf{PS}
acts on all of
\mathbb{S}_{O}

(Assumption
5.1
)
, the receiver
inherits the deductive closure
\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
, the
irredundant core
A^{(j)}=\text{Atom}(S_{O}^{(j)})
, and the
derivation-depth stratification

\text{Dd}(\cdot\mid A^{(j)})
.
The semantic invariants

\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{I}^{(j)})=|A^{(j)}|
and

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I}^{(j)})=\max_{q\in S_{O}^{(j)}}\text{Dd}(q\mid A^{(j)})

are therefore well-defined and computable

(Theorem
3.1
)
.
In general,
A^{(j)}\neq A^{(i)}
,

\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})\neq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
, and

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I}^{(j)})\neq\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I}^{(i)})
;
the overlap decomposition

(Definition
5.4
)
quantifies
each of these discrepancies.

Assumption 5.2

(Full enabling (heterogeneous setting))
.

For the heterogeneous semantic channel

\mathfrak{C}^{ij}
of
Definition
5.5
:

(FE1)

Full encoding enabling:

\mathcal{E}^{(i)}(s_{o})=S_{C}
for every

s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(i)}
.

(FE2)

Full decoding enabling:

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{dec}}^{(j)}(\hat{s}_{c})=S_{O}^{(j)}

for every
\hat{s}_{c}\in\hat{S}_{C}
.

Remark 5.9

(Role of the full enabling assumption)
.

Under Assumption
5.2
, the encoder
can map any semantic state to any carrier symbol, and the
decoder can produce any state in the receiver’s vocabulary from
any received carrier symbol.
All vocabulary-mismatch effects are then captured

entirely
by the noise pair

(S_{-}^{ij},S_{+}^{ij})
; no additional bottleneck arises
from the enabling structure.
This is the heterogeneous counterpart of the full enabling
condition in
Theorem
4.1
(iii)(a)–(b)
.
When the enabling is constrained (e.g., the encoder can access
only a subset of
S_{C}
for each
s_{o}
), additional capacity
reductions follow from
Proposition
4.13
(ii)
; such
cases are noted where relevant but not analyzed in full
generality.

Remark 5.10

(Carrier alphabet size requirement)
.

The block coding achievability results of
Theorem
4.6
do
not
require a
single-letter alphabet-size comparison between
|S_{O}^{(i)}|

and
|S_{C}|
, since block codes operate over the product
alphabet
S_{C}^{n}
.
However, the
capacity equality

C_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}(W_{ij})=C(W_{ij})

(Proposition
5.7
(ii))
invokes
Theorem
4.1
(iii)
, which requires

|S_{O}^{(i)}|\leq|S_{C}|
and
|S_{O}^{(j)}|\leq|\hat{S}_{C}|
.
Throughout Sections
V-C
–
V-D
,
we assume
|S_{C}|\geq\max_{i}|S_{O}^{(i)}|
and

|\hat{S}_{C}|\geq|S_{C}|
, which is the natural setting when the
physical carrier is designed to accommodate the largest agent
vocabulary.
These conditions ensure both the capacity equality and the
practical convenience of single-letter encoding when desired.

Assumption 5.3

(Standing assumptions for
Section
V
)
.

Throughout
Sections
V-B
–
V-D
,
the following conditions are in force unless explicitly stated
otherwise:

(SA1)

all standing assumptions of
Sections
III
–
IV
, including
Assumptions
3.1
,

3.2
,

3.3
,

3.4
,

3.5
,
Axiom
3.4
, and
Assumption
3.6
;

(SA2)

the common proof system assumption

(Assumption
5.1
)
;

(SA3)

the full enabling assumption

(Assumption
5.2
)
;

(SA4)

the carrier channel satisfies
C(W_{ij})>0
and the
carrier alphabet sizes satisfy

|S_{C}|\geq\max_{i}|S_{O}^{(i)}|
and

|\hat{S}_{C}|\geq|S_{C}|
;

(SA5)

the deductive independence of core elements

(Assumption
4.1
)
holds
for the sender’s knowledge base
S_{O}^{(i)}
when
converse bounds are invoked.

Table
I
collects the notation introduced
in this subsection for convenient reference throughout
Section
V
.

TABLE I:
Notation summary for
Section
V
.
All quantities are defined relative to a fixed
sender–receiver pair
(i,j)
.

Meaning

Knowledge base (semantic state set) of agent
i

i

S_{O}^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{(j)}

S_{-}^{ij}

S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)}

(\,=S_{O}^{-})

S_{+}^{ij}

Surplus states:

S_{O}^{(j)}\setminus S_{O}^{(i)}

(\,=S_{O}^{+})

A_{\cap}^{ij}

A^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{(j)}

A_{-}^{ij}

Lost sender core:

A^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)}

S_{+,d}^{ij}

S_{+}^{ij}\cap\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})

S_{+,n}^{ij}

S_{+}^{ij}\setminus\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})

End-to-end semantic kernel for pair
(i,j)

V-C

Instantiation of Semantic Channel Invariants

This subsection applies the invariant machinery of
Section
IV
to the heterogeneous pair
(i,j)
,
expressing each invariant of
Theorem
4.4
as a function of the
overlap decomposition of Section
V-B
.
Throughout, we fix a sender–receiver pair
(i,j)
with the
heterogeneous semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}^{ij}
of
Definition
5.5
, under the standing
assumptions of Assumption
5.3
.

Proposition 5.2

(Set-level invariants from overlap)
.

For the heterogeneous semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}^{ij}

with noise pair

(S_{O}^{-},S_{O}^{+})=(S_{-}^{ij},S_{+}^{ij})

(Proposition
5.1
(vi))
:

(i)

Preserved region:

\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}=S_{O}^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{(j)}=S_{\cap}^{ij}
.

(ii)

Core preservation ratio:

\rho_{\text{Atom}}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)}\bigr)\;=\;\frac{|A_{\cap}^{ij}|}{|A^{(i)}|}\;=\;1-\frac{|A_{-}^{ij}|}{|A^{(i)}|}.

(5.10)

In particular,
\rho_{\text{Atom}}=1
if and only if

A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
.

(iii)

Spurious derivability:

S_{O}^{+}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
if and only if

S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing
.

(i):
S_{O}^{-}=S_{-}^{ij}

(Proposition
5.1
(vi)), so

\tilde{S}_{O}^{\cap}=S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{-}=S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus(S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus S_{O}^{(j)})=S_{O}^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{(j)}=S_{\cap}^{ij}
.

(ii): By Definition
3.18
,

\rho_{\text{Atom}}=|A^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{(j)}|/|A^{(i)}|=|A_{\cap}^{ij}|/|A^{(i)}|
.
By Proposition
5.1
(iv),

|A_{\cap}^{ij}|=|A^{(i)}|-|A_{-}^{ij}|
.

(iii):
S_{O}^{+}=S_{+}^{ij}
and

S_{+}^{ij}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})

iff

S_{+}^{ij}\setminus\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})=\varnothing

iff
S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing
.
∎

Proposition 5.3

(Closure fidelity: necessary and sufficient
conditions)
.

For the sender–receiver pair
(i,j)
,

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)},\,S_{O}^{(j)}\bigr)=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\text{Cn}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)}\bigr)=\text{Cn}\bigl(S_{O}^{(j)}\bigr),

and this holds if and only if both of the following conditions
are satisfied:

(F1)

Sender core derivable from receiver:

A^{(i)}\subseteq\text{Cn}\bigl(S_{O}^{(j)}\bigr)
.

(F2)

No non-derivable surplus:

S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing

(
equivalently,

S_{+}^{ij}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
)
.

The first equivalence is the definition of

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}

(Definition
3.17
):

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}=1
iff the Jaccard index of the two
closures equals
1
, i.e., the closures coincide.
It remains to show

\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
iff
(F1)+(F2).

Sufficiency.

From (F1):

A^{(i)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
.
By monotonicity (Cn2),

\text{Cn}(A^{(i)})\subseteq\text{Cn}(\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)}))=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})

(idempotence (Cn3)). This chain of inclusions is a standard consequence of
the Tarski closure axioms; see,
e.g.,
[
37
]
.
Since

\text{Cn}(A^{(i)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})

(Proposition
3.3
(i)),

\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
.

From (F2):

S_{+}^{ij}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
.
Since

S_{\cap}^{ij}\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})

(reflexivity (Cn1)),

S_{O}^{(j)}=S_{\cap}^{ij}\cup S_{+}^{ij}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
.
By monotonicity and idempotence,

\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
.

Combining the two inclusions yields

\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
.

Necessity.

Suppose
\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
.

For (F1):

A^{(i)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(A^{(i)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
.

For (F2):

S_{+}^{ij}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
,
so
S_{+,n}^{ij}=S_{+}^{ij}\setminus\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})=\varnothing
.
∎

Remark 5.11

(Strong vs. weak core coverage)
.

Condition
(F1)
requires that each sender core element
be
derivable from
the receiver’s knowledge base; it
does
not
require the element to be literally present
in
S_{O}^{(j)}
.
A strictly stronger condition is

(F1
′
)

A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
, i.e.,

A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}
.

Condition
(F1
′
)
implies
(F1)
(since

S_{O}^{(j)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
) but not conversely:
a core element
a\in A_{-}^{ij}
may satisfy

a\in\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
even though
a\notin S_{O}^{(j)}
.

For
set-level
closure fidelity, the weak
condition
(F1)
is both necessary and sufficient

(Proposition
5.3
)
.
For
operational
closure reliability via the two-layer
code of Theorem
4.6
(ii)
, the
decoder outputs core elements directly and therefore requires
the strong condition
(F1
′
)
so that

A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}=\hat{S}_{O}
.
When only
(F1)
holds with

A_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
, a more sophisticated decoding
strategy is needed; this is addressed in
Section
V-D
.
Throughout the remainder of this subsection, results are stated
under whichever version is required, with the distinction noted
explicitly.

Corollary 5.1

(Sufficient condition via overlap scalars)
.

If
A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
and

S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing
, then

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)})=1
.
This is the instantiation of
Proposition
3.5
(iii)
in the
overlap language and the condition used in the achievability
results of Section
V-D
.

A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
gives

A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
, so
(F1) holds.

S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing
is (F2).
Apply Proposition
5.3
.
∎

Proposition 5.4

(Noise-pair probabilistic indices from
overlap)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}^{ij}
be a heterogeneous semantic channel
with kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}:S_{O}^{(i)}\rightsquigarrow S_{O}^{(j)}
.

(i)

Core preservation index:

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij})\;=\;\begin{cases}\displaystyle\min_{a\in A^{(i)}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}(a\mid a)&\text{if }A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing,\\[6.0pt]
0&\text{if }A_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing.\end{cases}

(5.11)

(ii)

Spurious probability index:

\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij})\;=\;\max_{s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(i)}}\;\sum_{\hat{s}_{o}\in S_{+}^{ij}}\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o}).

(5.12)

In particular,
\Psi_{+}=0
whenever

S_{+}^{ij}=\varnothing

(
i.e.,
S_{O}^{(j)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}
)
.

(iii)

Noiseless deterministic case:

If
W_{ij}=\mathrm{id}_{S_{C}}
and both

\kappa_{\text{enc}}
and
D
are deterministic with
induced end-to-end function

f:=D\circ\mathrm{id}\circ\kappa_{\text{enc}}:S_{O}^{(i)}\to S_{O}^{(j)}
, then

\displaystyle\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing\\
&\text{ and }f(a)=a\;\forall a\in A^{(i)},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(5.13)

\displaystyle\Psi_{+}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }f(S_{O}^{(i)})\subseteq S_{\cap}^{ij},\\
1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(5.14)

(i): By Proposition
5.1
(vi),

A^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{-}=A^{(i)}\cap S_{-}^{ij}=A_{-}^{ij}
.
When
A_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
,
Definition
4.25
sets

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}:=0
.
When
A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
,

A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}=\tilde{S}_{O}
, so

\pi(a)=\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}(a\mid a)
is
well-defined for every
a\in A^{(i)}
and

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}=\min_{a}\pi(a)
.

(ii): Direct from Definition
4.26
with

S_{O}^{+}=S_{+}^{ij}
.
When
S_{+}^{ij}=\varnothing
, the sum is empty and

\Psi_{+}=0
.

(iii): When
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}
is deterministic,

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}(\hat{s}_{o}\mid s_{o})\in\{0,1\}
for all
s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}
.
For
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
:

\pi(a)=\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}(a\mid a)=1
iff

f(a)=a
, and this must hold for all
a\in A^{(i)}

(which requires
a\in S_{O}^{(j)}
, i.e.,

A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
).
For
\Psi_{+}
:

p_{+}(s_{o})=\mathbf{1}[f(s_{o})\in S_{+}^{ij}]
, which
is
0
for all
s_{o}
iff

f(S_{O}^{(i)})\subseteq S_{\cap}^{ij}
, and
1
for some

s_{o}
otherwise; the maximum is therefore
0
or
1
.
∎

Remark 5.12

(Kernel dependence of probabilistic indices)
.

Unlike the set-level invariants

\rho_{\text{Atom}}
and
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}
, which depend only
on the knowledge-base pair

(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)})
, the probabilistic indices

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
and
\Psi_{+}
depend on the channel
kernel
\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}
and hence on the
specific encoder, physical channel, and decoder.
The overlap decomposition constrains the
range
of these
indices—
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}=0
whenever

A_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
, and

\Psi_{+}=0
whenever

S_{+}^{ij}=\varnothing
—but their precise values within
the feasible range are determined by the kernel.

Proposition 5.5

(Structural quality indices from overlap)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}^{ij}
be a heterogeneous semantic channel
with kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}:S_{O}^{(i)}\rightsquigarrow S_{O}^{(j)}

and let
A=A^{(i)}
.

(i)

Fidelity concentration under core-preserving
overlap:

If
A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
and

S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing
, then by
Corollary
4.2
,

\mathsf{F}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij})\;=\;1-\max_{a\in A^{(i)}}\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(a\mid\mathfrak{C}^{ij}).

(5.15)

That is, the worst-case closure distortion is attained
at a sender core element; redundant states contribute
zero.

(ii)

Depth expansion under vocabulary match:

If
S_{-}^{ij}=S_{+}^{ij}=\varnothing

(
i.e.,
S_{O}^{(i)}=S_{O}^{(j)}
)
,
then every reachable
\hat{s}_{o}\in S_{O}^{(j)}=S_{O}^{(i)}

lies in
\text{Cn}(A)
and the depth distortion reduces to
its first branch (
4.19
).
In particular,

\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij})=0
iff the kernel
preserves the derivation depth of every state

(Proposition
4.10
(iii))
.

(i): By Corollary
5.1
,

\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
and

A^{(i)}\cap S_{O}^{-}=A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
.
Proposition
4.9
(i) gives

d_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o},\hat{s}_{o}\mid S_{O}^{(i)})=0
for every

s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus A
and

\hat{s}_{o}\in S_{O}^{(j)}
.
Hence

\max_{s_{o}\in S_{O}^{(i)}}\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(s_{o}\mid\mathfrak{C}^{ij})=\max_{a\in A}\bar{d}_{\text{Cn}}(a\mid\mathfrak{C}^{ij})
,
and the conclusion follows from
Definition
4.23
.

(ii): When
S_{O}^{(i)}=S_{O}^{(j)}
, the noise pair is trivial and
every
\hat{s}_{o}\in S_{O}^{(j)}=S_{O}^{(i)}
satisfies

\hat{s}_{o}\in\text{Cn}(A)

(Proposition
3.3
(iv)).
The claim follows from
Proposition
4.9
(ii) and
Proposition
4.10
(iii).
∎

Proposition 5.6

(Receiver-side structural comparison from
overlap)
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}^{ij}
be a heterogeneous semantic channel
with
\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}^{(j)}
.

(i)

Atomicity shift:

\Delta\mathsf{A}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij})=|A^{(j)}|-|A^{(i)}|
.

(ii)

Depth shift:

\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij})=\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I}^{(j)})-\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I}^{(i)})
.

(iii)

Under core-preserving conditions
(
A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
,

S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing
):

(a)

\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})

(Corollary
5.1
)
.

(b)

|A^{(j)}|\leq|A^{(i)}|
, i.e.,

\Delta\mathsf{A}\leq 0

(Proposition
4.12
(ii))
.
The inequality is strict when some element of

A^{(i)}
becomes redundant in
S_{O}^{(j)}

due to the presence of derivable surplus
states
S_{+,d}^{ij}
.

(c)

Equality

\Delta\mathsf{A}=0
holds when

S_{+,d}^{ij}=\varnothing
and

S_{-}^{ij}\cap J^{(i)}=S_{-}^{ij}

(
only non-core states are
lost
)
, because then

S_{O}^{(j)}=A^{(i)}\cup(J^{(i)}\setminus S_{-}^{ij})
and

A^{(i)}
remains irredundant
in
S_{O}^{(j)}
.

(iv)

Trivial noise pair
(
S_{O}^{(i)}=S_{O}^{(j)}
):

\Delta\mathsf{A}=0
and

\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}=0

(Proposition
4.12
(i))
.

Parts (i) and (ii) are direct from
Definition
4.27
with

\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}^{(j)}
.

(iii)(a):
Corollary
5.1
.

(iii)(b):
By Proposition
4.12
(ii), applied
with
A=A^{(i)}
,
S_{O}^{-}=S_{-}^{ij}
,

S_{O}^{+}=S_{+}^{ij}
.
When
S_{+,d}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
, some

d\in S_{+,d}^{ij}\subseteq\text{Cn}(A^{(i)})
may make a
previously irredundant
a\in A^{(i)}
redundant
in
S_{O}^{(j)}
(if

a\in\text{Cn}\bigl((S_{O}^{(j)}\setminus\{a\})\bigr)
due to
the presence of
d
).

(iii)(c): When
S_{+,d}^{ij}=S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing
, we have

S_{+}^{ij}=\varnothing
and

S_{O}^{(j)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}
.
Since
A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
,

A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}
.
Since
S_{-}^{ij}\subseteq J^{(i)}
,

S_{O}^{(j)}=A^{(i)}\cup(J^{(i)}\setminus S_{-}^{ij})
.

We show
\text{Atom}(S_{O}^{(j)})=A^{(i)}
by a simultaneous induction on
the canonical order of
S_{O}^{(j)}
.
Let
s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{m}
be the elements of
S_{O}^{(j)}
in
canonical order, and let
B_{k}
denote the current set after
the irredundantization procedure
(Definition
3.8
) has scanned

s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}
.

Inductive claim.

After scanning
s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}
:
(a) every
a\in A^{(i)}
with
a\leq s_{k}
has
survived (remains in
B_{k}
); and
(b) every
j^{\prime}\in J^{(i)}\setminus S_{-}^{ij}
with

j^{\prime}\leq s_{k}
has been removed.

The base case
k=0
is vacuous.
For the inductive step, suppose the claim holds through
s_{k}

and consider
s_{k+1}
.

Case 1:
s_{k+1}=a\in A^{(i)}
.

By the inductive hypothesis, every element of

J^{(i)}\setminus S_{-}^{ij}
preceding
a
has been
removed, and every element of
A^{(i)}
preceding
a
has
survived.
Hence the current set satisfies

B_{k}\setminus\{a\}\;=\;\bigl(A^{(i)}\cap\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\}\bigr)\;\cup\;\{s_{k+2},\ldots,s_{m}\}.

In the irredundantization of
S_{O}^{(i)}
, when
a
was
scanned, the current set
B^{(i)}
had precisely the same
structure—surviving core elements before
a
plus all
elements of
S_{O}^{(i)}
after
a
—because elements of

J^{(i)}
before
a
were likewise removed at their own
scan steps (they are in
J^{(i)}
by definition).
Since
\{s_{k+2},\ldots,s_{m}\}\subseteq\{\text{elements of }S_{O}^{(i)}\text{ after }a\}

(the former is a subset, possibly missing elements
of
S_{-}^{ij}
after
a
),

B_{k}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq B^{(i)}\setminus\{a\}
.
By monotonicity (Cn2),

\text{Cn}(B_{k}\setminus\{a\})\subseteq\text{Cn}(B^{(i)}\setminus\{a\})
.
Since
a
survived in the irredundantization of
S_{O}^{(i)}
,

a\notin\text{Cn}(B^{(i)}\setminus\{a\})
, hence

a\notin\text{Cn}(B_{k}\setminus\{a\})
, and
a
survives.

Case 2:
s_{k+1}=j^{\prime}\in J^{(i)}\setminus S_{-}^{ij}
.

By the inductive hypothesis,

A^{(i)}\cap\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\}\subseteq B_{k}
, and all
elements of
A^{(i)}
after
s_{k}
are in
B_{k}

(not yet scanned).
Hence
A^{(i)}\subseteq B_{k}\setminus\{j^{\prime}\}

(noting
j^{\prime}\notin A^{(i)}
).
By monotonicity,

\text{Cn}(A^{(i)})\subseteq\text{Cn}(B_{k}\setminus\{j^{\prime}\})
.
Since

j^{\prime}\in J^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(A^{(i)})

(Proposition
3.3
(iv)),

j^{\prime}\in\text{Cn}(B_{k}\setminus\{j^{\prime}\})
, and
j^{\prime}
is removed.

By induction, the output of the irredundantization
of
S_{O}^{(j)}
retains exactly
A^{(i)}
.
For the reverse inclusion

\text{Atom}(S_{O}^{(j)})\subseteq A^{(i)}
:
suppose for contradiction that

b\in\text{Atom}(S_{O}^{(j)})\setminus A^{(i)}
.
Then
b\in J^{(i)}\setminus S_{-}^{ij}
, but the induction
shows that every such element is removed—a contradiction.
Hence
\text{Atom}(S_{O}^{(j)})=A^{(i)}
and

\Delta\mathsf{A}=0
.

(iv):
Immediate from
Proposition
4.12
(i).
∎

Corollary 5.2

(Heterogeneous semantic Fano bound)
.

Let
P_{O}\in\Delta(S_{O}^{(i)})
be full-support and let

\epsilon:=\bar{d}_{H}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij},P_{O})
.
Then

I_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}(P_{O},\mathfrak{C}^{ij})\;\geq\;H(\mathsf{S}_{o})-h_{b}(\epsilon)-\epsilon\log\bigl(|S_{O}^{(j)}|-1\bigr),

(5.16)

where
h_{b}
is the binary entropy.
Moreover, by
Proposition
4.11
(v)
,

\epsilon\;\leq\;1-\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij})\cdot P_{O}(A^{(i)}).

(5.17)

Consequently, when
A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
and

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C}^{ij})
is close to
1

(
e.g., because the carrier channel is
reliable
)
, the right-hand side
of (
5.16
) is close to

H(\mathsf{S}_{o})
, i.e., nearly all source entropy is
transmitted.
When
A_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
,

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}=0
by (
5.11
) and the bound
yields only the trivial lower bound

I_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}\geq 0
.

Equation (
5.16
) is
Theorem
4.3
(i) applied with

\tilde{S}_{O}=S_{O}^{(j)}
.
Equation (
5.17
) is
Proposition
4.11
(v).
When
A_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
,

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}=0
gives
\epsilon\leq 1
, so

h_{b}(\epsilon)+\epsilon\log(|S_{O}^{(j)}|-1)\leq\log|S_{O}^{(j)}|

and the lower bound cannot exceed zero in a nontrivial way.
∎

Proposition 5.7

(Semantic capacity from overlap)
.

Let
W_{ij}:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}
be the carrier
channel kernel for the pair
(i,j)
.
Under the full enabling assumption

(Assumption
5.2
)
and
the carrier size conditions of
Remark
5.10
:

(i)

Data processing chain:

I_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}\leq C_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}(W_{ij})\leq C(W_{ij})
.

(ii)

Capacity equality under full enabling:

C_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}(W_{ij})=C(W_{ij})
.

(iii)

Source entropy bound:

I_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}\leq\min\bigl(\log|S_{O}^{(i)}|,\;\log|S_{O}^{(j)}|\bigr)
.

Part (i) is
Theorem
4.1
(i).
Part (ii) follows from
Theorem
4.1
(iii): the full enabling
assumption ensures conditions (a)–(b) of that theorem, and
the carrier size conditions ensure

|S_{O}^{(i)}|\leq|S_{C}|
and

|S_{O}^{(j)}|\leq|\hat{S}_{C}|
.
Part (iii) follows from

I(\mathsf{S}_{o};\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o})\leq\min(H(\mathsf{S}_{o}),\,H(\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{o}))
.
∎

Remark 5.13

(Summary: invariant families and overlap
dependence)
.

Table
II
collects the six invariant
families of Theorem
4.4
, their
expressions in the overlap language, and the data they depend
on beyond the knowledge-base pair.

The set-level invariants (
\rho_{\text{Atom}}
,

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}
) and the structural comparison indices
(
\Delta\mathsf{A}
,
\Delta\mathsf{D_{d}}
) are

fully determined
by the knowledge-base pair

(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)})
and the proof
system
\mathsf{PS}
; they do not depend on the channel
kernel.
The noise-pair indices (
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
,
\Psi_{+}
) and
the structural quality indices (
\mathsf{F}
,

\mathsf{E}
) are constrained by the overlap structure
(e.g.,
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}=0
whenever

A_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
) but additionally depend on the
channel kernel.
The information-theoretic invariants
(
I_{\mathrm{sem}}
,
C_{\mathrm{sem}}
,
C(W)
) depend
on the kernel and the carrier channel; the overlap structure
enters only through the enabling constraints and the output
alphabet size
|S_{O}^{(j)}|
.

language of

5.4
.

alone; “+kernel” indicates additional dependence on the
channel kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}
.

)

; else
