Remark 5.14

(Diagnostic use of the overlap–invariant
correspondence)
.

The correspondence in
Table
II
enables a two-stage
diagnostic workflow for heterogeneous semantic channel design:

Stage 1 (offline, kernel-free).

Given the knowledge-base pair
(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)})
,
compute the overlap decomposition

(Definition
5.4
)
and
evaluate the binary feasibility tests

A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
and

S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing
.
If both hold, set-level closure fidelity

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}=1
is guaranteed

(Corollary
5.1
)
, and the
two-layer code of Section
V-D
is directly
applicable.
If
S_{+,n}^{ij}\neq\varnothing

(
condition

(F2)
of Proposition
5.3

fails
)
, then
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}<1
is unavoidable.
If
A_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
but

A^{(i)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})

(
the weak condition
(F1)
holds despite

(F1
′
)
failing
)
, then

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}=1
remains possible but the standard
two-layer code does not apply; see
Remark
5.11
and
Remark
5.16
in
Section
V-D
.
If
A^{(i)}\not\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
, then

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}<1
is unavoidable

(Proposition
5.3
)
, and the
designer must augment the receiver’s vocabulary or accept
degraded fidelity (cf. the minimum-vocabulary criterion of
Proposition
5.8
).

Stage 2 (online, kernel-dependent).

Given a specific channel kernel

\kappa_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}
, compute

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
,
\Psi_{+}
,
\mathsf{F}
, and

\mathsf{E}
to quantify the probabilistic quality of the
channel.
The Fano bound

(Corollary
5.2
)
then
translates the probabilistic indices into a lower bound on

I_{\mathrm{sem}}^{ij}
, completing the link between
knowledge-base structure and information throughput.

V-D

Main Results: Heterogeneous Compression and Broadcast

This subsection derives the principal new results of the paper.
Part 1 addresses the pairwise unicast scenario
(Definition
5.1
):
closure-reliable achievability, a heterogeneous deductive
compression theorem, an impossibility result when core coverage
fails, and a vocabulary design criterion.
Part 2 extends the theory to the broadcast scenario
(Definition
5.2
).
Throughout, the standing assumptions of
Assumption
5.3
are in force.

Part 1: Pairwise Heterogeneous Communication

Theorem 5.1

(Closure reliability for a heterogeneous pair)
.

Let
(i,j)
be a sender–receiver pair with heterogeneous
semantic channel
\mathfrak{C}^{ij}

(Definition
5.5
)
,
carrier channel kernel

W_{ij}:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}
with
C(W_{ij})>0
,
and overlap decomposition

(Definition
5.4
)
.
Assume:

(H1)

A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing

(
the sender’s irredundant core is contained in
the receiver’s vocabulary:

A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}
)
;

(H2)

S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing

(
all surplus states in the receiver’s vocabulary
are derivable from the sender’s knowledge base:

S_{+}^{ij}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
)
.

Then:

(i)

Set-level closure fidelity:

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)})=1

(Corollary
5.1
)
.

(ii)

Achievability:

There exists a sequence of

(n,|S_{O}^{(i)}|)
semantic block codes

(Definition
4.28
)

with message set
\mathcal{M}=S_{O}^{(i)}
,
encoding into
S_{C}^{n}
, decoding into

\hat{S}_{O}=S_{O}^{(j)}
, and

P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n)}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, provided

\frac{\log|A^{(i)}|}{n}\;<\;C(W_{ij}).

(5.18)

(iii)

Converse
(under
Assumption
4.1

for
S_{O}^{(i)}
with output space

S_{O}^{(j)}
):

Any
(n,|S_{O}^{(i)}|)
code with

P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n)}\leq\epsilon
satisfies

\log|A^{(i)}|\;\leq\;\frac{nC(W_{ij})+1}{1-\epsilon}.

(5.19)

(i): Immediate from Corollary
5.1
.

(ii): The code is constructed in two layers, adapting
Theorem
4.6
(ii) to the heterogeneous
output alphabet
\hat{S}_{O}=S_{O}^{(j)}
.

Layer 1 (core code).

Since
\log|A^{(i)}|/n<C(W_{ij})
, the classical channel
coding theorem

[
34
,
8
]
yields an

(n,|A^{(i)}|)
block code

(f_{n}^{A},g_{n}^{A})
for
W_{ij}
with message set

A^{(i)}
and

P_{e}^{(n)}(A^{(i)})\to 0
.
By (H1),

A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}=\hat{S}_{O}
, so the decoder
can output elements of
A^{(i)}
.

Layer 2 (redundant extension).

Fix an arbitrary
a_{0}\in A^{(i)}
.
For each redundant state

j\in J^{(i)}=S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus A^{(i)}
, set

f_{n}(j):=f_{n}^{A}(a_{0})
.
The decoder first applies
g_{n}^{A}
to recover

\hat{a}\in A^{(i)}
(or an incorrect element in the error
event), and outputs
\hat{a}
.

Closure analysis.

For
m\in A^{(i)}
: if the core code decodes correctly
(
\hat{a}=m
), then

d_{\text{Cn}}(m,m\mid S_{O}^{(i)})=0
.
Error probability:

P_{e}^{(n)}(A^{(i)})\to 0
.

For
m=j\in J^{(i)}
: the decoder outputs some

\hat{a}\in A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
.
Since
j
is redundant in
S_{O}^{(i)}
,

\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus\{j\})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
.
Because

\hat{a}\in\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus\{j\})
,
monotonicity and idempotence of
Cn
give

\text{Cn}\bigl((S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus\{j\})\cup\{\hat{a}\}\bigr)=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)}\setminus\{j\})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})
,
so
d_{\text{Cn}}(j,\hat{a}\mid S_{O}^{(i)})=0
.
The closure error probability for redundant messages
is
zero
for all
n
.

Combining:

P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n)}\leq P_{e}^{(n)}(A^{(i)})\to 0
.

(iii): The argument is identical to the proof of
Theorem
4.5
(iii), with

S_{O}
replaced by
S_{O}^{(i)}
,

\hat{S}_{O}
replaced by
S_{O}^{(j)}
, and

A=\text{Atom}(S_{O}^{(i)})=A^{(i)}
.
Under Assumption
4.1
(applied to

A^{(i)}
with acceptable sets in
S_{O}^{(j)}
),
the pairwise-disjoint decoding regions and the Fano argument
yield (
5.19
).
∎

Remark 5.15

(Role of conditions (H1) and
(H2))
.

Condition
(H1)
is used only in the achievability
proof to ensure that the decoder can output core elements
(
A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}
).
Condition
(H2)
is used only to establish
set-level closure fidelity

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}=1
in part
(i)
;
it does
not
enter the achievability or converse
proofs, which depend only on the closure distortion

d_{\text{Cn}}(\cdot,\cdot\mid S_{O}^{(i)})
measured relative
to the
sender’s
knowledge base.
Hence the coding-theoretic conclusions
(ii)

and
(iii)
hold under
(H1)
alone.
Condition
(H2)
provides the additional guarantee
that the receiver’s overall knowledge base generates the
same deductive closure as the sender’s.

Remark 5.16

(Weak core coverage and alternative decoding)
.

Theorem
5.1
uses the strong core
coverage condition

(H1)
:
A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}
.
As noted in Remark
5.11
, set-level
closure fidelity
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}=1
requires only the
weaker condition
(F1)
:

A^{(i)}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
.
When
(F1)
holds but
(H1)
fails
(i.e., some core element
a\in A_{-}^{ij}
is derivable from

S_{O}^{(j)}
but not literally present), the two-layer code
cannot directly output
a
.
A modified decoder could instead output a

proxy element

\hat{a}\in S_{O}^{(j)}
satisfying

d_{\text{Cn}}(a,\hat{a}\mid S_{O}^{(i)})=0
—i.e., a state in
the receiver’s vocabulary whose substitution for
a
preserves
the sender’s deductive closure.
Such a proxy exists whenever

a\in\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})
, but identifying it requires
knowledge of the sender’s closure structure at the decoder,
making the code design more involved.
A complete treatment of proxy-based decoding is deferred to
future work; the results of this section focus on the
operationally simpler setting where
(H1)
holds.
The proxy-based decoding strategy shares conceptual
affinity with the inverse contextual reasoning of
Seo et al.
[
32
]
, who address the problem
of inferring a sender’s communication context from noisy
observations using Bayesian methods.

Theorem 5.2

(Heterogeneous deductive compression)
.

Under the hypotheses of
Theorem
5.1
, the
minimum blocklength for closure-reliable communication
of the full knowledge base
S_{O}^{(i)}
to agent
j

satisfies, for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
:

(i)

Closure blocklength:

\displaystyle\frac{(1-\epsilon)\log|A^{(i)}|-1}{C(W_{ij})}\;\leq\;

\displaystyle n^{*}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)},W_{ij},P_{e,\text{Cn}},\epsilon\bigr)

\displaystyle\;\leq\;

\displaystyle\left\lceil\frac{\log|A^{(i)}|}{C(W_{ij})-\delta(\epsilon)}\right\rceil,

(5.20)

where
\delta(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

(ii)

Hamming baseline:

Under the additional hypothesis

S_{O}^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}

(
i.e.,
S_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
,
which strengthens
(H1)
to full vocabulary
containment
)
,

n^{*}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)},W_{ij},P_{e},\epsilon\bigr)\;\geq\;\frac{(1-\epsilon)\log|S_{O}^{(i)}|-1}{C(W_{ij})}.

(5.21)

(iii)

Deductive compression ratio:

When both bounds apply,

\frac{n^{*}(P_{e,\text{Cn}})}{n^{*}(P_{e})}\;\approx\;\frac{\log|A^{(i)}|}{\log|S_{O}^{(i)}|}\,,

(5.22)

identical to the homogeneous ratio of
Corollary
4.4
.

Part (i) combines
Theorem
5.1
(ii)
(upper bound) and (iii) (lower bound).
Part (ii) is
Theorem
4.5
(i) applied with

M=|S_{O}^{(i)}|
; the condition

S_{O}^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}
ensures that the
Hamming criterion is meaningful (each sent state has a valid
identity reconstruction in the receiver’s vocabulary).
Part (iii) follows by dividing the bounds.
∎

Remark 5.17

(Heterogeneity does not degrade the compression
ratio)
.

The deductive compression
ratio (
5.22
) depends only on
the sender’s knowledge-base structure
(
|A^{(i)}|
vs.
|S_{O}^{(i)}|
) and not on the
receiver’s vocabulary
S_{O}^{(j)}
, provided the core
coverage condition
(H1)
holds.
This invariance is a consequence of the two-layer code
structure: the core sub-code operates identically regardless
of the receiver’s surplus states, and the redundant extension
incurs zero closure distortion by the algebraic properties
of
Cn
.

The Hamming baseline (
5.21
)
does, however, depend on the receiver’s vocabulary: it
requires
S_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
(full vocabulary
containment), a strictly stronger condition than
(H1)
.
When
S_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
, perfect Hamming
reconstruction is impossible (some sent states have no
counterpart in the receiver’s vocabulary), while closure
reliability may still be achievable under
(H1)
.
This gap illustrates the advantage of semantic fidelity
criteria over symbol-level criteria in heterogeneous settings.

Corollary 5.3

(Impossibility under core loss)
.

Let
(i,j)
be a sender–receiver pair.

(i)

Set-level impossibility:

If condition
(F1)
of
Proposition
5.3
fails,
i.e.,

A^{(i)}\not\subseteq\text{Cn}\bigl(S_{O}^{(j)}\bigr)
,
then

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)})<1
.
This is a property of the knowledge-base pair,
independent of the channel, the blocklength, and the
coding strategy.

(ii)

Quantitative bound:

Under the hypothesis of
(i)
, the closure
fidelity satisfies

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}\bigl(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)}\bigr)\;=\;\frac{|\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})\cap\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})|}{|\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})\cup\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j)})|}\;<\;1.

(5.23)

(iii)

Core preservation ratio:

If
A_{-}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
, then

\rho_{\text{Atom}}(S_{O}^{(i)},S_{O}^{(j)})=1-|A_{-}^{ij}|/|A^{(i)}|<1

(Proposition
5.2
(ii))
.

Similarly, if condition
(F2)
fails

(
S_{+,n}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
)
, then

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}<1
regardless of any coding strategy.

Part (i) is the contrapositive of
Proposition
5.3
.
Part (ii) is the definition of

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}

(Definition
3.17
); the strict
inequality follows from (i).
Part (iii) is
Proposition
5.2
(ii).
The final claim follows from
Proposition
5.3
(necessity
of (F2)).
∎

Proposition 5.8

(Minimum receiver vocabulary for
closure-reliable communication)
.

Given a sender knowledge base
S_{O}^{(i)}
, the
minimum-cardinality subset

V\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}
serving as a receiver vocabulary
(
\hat{S}_{O}=V
) that simultaneously achieves:

(a)

the two-layer code of
Theorem
5.1
(ii)

achieves
P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n)}\to 0
, and

(b)

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(i)},V)=1
,

is
V^{*}=\text{Atom}(S_{O}^{(i)})=A^{(i)}
, with

|V^{*}|=\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{I}^{(i)})
.

Sufficiency.

Set
V=A^{(i)}
.
Since
A^{(i)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}
, the overlap
with sender
i
and “receiver”
V
gives

S_{+}^{ij}=V\setminus S_{O}^{(i)}=\varnothing
and

A_{-}^{ij}=A^{(i)}\setminus V=\varnothing

(condition (H1)).
Condition (H2) holds trivially since

S_{+}^{ij}=\varnothing
.
Closure fidelity:

\text{Cn}(A^{(i)})=\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(i)})

(Proposition
3.3
(i)),
giving
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(i)},A^{(i)})=1
.
The two-layer code of
Theorem
5.1
(ii) applies
with
\hat{S}_{O}=V=A^{(i)}
.

Minimality.

Let
V\subseteq S_{O}^{(i)}
satisfy both
conditions (a) and (b).
Condition (a) requires the two-layer code
of Theorem
5.1
(ii) to
succeed.
That code’s decoder outputs elements of
A^{(i)}
,
so the output alphabet
\hat{S}_{O}=V
must contain
every core element:
A^{(i)}\subseteq V
.
Hence
|V|\geq|A^{(i)}|
.
Since
V=A^{(i)}
achieves this bound, it is
minimal.
∎

Remark 5.18

(Vocabulary design rule)
.

Proposition
5.8
yields a principled
vocabulary-selection rule for receiver design:

the receiver need store only the sender’s irredundant
core
.
All remaining semantic states (the sender’s stored
shortcuts
J^{(i)}
) can be reconstructed by the receiver’s
inference engine via
\text{Cn}(A^{(i)})
.
The channel-use cost of this strategy is

n^{*}\approx\log|A^{(i)}|/C(W_{ij})
, the minimum
achievable under closure reliability.

When the receiver already maintains a richer vocabulary

S_{O}^{(j)}\supsetneq A^{(i)}
, the additional states are
harmless provided
S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing

(condition
(H2)
); they do not increase the
blocklength.
When some surplus states are non-derivable
(
S_{+,n}^{ij}\neq\varnothing
), set-level closure fidelity
drops below
1

(Corollary
5.3
), but the
coding-theoretic closure reliability may still hold if the
two-layer code is used (since it ignores the surplus entirely;
see Remark
5.15
).

Part 2: Broadcast Extension

We now extend the pairwise results to the broadcast scenario
of Definition
5.2
.
Agent
0
(the sender) communicates its knowledge base

S_{O}^{(0)}
to
K
receivers over a common carrier
channel
W:S_{C}\rightsquigarrow\hat{S}_{C}
with

C(W)>0
.

Theorem 5.3

(Broadcast deductive compression)
.

Suppose that for every receiver

j\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, the overlap conditions hold:

(BH1)

A_{-}^{0j}=\varnothing

(
the sender’s core is contained in every
receiver’s vocabulary:

A^{(0)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}
)
;

(BH2)

S_{+,n}^{0j}=\varnothing

(
every receiver’s surplus is derivable from
the sender:

S_{+}^{0j}\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(0)})
)
.

Then:

(i)

Simultaneous closure fidelity:

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(0)},S_{O}^{(j)})=1
for
every
j\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
.

(ii)

Broadcast achievability:

There exists a
single
sequence of

(n,|S_{O}^{(0)}|)
semantic block codes
(with a common encoding function
f_{n}
) such that

P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n,j)}\to 0
simultaneously for all
receivers
j\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, provided

\frac{\log|A^{(0)}|}{n}\;<\;C(W).

(5.24)

(iii)

Blocklength independence from
K
:

The minimum blocklength for broadcast closure
reliability is

n^{*}_{\mathrm{bc}}\;\approx\;\frac{\log|A^{(0)}|}{C(W)},

(5.25)

independent of the number of receivers
K
.

(iv)

Broadcast converse:

Under
Assumption
4.1
for

S_{O}^{(0)}
with output space
S_{O}^{(j)}
for
each
j
, any code achieving

\max_{j}P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n,j)}\leq\epsilon
satisfies

\log|A^{(0)}|\leq(nC(W)+1)/(1-\epsilon)
.

(i): For each
j
, conditions (BH1)–(BH2)
instantiate (H1)–(H2) of
Theorem
5.1
, giving

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(0)},S_{O}^{(j)})=1
by
Corollary
5.1
.

(ii): Construct a single two-layer code as in the proof of
Theorem
5.1
(ii), with the

common
core code
(f_{n}^{A},g_{n}^{A})
for message
set
A^{(0)}
.
All
K
receivers observe the same channel output

\hat{S}_{C}^{n}
and each independently applies the
same core decoder
g_{n}^{A}
.
Since
A^{(0)}\subseteq S_{O}^{(j)}
for every
j

(by (BH1)), the decoded core element

\hat{a}\in A^{(0)}
is a valid output for every receiver.
The Layer 2 redundant extension and closure analysis are
identical to the pairwise case (using the sender’s
closure structure only), so

P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n,j)}\leq P_{e}^{(n)}(A^{(0)})\to 0

simultaneously for all
j
.

(iii): The blocklength is determined by the core code,
which has rate
\log|A^{(0)}|/n
, independent of
K
.

(iv): Fix any receiver
j
.
Theorem
5.1
(iii) applied to the
pair
(0,j)
gives

\log|A^{(0)}|\leq(nC(W)+1)/(1-\epsilon)
whenever

P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n,j)}\leq\epsilon
.
Since this must hold for every
j
, the bound holds under

\max_{j}P_{e,\text{Cn}}^{(n,j)}\leq\epsilon
.
∎

Proposition 5.9

(Broadcast semantic bottleneck)
.

In the broadcast scenario of
Definition
5.2
, suppose
there exists a receiver
j^{*}\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
such that
condition
(F1)
of
Proposition
5.3
fails for
the pair
(0,j^{*})
:

A^{(0)}\not\subseteq\text{Cn}\bigl(S_{O}^{(j^{*})}\bigr).

Then:

(i)

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(0)},S_{O}^{(j^{*})})<1
,
regardless of the carrier channel
W
, the
blocklength
n
, and the encoding/decoding strategy.

(ii)

Even if the carrier channel is noiseless
(
W=\mathrm{id}_{S_{C}}
), the closure fidelity at
receiver
j^{*}
is bounded by

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}\bigl(S_{O}^{(0)},S_{O}^{(j^{*})}\bigr)\;=\;\frac{|\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(0)})\cap\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j^{*})})\!|}{|\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(0)})\cup\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j^{*})})\!|}\;<\;1.

(5.26)

(iii)

Receiver
j^{*}
is a
semantic bottleneck
:
its performance limitation arises from vocabulary
mismatch, not from the physical channel.
The other receivers
j\neq j^{*}
satisfying

(BH1)
–
(BH2)
achieve

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}=1
and closure reliability
simultaneously, unaffected by
j^{*}
.

Parts (i) and (ii) follow from
Corollary
5.3
(i)–(ii)
applied to the pair
(0,j^{*})
.
Part (iii): the common encoding and core code are
shared by all receivers; the failure at
j^{*}
is due
solely to the mismatch

A^{(0)}\not\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j^{*})})
, which is
independent of the channel.
Receivers satisfying
(BH1)–(BH2) are handled by
Theorem
5.3
.
∎

Remark 5.19

(Semantic bottleneck vs. classical channel
degradation)
.

In classical broadcast channel theory

[
8
,
9
]
, the
performance at each receiver is
determined by the physical channel degradation order: the
“weakest” receiver is the one with the noisiest channel.
The rate region is a function of the channel transition
probabilities alone.

In the semantic broadcast setting, a second axis of
“weakness” emerges that is invisible to classical theory:

vocabulary overlap
.
Proposition
5.9
shows that even
over a
noiseless
carrier, a receiver with insufficient
vocabulary overlap (
A^{(0)}\not\subseteq\text{Cn}(S_{O}^{(j^{*})})
)
cannot achieve perfect closure fidelity.
No amount of coding, no increase in blocklength, and no
improvement in the physical channel can overcome this
semantic bottleneck.
This constitutes the sharpest qualitative distinction between
the semantic channel framework and classical Shannon theory:
the former recognizes an irreducible
structural

limitation that the latter cannot express.

To resolve a semantic bottleneck, the system designer must
augment the vocabulary of receiver
j^{*}
to satisfy the
core coverage condition—e.g., by pre-loading the sender’s
irredundant core
A^{(0)}
into
S_{O}^{(j^{*})}

(Proposition
5.8
).
This is a
design-time
action on the knowledge-base
structure, fundamentally different from the
coding-time

actions (encoder/decoder optimization) that suffice in the
classical setting.

Remark 5.20

(Classical recovery)
.

When all receivers share the sender’s knowledge base
(
S_{O}^{(j)}=S_{O}^{(0)}
for all
j
), every noise pair
is trivial, conditions
(BH1)
–
(BH2)
hold
vacuously, and
Theorem
5.3
reduces to

K
independent applications of the homogeneous
achievability result
(Theorem
4.6
(ii)).
If additionally
S_{O}^{(0)}
is irredundant
(
A^{(0)}=S_{O}^{(0)}
), the deductive compression gain
vanishes and the broadcast blocklength becomes

n^{*}\approx\log|S_{O}^{(0)}|/C(W)
, recovering the classical
channel coding theorem for a broadcast DMC

[
8
]
.

Remark 5.21

(Summary of answers to Q1–Q4)
.

The results of this subsection answer the four key questions
posed in Section
V-A
:

Q1
(closure reliability from overlap):
Proposition
5.3
and
Corollary
5.1
provide the
necessary and sufficient conditions; the operational
achievability under the strong condition
(H1)
is
Theorem
5.1
.

Q2
(heterogeneous compression):
Theorem
5.2
establishes
that the deductive compression ratio is invariant under
vocabulary heterogeneity.
Corollary
5.3
characterizes
the impossibility regime.

Q3
(invariant diagnosis):
Section
V-C

(Propositions
5.2
–
5.6

and Table
II
)
expresses every invariant family in terms of the overlap
decomposition.

Q4
(broadcast bottleneck):
Theorem
5.3
shows
blocklength independence from
K
under core coverage;
Proposition
5.9
identifies
the semantic bottleneck phenomenon.

V-E

Numerical Example: Datalog Knowledge Bases

This subsection verifies the theoretical results of
Sections
V-C
–
V-D
on
an explicit Datalog knowledge-base instance.
Every invariant is computed in closed form and cross-checked
numerically; the example also illustrates the vocabulary design
criterion of Proposition
5.8
and the
broadcast bottleneck of
Proposition
5.9
.

Instance Specification

Example 5.1

(Datalog path-reachability knowledge bases)
.

Fix a domain of four entities

\mathcal{D}=\{a,b,c,d\}
and two binary relation symbols

\mathit{Edge}
and
\mathit{Path}
in

\Sigma_{\mathrm{sem}}
.
The inference fragment

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{kb}}

[
7
,
1
]

consists of ground atoms of the
form
\mathit{Edge}(x,y)
and
\mathit{Path}(x,y)
with

x,y\in\mathcal{D}
, and the proof system
\mathsf{PS}

consists of two Datalog rules:

\displaystyle\mathit{Path}(x,y)\;\leftarrow\;\mathit{Edge}(x,y),

(5.27)

\displaystyle\mathit{Path}(x,z)\;\leftarrow\;\mathit{Edge}(x,y),\,\mathit{Path}(y,z).

(5.28)

The immediate consequence operator
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
applies
both rules to all matching ground instantiations in a single
step.

Agent knowledge bases.

We define one sender and three receivers.

Sender (agent 1):

\displaystyle S_{O}^{(1)}=\{

\displaystyle\mathit{Edge}(a,b),\mathit{Edge}(a,c),\mathit{Edge}(b,c),\mathit{Edge}(c,d),

\displaystyle\mathit{Path}(a,b),\mathit{Path}(b,c),\mathit{Path}(c,d),\mathit{Path}(a,d)\}.

Thus
|S_{O}^{(1)}|=8
.

Receiver 2 (core loss + non-derivable surplus):

\displaystyle S_{O}^{(2)}=\{

\displaystyle\mathit{Edge}(a,b),\mathit{Edge}(b,c),\mathit{Edge}(c,d),

\displaystyle\mathit{Path}(a,b),\mathit{Path}(b,c),\mathit{Path}(c,d),\mathit{Path}(a,d),

\displaystyle\mathit{Path}(b,d),\mathit{Edge}(d,a)\}.

Thus
|S_{O}^{(2)}|=9
.
Compared with agent 1, receiver 2 loses the core element

\mathit{Edge}(a,c)
and gains
\mathit{Path}(b,d)

(derivable from the sender) and
\mathit{Edge}(d,a)
(not
derivable).

Receiver 2
′
(augmented—vocabulary design):

\displaystyle S_{O}^{(2^{\prime})}=\{

\displaystyle\mathit{Edge}(a,b),\mathit{Edge}(a,c),\mathit{Edge}(b,c),

\displaystyle\mathit{Edge}(c,d),\mathit{Path}(a,b),\mathit{Path}(b,c),

\displaystyle\mathit{Path}(c,d),\mathit{Path}(a,d),\mathit{Path}(b,d)\}.

Thus
|S_{O}^{(2^{\prime})}|=9
.
This is receiver 2 augmented with the lost core element

\mathit{Edge}(a,c)
and with the non-derivable surplus

\mathit{Edge}(d,a)
removed.

Receiver 3 (broadcast, no core loss):

\displaystyle S_{O}^{(3)}=\{

\displaystyle\mathit{Edge}(a,b),\;\mathit{Edge}(a,c),\;\mathit{Edge}(b,c),\;

\displaystyle\mathit{Edge}(c,d),\mathit{Path}(a,c),\;\mathit{Path}(b,d)\}.

Thus
|S_{O}^{(3)}|=6
.
All surplus states are derivable from the sender.

Carrier channel.

We use a
q
-ary symmetric channel with alphabet size

q=10
(
|S_{C}|=|\hat{S}_{C}|=10\geq\max_{i}|S_{O}^{(i)}|
)
and crossover probability
p=0.1
:

W(y\mid x)=\begin{cases}1-p&\text{if }y=x,\\
p/(q-1)&\text{if }y\neq x.\end{cases}

Its Shannon capacity is

C(W)=\log q+(1{-}p)\log(1{-}p)+p\log\bigl(p/(q{-}1)\bigr)

bits.

Source-Side Structural Invariants

Applying the irredundantization procedure
(Definition
3.8
) to
S_{O}^{(1)}
in
alphabetical canonical order yields

\displaystyle A^{(1)}=\bigl\{

\displaystyle\mathit{Edge}(a,b),\;\mathit{Edge}(a,c),\;\mathit{Edge}(b,c),\;

\displaystyle\mathit{Edge}(c,d)\bigr\},

with
|A^{(1)}|=4
and

J^{(1)}=S_{O}^{(1)}\setminus A^{(1)}=\{\mathit{Path}(a,b),\,\mathit{Path}(b,c),\,\mathit{Path}(c,d),\,\mathit{Path}(a,d)\}
.
All four
\mathit{Path}
facts are derivable from the edge
facts via rules (
5.27
)–(
5.28
).

The
T_{\mathsf{PS}}
-iteration from
A^{(1)}
stabilizes
in two steps:

TABLE III:
Stratum decomposition of

T_{\mathsf{PS}}
-iteration from
A^{(1)}
.

\mathit{Edge}(a,b)
,
\mathit{Edge}(a,c)
,

\mathit{Edge}(b,c)
,
\mathit{Edge}(c,d)

T^{1}\!\setminus\!T^{0}

\mathit{Path}(a,b)
,
\mathit{Path}(a,c)
,

\mathit{Path}(b,c)
,
\mathit{Path}(c,d)

T^{2}\!\setminus\!T^{1}

\mathit{Path}(a,d)
,
\mathit{Path}(b,d)

Hence the derivation depths of the stored states are:

\text{Dd}=0
for all edges,
\text{Dd}=1
for

\mathit{Path}(a,b),\mathit{Path}(b,c),\mathit{Path}(c,d)
,
and
\text{Dd}=2
for
\mathit{Path}(a,d)
.
The source-side invariants are

\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{I}^{(1)})=4
and

\mathsf{D_{d}}(\mathcal{I}^{(1)})=2
.

(Definition

) and the resulting
set-level invariants for each sender–receiver pair.

and

Vocabulary Augmentation (Receiver 2
\to
2
′
)

Comparing receiver 2 with its augmented version 2
′

demonstrates the vocabulary design criterion of
Proposition
5.8
:
adding the single lost core element

\mathit{Edge}(a,c)
and removing the non-derivable surplus

\mathit{Edge}(d,a)
causes
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}
to jump
from
3/7\approx 0.429
to
1
,

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
to rise from
0
to
0.900
, and the minimum closure
blocklength to drop to

\lceil\log_{2}|A^{(1)}|/C(W)\rceil=\lceil 2/2.536\rceil=1
channel use
(where
\log_{2}4=2
bits).
This confirms that the receiver need only store the sender’s
irredundant core to achieve full closure reliability.

Broadcast Sub-Example

Agent 1 broadcasts to receivers 2 and 3 simultaneously over
the same carrier channel
W
.
Since
A_{-}^{13}=\varnothing
and

S_{+,n}^{13}=\varnothing
, receiver 3 satisfies
conditions (BH1)–(BH2) of
Theorem
5.3
.
Receiver 2 violates (BH1) (
A_{-}^{12}\neq\varnothing
)
and is therefore a
semantic bottleneck

(Proposition
5.9
):

\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(1)},S_{O}^{(2)})<1
regardless of
the carrier channel quality.
At receiver 3, the broadcast closure blocklength is

n^{*}_{\mathrm{bc}}=\lceil\log|A^{(1)}|/C(W)\rceil
,
identical to the pairwise value for
(1,3)

and independent of whether receiver 2 is present.
This illustrates the blocklength-independence result of
Theorem
5.3
(iii) and the semantic
bottleneck phenomenon of
Proposition
5.9
.

Summary of Numerical Findings

The numerical example confirms all theoretical predictions:
(i) the overlap decomposition fully determines the
set-level invariants and constrains the probabilistic indices
(e.g.,
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C}^{12})=0
because

A_{-}^{12}\neq\varnothing
, whereas

\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C}^{12^{\prime}})=\Phi_{\text{Atom}}(\mathfrak{C}^{13})=0.900
);
(ii) core loss (
A_{-}^{12}=\{\mathit{Edge}(a,c)\}\neq\varnothing
) is an
irreducible barrier to closure fidelity
(
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(1)},S_{O}^{(2)})=3/7<1
),
independent of channel quality;
(iii) vocabulary augmentation by the sender’s core
restores full closure reliability
(
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}(S_{O}^{(1)},S_{O}^{(2^{\prime})})=1
);
(iv) the deductive compression ratio

\log|A^{(1)}|/\log|S_{O}^{(1)}|=\log 4/\log 8=2/3
is
invariant across all receiver pairs satisfying (H1),
yielding closure blocklength

n^{*}_{\text{Cn}}\approx 0.789
versus Hamming blocklength

n^{*}_{H}\approx 1.183
;
(v) the broadcast blocklength depends only on the
sender’s core (
n^{*}_{\mathrm{bc}}\approx 0.789
),
not on the number of receivers.

VI
Conclusion

This paper has developed a logical-information-theoretic framework
for semantic communication that integrates formal proof systems
with Shannon-theoretic tools.
The framework rests on three pillars: an axiomatic

information model
that links semantic and carrier state
spaces through computable enabling maps
(Section
III
); a
semantic channel
constructed
as a composition of enabling kernels whose probabilistic
structure respects the underlying logical constraints
(Section
IV
); and an
overlap-based

heterogeneous multi-agent theory that translates knowledge-base
structure into coding-theoretic performance guarantees
(Section
V
).

The central quantitative result is the
deductive
compression gain
: under a closure-based fidelity criterion that
deems a reconstruction acceptable whenever it preserves the
deductive closure of the sender’s knowledge base, the minimum
number of channel uses drops from

n_{H}^{*}\approx\log|S_{O}|/C(W)
(Hamming reliable) to

n_{\text{Cn}}^{*}\approx\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|/C(W)
(closure reliable),
yielding a compression ratio

\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|/\log|S_{O}|
that depends only on the
sender’s knowledge-base redundancy and is invariant under receiver
vocabulary heterogeneity
(Theorems
4.6
–
5.2
).
This gain arises because the receiver’s inference engine can
reconstruct all redundant states from a faithful copy of the
irredundant core at zero additional channel cost—a mechanism
that has no counterpart in classical Shannon theory, where every
source symbol must be individually protected against channel
errors.

Alongside the coding-theoretic results, the framework contributes
six families of computable semantic channel invariants
(Theorem
4.4
) that provide a multi-scale
fingerprint of channel quality: from set-level fidelity metrics
(
\rho_{\text{Atom}}
,
\mathsf{F}_{\text{Cn}}
) that depend only on the
knowledge-base pair, through noise-pair probabilistic indices
(
\Phi_{\text{Atom}}
,
\Psi_{+}
) that capture core preservation
and hallucination probabilities, to information-theoretic
quantities (
I_{\mathrm{sem}}
,
C_{\mathrm{sem}}
) that
bound achievable throughput.
The semantic Fano bound
(Theorem
4.3
) closes the loop by showing that
high probabilistic core preservation forces high mutual
information, quantifying the intuition that faithful transmission
of the irredundant core is both necessary and approximately
sufficient for information-theoretic performance.

In the heterogeneous multi-agent setting, the overlap
decomposition
(Definition
5.4
) translates
knowledge-base structure into two binary feasibility tests—no
core loss (
A_{-}^{ij}=\varnothing
) and no non-derivable
surplus (
S_{+,n}^{ij}=\varnothing
)—that fully determine
whether perfect closure fidelity is achievable
(Proposition
5.3
).
The broadcast extension reveals a
semantic bottleneck

phenomenon
(Proposition
5.9
): a receiver whose
vocabulary does not cover the sender’s irredundant core cannot
achieve perfect closure fidelity regardless of the carrier
channel quality, the blocklength, or the coding strategy.
This structural limitation is invisible to classical broadcast
channel theory and constitutes the sharpest qualitative
distinction between the semantic and classical frameworks.
Resolving a semantic bottleneck requires a
design-time

action on the knowledge-base structure—augmenting the receiver’s
vocabulary—rather than the
coding-time
optimizations
(encoder/decoder design) that suffice in classical settings.

Connections to Prior Work

The framework developed here may be viewed as a formal bridge
between several previously disjoint lines of research.
It gives operational, coding-theoretic content to the
philosophical notion of semantic information pioneered by
Carnap and Bar-Hillel
[
6
]
and
Floridi
[
11
]
, by replacing the
possible-worlds measure of content with computable,
proof-system-relative invariants (
Atom
,
Dd
,

Cn
) that enter directly into rate and distortion
expressions.
It complements the deep-learning-based semantic communication
literature
[
41
,
30
,
23
]

by providing a formal guarantee layer: the invariants and coding
theorems of Sections
IV-D
–
IV-E

can serve as benchmarks against which the performance of learned
encoders and decoders is evaluated.
It extends Shannon’s theory in a strict sense: when the knowledge
base is irredundant and sender and receiver share the same
vocabulary, every semantic result reduces to its classical
counterpart (Corollary
4.3
),
confirming that the generalization is conservative.

Connections to Recent Work on Semantic
Communication Theory

The recently established mathematical theory of semantic
communication by Niu and
Zhang
[
28
,
44
]
provides the
closest point of comparison for the information-theoretic
aspects of our framework.
Their approach partitions the source alphabet into synonymous
equivalence classes and defines semantic entropy and capacity
relative to this partition, yielding a semantic capacity

C_{s}\geq C
that exceeds the Shannon limit by a factor related
to the average synonymous length.
Our framework achieves an analogous—but mechanistically
distinct—compression advantage: the deductive compression
ratio
\log|\text{Atom}(S_{O})|/\log|S_{O}|

(Corollary
4.4
) reduces the effective
source size not through a pre-defined equivalence partition, but
through the receiver’s ability to re-derive redundant states via
logical inference.
The two approaches are complementary rather than competing: the
synonymous-mapping framework captures compression gains from

source-side
semantic equivalence, while our framework
captures gains from
receiver-side
deductive
reconstruction.
A unified theory that combines both mechanisms—synonymous
collapsing of the irredundant core followed by deductive
expansion at the receiver—is an attractive direction for
future work.

The semantic channel coding theorem of Ma
et al.
[
24
]
for many-to-one sources and the
companion computational tools of
Han et al.
[
14
]
and
Liang et al.
[
21
]
address the algorithmic
realization of semantic coding gains within the
synonymous-mapping paradigm.
Our coding theorems
(Theorems
4.5
–
4.6
and
their heterogeneous extensions in
Section
V
) differ in two respects.
First, the fidelity criterion is
closure-based

(preservation of the deductive closure
\text{Cn}(S_{O})
) rather
than based on a pre-defined synonymous partition; this allows
the framework to handle structured knowledge bases where the
notion of “same meaning” is defined by logical entailment
rather than by an exogenous equivalence relation.
Second, the achievability mechanism is a
two-layer code

in which only the irredundant core is channel-coded, while
redundant states are reconstructed by the decoder’s inference
engine; this is structurally different from the
random-coding arguments
over synonymous sets used
in
[
24
,
21
]
, and it naturally
extends to the heterogeneous multi-agent setting where sender
and receiver maintain different vocabularies.

On the multi-agent front, Seo
et al.
[
32
]
and Alshammari and
Bennis
[
3
]
address heterogeneous
semantic communication from, respectively, a Bayesian
estimation and a modal-logic resilience perspective.
Our overlap decomposition
(Definition
5.4
) and the semantic
bottleneck phenomenon
(Proposition
5.9
) provide a
coding-theoretic complement to these approaches: where
Seo et al. quantify the inference cost of context mismatch
and Alshammari and Bennis formalize resilience conditions, our
framework quantifies the
minimum number of channel uses

needed to overcome (or the impossibility of overcoming)
vocabulary mismatch, and identifies the irredundant core as
the minimal vocabulary that must be shared for closure-reliable
communication
(Proposition
5.8
).

The goal-oriented metrics proposed by Li
et al.
[
20
]
and the interpretable semantic
communication guidelines of Wu
et al.
[
40
]
address the question of
what
to measure
in semantic communication.
Our composite distortion function
d_{\mathrm{sem}}

(Definition
4.16
) provides a
principled answer grounded in proof-system structure: the
three components—Hamming, closure, and depth
distortion—capture symbol fidelity, deductive completeness,
and inferential complexity, respectively, and their relative
weights can be tuned to match specific task requirements.
The semantic Fano bound
(Theorem
4.3
) then connects these
structural distortion measures to mutual information, closing
the loop between task-oriented fidelity and
information-theoretic throughput.

Finally, the logical-complexity results of
Marx
[
25
]
and Abo Khamis and
Chen
[
2
]
on conjunctive query
evaluation suggest a deeper connection between database query
complexity and semantic communication complexity that merits
further exploration.
The irredundant core
\text{Atom}(S_{O})
can be viewed as a
form of
query-aware
source compression: only the facts
that cannot be re-derived (analogous to base relations that
cannot be materialized from views) need to be transmitted.
Investigating whether the submodular-width hierarchy
of
[
25
]
imposes additional structure on
the derivation-depth stratification
\text{Dd}(\cdot\mid\text{Atom}(S_{O}))
,
and whether this structure can be exploited for more efficient
code constructions, is a promising avenue for future work.

Limitations and Future Directions

Several limitations of the present work point to directions for
future research.

First, the framework assumes that all agents share a common proof
system
\mathsf{PS}

(Assumption
5.1
).
In practice, agents may employ different inference engines with
different rule sets or computational capabilities.
Extending the theory to
heterogeneous proof systems
—where
each agent
i
uses a sub-system

\mathsf{PS}^{(i)}\subseteq\mathsf{PS}
or even an
incompatible system—would require a generalized notion of
closure compatibility and could yield richer compression/fidelity
trade-offs.

Second, the coding theorems of
Section
IV-E
are stated for single-letter (or
fixed-blocklength) semantics: each channel use transmits (a block
encoding of) a single semantic state.
A
multi-letter
extension, in which the source emits a

sequence
of semantically correlated states (e.g., a
temporal stream of knowledge-base updates), would connect the
framework to ergodic source theory and network information theory,
and is expected to yield tighter achievability bounds via joint
source–channel semantic coding.

Third, the current treatment of the enabling map
\mathcal{E}

is set-valued and non-parametric.
In many practical systems, the enabling structure has additional
parametric regularity (e.g., the set of admissible carrier states
depends smoothly on the semantic state).
Incorporating such regularity could yield sharper capacity
characterizations and more efficient code constructions.

Fourth, the relay scenario sketched in
Remark
5.1
—where an intermediate agent
performs inference before re-encoding—raises the fundamental
question of whether semantic relaying can increase end-to-end
capacity beyond what the physical channel alone permits.
The information-model composition machinery of
Definition
3.4
provides the formal
substrate for this analysis, but a complete treatment requires
multi-hop coding theorems that remain to be developed.

Fifth, the Datalog instance of Section
V-E

serves as a proof of concept; scaling the framework to
large real-world knowledge bases (e.g., knowledge graphs with
millions of entities) will require efficient algorithms for
core extraction, closure computation, and overlap analysis, as
well as empirical validation on practical communication tasks.

Finally, the connection between derivation depth
Dd
and
Bennett’s logical depth
[
5
]
merits further
investigation.
If the two notions can be formally linked under explicit
simulation assumptions, the semantic channel invariants
would inherit a resource-theoretic interpretation: the
“intrinsic computational cost” of a semantic state would
directly enter the distortion and capacity expressions,
unifying information-theoretic and computational-complexity
perspectives on semantic communication.

Summary

We have introduced the semantic channel as a formal object that
integrates deductive logic with Shannon information theory,
defined computable invariants that characterize its
information-theoretic and structural properties, derived coding
theorems that quantify the deductive compression gain achievable
under semantic fidelity criteria, and demonstrated the
framework’s explanatory power in heterogeneous multi-agent
settings through both analytical results and a complete numerical
example.
The framework provides a principled foundation for the
theoretical analysis of semantic communication systems and opens
several avenues for further research at the intersection of
information theory, formal logic, and multi-agent systems.
